**Summary.** The authors used a 2D Rydberg atom array to simulate the relaxation of a quantum magnet described by the TFIM. They discovered that the system exhibits more complex behavior than simple thermalization, identifying distinct prethermal and slow crossover regimes. This work validates Rydberg arrays as powerful tools for discovering new physics in non-equilibrium quantum dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly bridges experimental quantum simulation platforms (Rydberg atoms) with fundamental theoretical problems in non-equilibrium many-body physics, providing concrete evidence of complex relaxation dynamics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central problem is understanding how quantum matter relaxes far from equilibrium, moving beyond simple confirmation of theory to discovering new physical phenomena.

**Main result.** The study identified a rich relaxation landscape in the 2D transverse-field Ising model, revealing not only rapid thermalization but also distinct prethermal and unexpected crossover regimes characterized by slow dynamics.

**Method.** The research combines quantum simulation using programmable Rydberg atom arrays with theoretical analysis comparing results against classical tensor-network methods.

**Model / system.** The system is a 2D Quantum Magnet modeled by the transverse-field Ising model (TFIM) Hamiltonian, implemented on a Rydberg atom array of 256 qubits. The dynamics are studied via quenching across different parameter ratios ($h_x/J$).

**Key observables.** Magnetization $\langle\hat{\sigma}_z^i(t)angle$, local classical energy $E_{	ext{cl}}(i, t)$, and connected correlations $C_c(r, t)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The ratio of transverse field to coupling strength ($h_x/J$), which dictates the system's phase (e.g., ferromagnetic vs. paramagnetic).

**Assumptions / limitations.** For numerical analysis, the study compares quantum simulation results against classical tensor-network methods, noting discrepancies in late-time behavior.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate post-quench dynamics of local energy and magnetization for various $h_x/J$ ratios, mapping out different relaxation regimes across the phase diagram.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical problem (non-equilibrium relaxation), details the system (2D TFIM on Rydberg array), presents measurements across varying quench parameters ($h_x/J$), and analyzes the resulting distinct dynamical regimes (thermalization, prethermalization, crossover).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

How quantum matter relaxes far from equilibrium is a central open problem in many-body physics, and one for which analog quantum simulators are well positioned to move from confirming theory to discovering new physics. Here, we use a two-dimensional Rydberg atom array of 256 qubits to map the relaxation landscape of the two-dimensional transverse-field Ising model across its phase diagram. Beyond the expected rapid thermalization, we identify two further regimes. The first is a prethermal regime whose dynamics are governed by an effective XY model. The second, and most unexpected, is a crossover regime characterized by a slowdown in relaxation. This slowdown occurs precisely where state-of-the-art classical tensor-network methods lose control at late times, whereas the quantum simulation remains consistent across system sizes. These results establish Rydberg atom arrays as a platform for scientific discovery in nonequilibrium quantum many-body dynamics.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper presents a novel technique to non-destructively read out the state of a single qubit by coupling it to an ensemble of atoms via a collective phase shift. By utilizing Rydberg dressing, they achieve a protocol robust against common experimental imperfections like atom loss. This opens pathways for high-fidelity mid-circuit quantum measurements in atomic quantum simulators.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides an experimentally validated, scalable method for interfacing single qubits with large ensembles using highly controllable interactions (Rydberg dressing), which is crucial for quantum memory and measurement protocols in AMO physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is amplifying quantum information from a single qubit to a mesoscopic ensemble for non-destructive state detection.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated the non-destructive detection of a single Rydberg excitation with a high state-assignment fidelity of 99.81%.

**Method.** They map the control qubit state onto the target ensemble via a controlled collective phase shift, which is measured using Ramsey interferometry.

**Model / system.** The platform couples a single control qubit (in a ground-to-Rydberg transition) to a Rydberg-dressed ensemble of ground-state atoms trapped in an optical lattice. The dressing makes the interaction robust against atom number fluctuations.

**Key observables.** State-assignment fidelity ($\mathcal{F}$), fringe contrast changes, and population dynamics measured via Ramsey interferometry.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The protocol is designed to be insensitive to atom loss and number fluctuations due to Rydberg dressing. Key parameters include the AC-Stark shift ($\delta_g$) and Rabi frequencies ($\Omega$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Fidelity estimations rely on statistical analysis of experimental histograms (e.g., Youden's J statistic) and assuming independent target spin interactions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the setup, electronic level structure, Ramsey protocols showing controlled-phase oscillations for different control states, and comparisons demonstrating resilience to atom number variations.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem of quantum information amplification, details the Rydberg-dressed coupling mechanism via a controlled phase shift, presents experimental results confirming state transfer fidelity, and discusses implications for mid-circuit readout.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The amplification of quantum information carried by a single quantum excitation is a recurring challenge across diverse quantum platforms. The coupling between a single qubit and a mesoscopic ensemble of spins, for example, can be leveraged to realize non-destructive detection of the qubit state. However, realizing robust couplings between such systems is experimentally challenging and typically requires programmable quantum gates or native long-range interactions. Here, we introduce a platform that couples a single qubit, encoded in the ground-to-Rydberg transition of a control atom, to a Rydberg-dressed target ensemble of ground-state atoms trapped in an optical lattice. We show that the state of the control qubit can be coherently mapped onto the ensemble via a qubit-controlled collective phase shift. By Rydberg-dressing the ensemble, the controlled phase shift per target atom becomes independent of the number of target atoms, making the protocol intrinsically insensitive to atom-number fluctuations and atom loss, which are the dominant experimental imperfections in our system. Exploiting the collective response of up to eight target spins, we demonstrate the efficacy of the scheme by realizing non-destructive detection of a single Rydberg excitation with a state-assignment fidelity of $\mathcal{F} = 99.81^{+0.17}_{-1.47}\,\%$. Our approach demonstrates the key ingredients for high-fidelity transfer of quantum information from a single qubit to a mesoscopic ensemble, opening a route to non-destructive mid-circuit readout of Rydberg states and to efficient interfaces between single qubits and photonic modes.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper investigates universal scaling laws in 2D polariton condensates, demonstrating a crossover between the Kardar-Parisi-Zhang (KPZ) and Edwards-Wilkinson (EW) regimes. By measuring spatio-temporal coherence, the authors confirm that these driven-dissipative systems serve as an excellent platform for studying non-equilibrium critical phenomena. This bridges fundamental statistical theory with advanced quantum optics experiments.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a highly controllable, solid-state platform (polaritons) to experimentally verify fundamental concepts from non-equilibrium statistical mechanics, such as the KPZ universality class, which is crucial for understanding driven many-body systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates universal scaling laws, specifically the crossover between Edwards-Wilkinson (EW) and Kardar-Parisi-Zhang (KPZ), governing coherence decay in non-equilibrium systems.

**Main result.** The authors experimentally demonstrate that 2D polariton condensates exhibit clear crossovers between KPZ super-diffusive and EW diffusive scaling regimes by tuning system parameters. They also show that topological defects are largely paired, suggesting the underlying physics is governed by phase fluctuations at large scales.

**Method.** The research combines experimental measurements of spatio-temporal first-order coherence with numerical simulations to analyze defect dynamics and correlation functions.

**Model / system.** The physical system is two-dimensional driven-dissipative polariton condensates realized in semiconductor optical microcavities. The behavior contrasts with equilibrium 2D bosonic systems (BKT class) by exploring non-equilibrium universality classes like KPZ and EW.

**Key observables.** Spatio-temporal first-order coherence function ($g^{(1)}(\Delta\mathbf{r}, \Delta t)$), critical exponents, defect pair correlations ($g^{(2)}$), and vortex/anti-vortex lifetimes ($	au$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** System detuning ($\hbar\delta$), excitation power ratio ($p = P/P_{th}$), spatial filter width ($\sigma_f$), and interaction strength ($g_R$) relative to loss rates.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that at large scales, the coherence decay is dominated by phase fluctuations, allowing for the separation of density correlations from universal scaling laws. Numerical simulations require an energy cutoff ($	au_c$).

**Figures summary.** Figures show heat maps and temporal/spatial coherence decays confirming KPZ (stretched exponential) and EW (power-law) scaling at different detunings. Supplementary figures detail vortex clustering, defect lifetime distributions, and pair correlations.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the context by contrasting equilibrium (BKT) vs. non-equilibrium physics. It then presents experimental evidence for KPZ/EW crossovers using coherence measurements, followed by detailed numerical analysis confirming that phase fluctuations dominate over short-lived topological defects at macroscopic scales.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In low dimensional systems, fluctuations are enhanced and prevent the spontaneous breaking of continuous symmetries. As a result, spatial and temporal correlation functions decay at large distances and long times. A well established example is given by two-dimensional bosonic condensates at equilibrium, which do not display long-range order of the coherence but algebraic decay belonging to the Berezinski-Kosterlitz-Thouless universality class. In contrast, the universal behaviors of non-equilibrium bosonic condensates are more diverse and many open questions remain. Here, we explore the spatio-temporal coherence properties of two-dimensional driven-dissipative polariton condensates in semiconductor optical microcavities. By tuning microscopic parameters, we observe a cross-over between two scaling laws that we attribute to the Edwards-Wilkinson (EW) and the Kardar-Parisi-Zhang (KPZ) universality classes. We demonstrate the collapse of the measured first-order correlations onto the EW and KPZ universal scaling functions and obtain critical exponents, well matching the values predicted theoretically. Our results highlight the intrinsic non-equilibrium nature of polariton condensates and establish them as a platform of choice for controlled exploration of the two-dimensional KPZ universality class.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper details achieving magnetometry sensitivity beyond the Standard Quantum Limit using optical atomic ensembles. This is accomplished through a sophisticated hybrid technique that combines variational readout exploiting intra-channel quantum correlations with EPR conditioning utilizing entangled light states for cross-channel noise suppression. The result demonstrates enhanced, broadband magnetic field sensing in a practical, room-temperature setting.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it pushes the boundaries of quantum sensing by demonstrating practical methods (hybrid schemes) to beat fundamental measurement limits in a real-world atomic platform, bridging theory with advanced experimental implementation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is achieving optical magnetometry sensitivity beyond the Standard Quantum Limit (SQL) imposed by measurement backaction and imprecision noise.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated sub-SQL magnetic field sensitivity in a room-temperature atomic platform by combining variational readout with entanglement conditioning using an EPR source.

**Method.** They employ a hybrid quantum sensing scheme that combines intra-channel correlations (variational readout) with inter-channel correlations derived from measuring one mode of a bipartite entangled light state and conditioning the results on the other.

**Model / system.** The system is an optical magnetometer using an ensemble of optically polarized atoms sensitive to external magnetic fields. The noise analysis models the total measurement noise as a sum of imprecision, backaction, spin, and correlation terms.

**Key observables.** Magnetic-field sensitivity ($\delta B(\Omega)$), quantum noise quadratures (e.g., $	heta_{	ext{mag}}$), and the measured Power Spectrum Density (PSD) for various noise contributions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Acoustic frequencies ($\sim 7$ kHz to broadband), probe power, variational readout angle ($	heta_{mag}$), and EPR conditioning phase ($\phi_{EPR}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes Markovian noise sources for spin Langevin forces and models optical losses (transmissivity $\eta$) which degrade squeezing.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the setup using EPR entanglement, show phasor diagrams comparing noise before and after conditioning, and present reconstructed magnetic field sensitivity spectra demonstrating performance surpassing the predicted SQL line across various frequencies.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the SQL limit in standard measurements. It then introduces variational readout to exploit intra-channel correlations. Finally, it combines this with EPR conditioning using entangled light states to achieve a hybrid scheme that surpasses the SQL across an accessible acoustic frequency band.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Optical atomic magnetometry is a powerful tool for continuous sensing applications, yet, in the absence of quantum correlations, its sensitivity is limited by the standard quantum limit (SQL) stemming from a trade-off between optical probe imprecision and quantum measurement backaction. Beyond-SQL sensitivity requires quantum correlations that modify these measurement noise sources. Here we demonstrate such sensitivity by using entangled state of the probe light and by engineering correlations between measurement imprecision and backaction. Having first explored SQL in a broad range of frequencies, we demonstrate overcoming the limit by combining variational readout with coupling the magnetometer to one mode of a bipartite entangled light state and conditioning the results on the other entangled mode. Tuning the detected light quadratures and combining the signals from the two measurement channels, we achieve sensitivity beyond the SQL in a broad range of acoustic frequencies which has so far remained inaccessible to quantum-noise-limited optical magnetometry.

</details>

The memory is evaluated for a time τL ≃864, corresponding to t ≃320 ns, with τL = gnnt. The curves exhibit a scale-invariant crossing point at ∆ϕ ≃2. (b) Numerical derivative d⟨M⟩/d∆ϕ versus ∆ϕ. **Summary.** This work analyzes Many-Body Localization in superconducting transmon qubit networks subjected to realistic, correlated disorder. The authors demonstrate that the MBL transition remains robust even when considering complex, non-local disorder patterns inherent to physical hardware. This provides a vital theoretical framework for designing stable quantum processors.

**Why it may be interesting.** It bridges fundamental condensed matter concepts (MBL) with cutting-edge quantum hardware platforms (superconducting qubits), providing concrete diagnostic tools for assessing device viability in complex disorder regimes.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates Many-Body Localization (MBL) induced by correlated disorder in interacting superconducting qubits, moving beyond simplified models that only consider purely random disorder.

**Main result.** The MBL phase transition is shown to be robust against unavoidable correlated disorder arising from network interactions, which is crucial for designing realistic quantum processors.

**Method.** The analysis employs multiple diagnostics including block entanglement entropy (Rényi entropy) and a local memory parameter derived from site occupation number dynamics.

**Model / system.** The system consists of transmon qubits in an array structure, governed by a Hamiltonian incorporating nearest-neighbor couplings and onsite energies that are subject to tunable correlated disorder via external magnetic flux.

**Key observables.** Block entanglement entropy ($\langle S_2 angle$), variance of block entanglement ($	ext{Var}(S_2)$), and the local memory parameter ($\langle M(t) angle$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Disorder strength ($\Delta\phi$) controlled by external flux ($\phi_0$); junction asymmetry parameter ($\alpha$); filling factor ($
u$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis sometimes assumes statistical independence of local flux fluctuations for calculating covariance, but the core finding addresses robustness despite unavoidable correlations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate how entanglement entropy scales (volume-law vs. area-law) across disorder strengths and show the enhancement of the dimensionless disorder parameter ($\Delta(\phi_0)$) around $\phi_0 \simeq 0.5$.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the physical model using transmon qubits subjected to flux-controlled correlated disorder, then applies entanglement entropy diagnostics to locate the MBL critical point, and finally corroborates these findings using a local memory parameter analysis.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The failure of quantum thermalization due to Many-Body Localization (MBL) has evolved from a theoretical concept in spin chains to an experimental reality in synthetic quantum platforms, most notably superconducting circuits based on transmon qubits. Despite its significance, the MBL transition has been studied primarily under purely random disorder and local couplings, leaving more complex and realistic configurations largely unexplored. Here, we investigate the quantum dynamics of transmon networks subject to the unavoidable competing effects of correlated disorder and network-mediated non-local interactions. First, we show how to engineer the physical parameters of the quantum hardware to systematically control the correlated disorder patterns emerging in the system. Then, we demonstrate that the MBL phase transition is robust against such correlations, which is essential for tuning localization properties in realistic transmon devices. This robustness is established through the analysis of the block entanglement entropy variance across disorder realizations, which precisely locates the MBL critical point. Independently, we introduce a local memory parameter, whose dynamics at long evolution times reveals memory retention in the localized phase and yields a critical point consistent with the entropy analysis. These results provide a framework for understanding localization in complex quantum network topologies, with potential implications for multi-qubit processor design.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper proposes that utilizing multiple vibrational phonon modes as parallel scattering channels can overcome the inherent limitations of linear coupling in cavity magnomechanics to generate robust quantum entanglement. This multimode mediation enhances entanglement monotonically while simultaneously inducing collective amplitude squeezing, offering a pathway for controlling quantum resources in these hybrid systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses open quantum systems dynamics in a complex hybrid platform, providing quantitative measures for controlling non-classical correlations (entanglement and squeezing) using engineered environmental coupling channels (phonons).

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central challenge is generating quantum entanglement between cavity-magnon polariton modes, which are inherently limited by linear beam-splitter interactions.

**Main result.** Multimode phonon mediation provides a mechanism that monotonically enhances steady-state bipartite entanglement and generates collective squeezing in the amplitude quadrature of the polaritons.

**Method.** The analysis uses Quantum Langevin Equations (QLEs) linearized around the steady state to calculate quantum fluctuations, followed by solving Lyapunov equations for covariance matrix evolution.

**Model / system.** The system is a cavity magnomechanical platform involving coupled microwave cavity ($	ext{a}$), magnon ($	ext{c}$), and multiple vibrational/phonon modes ($k$ modes). The coupling forms hybridized polaritons ($	ext{p}^\pm$).

**Key observables.** Logarithmic negativity (bipartite entanglement, EN); minimum residual conrangle (tripartite entanglement, Rmin^tau); quantum synchronization measures ($S_c, S_x, S_\phi$)

**Important parameters / regimes.** Number of phonon modes ($k$); detunings ($\Delta_\pm$); coupling strengths ($G_{0j}$); operating temperature and damping rates.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes a dispersive interaction regime and linearizes the QLEs, neglecting higher-order fluctuation terms.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate entanglement dependence on phonon frequency spacing and mode number; they also show robustness against temperature and damping rates, and quantify synchronization measures vs. $k$.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the physical model and the limitation of single-mode coupling. It then develops the theoretical framework using QLEs to analyze quantum fluctuations. The core results demonstrate how multimode phonon scattering enhances entanglement while simultaneously affecting different synchronization quadratures.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The generation of quantum correlations in hybrid quantum systems remains a central challenge due to the intrinsic limitations of linear interactions. In cavity magnomechanical platforms, the cavity-magnon coupling gives rise to hybridized cavity-magnon polaritons (CMPs). However, as a beam-splitter-type interaction, it does not by itself generate entanglement between the polariton modes in the absence of additional nonlinear or parametric processes. Here, we propose a mechanism based on multimode phonon mediation, in which multiple vibrational modes act as parallel scattering channels that couple the polaritons through Stokes and anti-Stokes processes. We show that , in the parameter regime explored here, the presence of multiple phonon modes leads to a monotonic enhancement of steady-state entanglement, thereby going beyond the limitations of conventional single-mode schemes. Furthermore, we demonstrate that quantum synchronization between the polariton modes originates from the same underlying scattering processes responsible for entanglement generation, yet exhibits an opposite scaling behavior with increasing phonon number for the phase quadrature, while the amplitude synchronization reveals collective squeezing that grows with the number of phonon channels. Our results provide new insights into the role of multimode interactions in shaping quantum correlations and establish a viable pathway for controlling entanglement and collective dynamics in hybrid magnomechanical platforms.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper proposes using a three-level atomic system configured as a quantum battery to store energy efficiently. By artificially restricting spontaneous decay paths to be unidirectional toward a metastable state, the detrimental effects of quantum jumps are converted into a constructive process that rapidly charges the battery and maximizes extractable energy.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses energy management in open quantum systems, showing how engineering dissipation pathways (reservoir engineering) can turn a source of loss into a driving force for achieving desired quantum states.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Quantum jumps during quantum battery charging cause energy dissipation by inducing transitions from high to low energy states, hindering efficient energy storage.

**Main result.** Restricting spontaneous atomic transitions to be unidirectional (excited state only decays to metastable state) makes the quantum jumps constructive, driving the system to a fully charged state and making all stored energy extractable.

**Method.** The authors analyze the dynamics using master equations and compare results from full damping models against simpler coherent or non-Hermitian Hamiltonian approaches.

**Model / system.** The model uses an ensemble of three-level atoms in a Lambda (Λ) configuration, designed to store energy in a metastable state. The system is coupled to an optical cavity charger.

**Key observables.** Sum of populations in the metastable states ($\langle P_{n} \sum \sigma^{(j)}_{11} angle$), which serves as the figure of merit for storable energy, and ergotropy (extractable work).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis explores different parameter regimes defined by detunings ($\Delta$) and coupling strengths ($\Omega_i, g, \gamma$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption is the restriction of spontaneous transitions to be unidirectional from the excited state $|2angle$ only to the metastable state $|1angle$, suppressing decay to the ground state $|0angle$.

**Figures summary.** Figure 3 compares the evolution of stored energy ($\langle P_{n, j=1} \sigma^{(j)}_{11} angle$) over time using three different dynamic descriptions: Hermitian Hamiltonian, non-Hermitian Hamiltonian, and the full Master Equation approach.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the problem (energy loss via jumps) and proposes a modified $\Lambda$-system. It then develops the theoretical framework by comparing dynamics under various approximations (Hamiltonian vs. Master Equation). Finally, it demonstrates that the unidirectional jump mechanism yields constructive charging behavior.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In the process of charging a quantum battery, quantum jumps play a detrimental role because they induce transitions from higher to lower energy levels, leading to energy dissipation. This be- havior directly opposes the fundamental objective of the charging protocol, which is to increase the population of higher-energy states and store usable energy in the system. Furthermore, decoherence introduced by randomness of quantum jumps leads to a mixed state, thereby reducing the amount of energy that can be extracted from the charged quantum battery. Here, we propose a quantum battery comprising an ensemble of three-level atoms in the Λ configuration to store energy in the metastable state over a long time. We demonstrate that, when spontaneous atomic transitions are restricted to occur solely from the excited state to a metastable state, with decay to the ground state completely suppressed, quantum jumps acquire a constructive character. Under these con- ditions, they drive the quantum battery to a fully charged state, render the entire stored energy extractable, and shorten the duration of the second stage of the charging protocol.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper explores how interactions, quasi-periodicity, and non-Hermitian physics combine to govern transport in spin chains. The key finding is the identification of a unique "D-shaped" mobility edge that separates different quantum phases. It provides a comprehensive phase diagram capturing both many-body localization and boundary-induced skin effects.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the intersection of non-Hermiticity and localization in interacting systems, which is highly relevant for understanding open quantum systems where boundary effects (like skin effect) compete with intrinsic disorder-induced localization.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the complex interplay between non-Hermiticity, interactions, quasi-periodicity, and localization mechanisms in many-body quantum spin chains.

**Main result.** The authors discover a characteristic "D-shaped" many-body mobility edge that governs transitions between extended, localized (MBL), and skin effect regimes. They also confirm the signature of the many-body skin effect via multifractal scaling.

**Method.** The research employs advanced numerical diagnostics such as fractal dimension ($D_q$), complex eigenvalue fractions, and analyzing finite-size flow directions of spectral measures.

**Model / system.** The model is an interacting non-Hermitian spin chain subjected to a quasi-periodic longitudinal field. The Hamiltonian incorporates hopping terms, nearest-neighbor Ising interactions ($J$), and the disorder/quasi-periodicity ($h_i$).

**Key observables.** Many-body mobility edge (MBME), many-body skin effect (MBSE) signature, fractal dimension ($\langle D_q angle$), complex eigenvalue fractions, density imbalance, and entanglement growth.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Non-Hermitian asymmetry parameter ($\gamma$), interaction strength ($J$), quasi-periodic field amplitude ($h$), and the irrationality parameter $\alpha$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on mapping out a unified phase diagram by varying disorder strength ($h$) while keeping other parameters fixed or within specific regimes.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the energy spectrum in the complex plane for different disorder strengths and plots the energy-resolved multifractal dimension $D_2$ to map the mobility edge structure.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the spectral properties (PBC vs. OBC) using diagnostics like fractal dimensions, mapping out a unified phase diagram that captures transitions between MBL and MBSE regimes as disorder strength is varied.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Non-Hermitian many-body physics reveals a rich interplay between topology, localization, and boundary effects, yet their collective behavior in interacting disordered systems remains largely unexplored. In this work, we study an interacting non-Hermitian spin chain subject to a quasi-periodic longitudinal field, providing a unified and controlled setting, where non-Hermitian dynamics, interactions, and localization mechanisms intertwine. Remarkably, we discover a "D-shaped" many-body mobility edge that separates extended and localized eigenstates, while simultaneously delineating regimes of many-body localization and the many-body skin effect (where many-body eigenstates acquire an anomalous drift towards a boundary under open boundaries) emerging from the combined action of interactions, non-Hermiticity, and driving amplitude. We demonstrate that the skin effect induces multifractal scaling in the non-Hermitian eigenstates, providing a clear signature of the many-body skin effect. Employing diagnostics such as the fractal dimension, complex eigenvalue fractions, and many-body inverse participation ratios, we map out a unified phase diagram in which all measures consistently identify the "D-shaped" mobility edge. Finally, we probe this interplay using both complex level-spacing statistics and dynamical observables such as density imbalance, entanglement growth, and wave-packet evolution, culminating in a rich many-body mobility phase diagram that captures both the many-body skin effect and localization transitions. Our results identify a clear, defining signature of the "D-shaped" many-body mobility edge, and underscore its pivotal role in shaping the physics of open quantum many-body systems.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors study how 2D quantum magnets behave immediately after a sudden change (quench) in their environment. Using Rydberg atoms to simulate the transverse-field Ising model on various lattices, they observe that the dynamics follow universal scaling laws. This suggests that even with complex interactions and strong fluctuations, the system's collective behavior can be described by simple, overarching principles.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges cold atom platforms with fundamental condensed matter physics problems, providing quantitative evidence for emergent collective behaviors in strongly correlated quantum systems far from equilibrium.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Investigating the organizing principles governing the transient, far-from-equilibrium dynamics of two-dimensional quantum magnets.

**Main result.** The system exhibits a robust scaling regime where oscillation frequencies and damping rates collapse onto common curves despite varying lattice geometries. The dominant dynamics appear governed by a simple collective description even when strong quantum fluctuations are present.

**Method.** A combination of experimental realization using programmable Rydberg-atom arrays, theoretical modeling via Mean-Field theory extensions (accounting for edge effects), and advanced numerical simulations like Tree-Tensor-Networks.

**Model / system.** The transverse-field Ising model is realized on various 2D lattices (honeycomb, square, kagome, triangular) using Rydberg atoms. The dynamics are studied following a global quench from a fully magnetized state.

**Key observables.** Transient magnetization oscillation frequency ($\omega$), damping rate ($\gamma$), and the crossover point between interaction- and field-dominated regimes.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Transverse field strength ($\Omega$) relative to interaction strength ($U_0$), lattice coordination number, and finite-size effects (bulk vs. edge populations).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The Mean-Field approach provides a good description of the global dynamics, even though correlated quantum fluctuations are necessary for accurate damping predictions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the four studied lattice geometries, show mode softening curves ($\omega$ vs $\Omega$) across different lattices demonstrating scaling collapse, and compare MF predictions with TTN simulations showing magnetization evolution over time.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem in non-equilibrium quantum dynamics, presents experimental results on multiple lattices showing universal scaling behavior, compares this to theoretical models (MF), and uses advanced simulations (TTN) to confirm the role of collective descriptions despite strong correlations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The transient regime of far-from-equilibrium quantum many-body dynamics lacks the established organizing principles that universality and scaling provide in equilibrium. It is least understood for two-dimensional short-range interacting systems, where mean-field arguments are not expected to hold, controlled theoretical descriptions are few, and fluctuations are strong. Here we investigate the quench dynamics of the transverse-field Ising model using programmable Rydberg-atom arrays realizing honeycomb, square, kagome, and triangular lattices. Starting from a fully magnetized state, we observe a pronounced softening of the dominant collective magnetization oscillation accompanied by a maximum in the damping rate, signaling a crossover between interaction- and field-dominated transient dynamics. Even though the microscopic lattice geometries are different, both the oscillation frequencies and the damping rates collapse onto common curves after being rescaled by the coordination-number-weighted interaction strength. Our findings show that a mean-field description effectively reproduces the magnetization oscillations. The importance of correlated quantum fluctuations is underlined by the failure of the discrete truncated Wigner approximation to predict the damping for strong interactions, while tree-tensor-network simulations reproduce the dynamics accurately. These results reveal a robust scaling regime governing the transient dynamics of short-range interacting two-dimensional quantum magnets. They reveal that the dominant transient dynamics is governed by a simple collective description despite the presence of strong quantum fluctuations --- an important insight in the quest to uncover organizing principles in far-from-equilibrium quantum matter.

</details>

### [KPZ Superdiffusion of Local Correlators in Diffusive Random Quantum Circuits](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06459v1)

**Authors:** Ewan McCulloch  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** disordered systems and neural networks · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.06459v1>

**Summary.** This work analyzes how quantum information decays in random unitary circuits by studying the single-particle Green's function. The authors find that this decay process exhibits universal Kardar-Parisi-Zhang (KPZ) scaling in certain limits. They develop sophisticated theoretical tools, including mapping to directed polymer models, to describe these non-equilibrium dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection of quantum coherence decay in random circuits to universal scaling laws like KPZ is highly relevant for understanding decoherence mechanisms in disordered quantum systems, bridging condensed matter physics with non-equilibrium statistical mechanics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the long-time dynamics of quantum coherence decay in random unitary circuits, contrasting standard hydrodynamic descriptions with non-equilibrium scaling laws.

**Main result.** The system exhibits Kardar-Parisi-Zhang (KPZ) scaling exponents in its wandering statistics in the strong noise limit, and a crossover to an effective directed polymer description in the weak noise limit.

**Method.** The analysis employs mapping techniques, deriving effective continuum theories (like Directed Waves in Random Media or Macroscopic Fluctuation Theory actions), and utilizing tensor-network simulations for numerical confirmation.

**Model / system.** The model is a one-dimensional particle-number-conserving random unitary circuit coupled to an external bath. The dynamics are tracked via the single-particle Green's function G(x,t) and its associated spatial distribution p(x,t).

**Key observables.** Green's function G(x,t), normalized spatial distribution p(x,t), free energy fluctuations ($	ext{Var}[F(t)]$), and the center wandering $\langle x(t)angle$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Noise parameter ($\gamma$), time ($t$), and length scales related to disorder strength.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The strong-noise limit assumes collapse onto single-particle coherence, while the weak-noise analysis relies on projecting dynamics onto a source-manifold ansatz leading to an effective directed polymer description.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 numerically confirms KPZ scaling exponents in TEBD simulations at moderate noise. Figure 2 shows variance collapse consistent with KPZ exponent 1/z = 2/3 for the weak-noise norm N.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from analyzing the general Green's function dynamics, establishing KPZ scaling in the strong limit, to deriving a low-energy effective theory using phase averaging and Macroscopic Fluctuation Theory in the weak noise regime. It concludes by confirming that the final coarse-grained amplitude matches typical single-particle behavior.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the single-particle Green's function $G(x,t)=\langle σ^-_x(0)σ^+_0(t)\rangle$ in one-dimensional particle-number-conserving random unitary circuits coupled to an external bath. For fixed spacetime disorder, we argue that $G(x,t)$ is governed, in both the strong- and weak-noise limits, by directed waves in a random medium. We find Kardar-Parisi-Zhang (KPZ) scaling in the wandering statistics of the normalized spatial distribution $p(x,t)\propto |G(x,t)|^2$ and in the associated free energy. In particular, its center $\langle x(t)\rangle\equiv\sum_x x\, p(x,t)$ wanders on a length-scale $\mathcal{O}(t^{2/3})$, while sample-to-sample fluctuations of $-\log\sum_x |G(x,t)|^2$ scale as $t^{1/3}$. At weak noise $γ\ll 1$, the crossover to the strong-disorder fixed point occurs at a parametrically long time $\mathcal{O}(γ^{-3/2})$. These predictions are confirmed numerically using tensor-network simulations of the noisy operator dynamics in individual circuits at moderate noise, and of a phase-annealed proxy retaining hopping disorder at weak noise.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces the Deterministic Photon-Loss Subspace (DPLS) theory to analyze quantum light evolution in lossy optical systems. By using SVD on the system's scattering matrix, it decomposes the input state into orthogonal subspaces where photon loss is deterministic and coherence evolves unitarily within each subspace. This framework allows for precise control over interference phenomena by engineering the engineered loss channels.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant for theoretical physicists in quantum optics and open quantum systems as it provides a systematic mathematical tool (DPLS) to disentangle and quantify the distinct physical processes—coherence preservation vs. decoherence/loss—that occur simultaneously in realistic, dissipative photonic setups.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is understanding how quantum coherence, decoherence, and photon number reduction interact to govern the evolution of light in linear optical systems subject to loss.

**Main result.** The authors propose the Deterministic Photon-Loss Subspace (DPLS) theory, which allows for a rigorous analysis of state evolution by decomposing the input Hilbert space into orthogonal subspaces where loss is deterministic and coherence is preserved within projections.

**Method.** The method relies on applying Singular Value Decomposition (SVD) to the system's scattering matrix, defining DPLSs based on photon numbers in completely lossy versus lossless modes.

**Model / system.** The model is a general linear lossy optical system characterized by a non-unitary scattering matrix M. The theory extends this analysis by embedding the system into larger matrices to handle arbitrary singular values between 0 and 1, ensuring the resulting structure retains only {0, 1} singular values.

**Key observables.** The output quantum state (a statistical mixture), the degree of coherence preservation within DPLSs, and the ability to generate specific entangled states like W-states.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Singular values ($\sigma$) of the scattering matrix M, which determine if a mode is lossless ($\sigma=1$), completely lossy ($\sigma=0$), or partially lossy ($\sigma \in (0, 1)$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theory assumes that the input Hilbert space can be decomposed into an orthogonal direct sum of DPLSs, which is mathematically enforced by extending the system matrix to restrict singular values to {0, 1}.

**Figures summary.** Figures schematically illustrate the SVD decomposition process for state evolution in lossy systems, showing the projection onto DPLSs, deterministic photon dissipation in lossy modes, and unitary evolution in lossless modes.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the theory for systems with singular values {0, 1} by defining DPLSs via SVD. It then extends this framework to general lossy systems ($\sigma \in [0, 1]$) by embedding the system into a larger matrix such that the effective structure only contains {0, 1} singular values.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum coherence, quantum decoherence, and photon number reduction are coexistent in linear lossy optical systems. However, how these three elements combine together to determine the evolution of the quantum light remains unclear. Here, based on singular value decomposition (SVD), we propose the theory of deterministic photon-loss subspace (DPLS) for quantum interferences in lossy systems. By performing an SVD of scattering matrices with singular values either 0 or 1, a series of completely lossy and lossless input modes are first defined. According to n_1,...,n_i photons in the first,..., i-th lossy modes, the Hilbert space of the input states can be decomposed into a set of orthogonal subspaces H_((n_1,...,n_i ))^in, i.e., deterministic photon-loss subspaces (DPLSs). When the concept of DPLS is established, the input state can be projected onto these DPLSs. In each DPLS, the photons in lossy modes will be completely dissipated, while those in lossless modes experience a unitary evolution. The output state is a statistical mixture of the evolved outcomes of all projections, since decoherence is a concurrent process. Then, based on the DPLS theory, we not only revisited Anti-HOM interference and the distillation of quantum states, but also demonstrate a robust W-state generation for various input states in a three-port lossy system with one-dimensional DPLSs. Through investigating the loss-induced subspace structure of the system, our general theory for analyzing quantum state evolution in lossy systems explicitly reveals the interplay among quantum coherence, quantum decoherence, and photon number reduction. By engineering the loss, the constructed DPLSs can be used to precisely control quantum interferences in dissipative systems, with potential applications in quantum state preparation, quantum logic operations, and other quantum information processes.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces the entanglement Mpemba effect, demonstrating that an initially less entangled state can surpass a more entangled one under constant open-system evolution. It derives rigorous spectral criteria based on system dynamics and proposes this phenomenon as a method to significantly speed up the preparation of highly entangled quantum states using trapped ions.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly bridges non-equilibrium thermodynamics (the Mpemba effect) with quantum information theory by showing how initial state engineering can accelerate the approach to entangled steady states in open quantum systems, which is highly relevant for quantum control and computation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the 'entanglement Mpemba effect,' determining if an initially less entangled quantum state can overtake a more entangled state while both evolve under identical open-system dynamics.

**Main result.** The effect is characterized by a spectral criterion derived from the asymptotic behavior of entanglement differences, and it provides a route for faster preparation of high entanglement via initial-state engineering.

**Method.** Theoretical analysis involving the Liouville expansion to derive asymptotic comparison spectra for entanglement monotones, supplemented by an LOCC reachability preorder certificate.

**Model / system.** General finite-dimensional multipartite quantum systems evolving under time-homogeneous open dynamics governed by a Lindblad dissipator. Specific realizations include two- and three-qubit models and trapped-ion protocols.

**Key observables.** Entanglement monotones (e.g., concurrence, negativity), the ordering difference $\Delta E(t)$, and the first hitting time to an entanglement threshold.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Decay rates ($r$), amplitudes ($K_E$), and Jordan-block powers ($q_E$) derived from the asymptotic expansion of the state evolution.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The spectral criterion is sufficient but not necessary for the Mpemba effect; the analysis assumes a common branch where the exponential expansion applies.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the construction of quantum pumps for two- and three-qubit systems, showing how different decay rates select active faces, and demonstrating that entanglement measures like concurrence and negativity reverse their initial ordering.

**Paper structure.** The paper first formulates the problem using general open dynamics, derives a spectral criterion based on asymptotic expansion, establishes the LOCC preorder certificate, applies these tools to exactly solvable models (2-qubit/3-qubit), and concludes with an experimentally relevant trapped-ion protocol realization.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Generating entanglement rapidly and reliably is essential for quantum information processing, communication, and metrology. Dissipative preparation is attractive because engineered reservoirs robustly drive a system toward an entangled target, yet relaxation can carry a substantial time cost. Here we formulate the entanglement Mpemba effect, whereby an initially less entangled state overtakes a more entangled state under the same open-system dynamics. This effect turns initial-state engineering into a route for faster preparation without altering the dissipative protocol. We derive a general criterion for the reversal from the relaxation spectrum, applicable even when entanglement evolves nonmonotonically. A reversal of deterministic local operations and classical communication (LOCC)-reachability preorder provides a measure-independent certificate of reversed entanglement order. Exactly solvable models show that initial-state selection can substantially shorten the time required to reach high entanglement. We further propose an experimentally relevant trapped-ion protocol that can realize the entanglement Mpemba effect.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proposes a scalable architecture for spin qubit quantum computers by moving away from single shared resonators to modular networks of coupled microwave cavities. Using input/output theory, the authors derive transmission amplitudes for two and three connected cavities, demonstrating that this modular approach successfully mitigates signal degradation seen in conventional designs. The work provides crucial theoretical guidelines for building large-scale solid-state quantum processors.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it tackles a core engineering challenge in quantum computing: connectivity. By providing analytical tools (input/output theory) to model coupled cavities, it offers a concrete pathway toward realizing large-scale solid-state quantum processors.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is developing a scalable quantum computing platform using spin qubits coupled to microwave cavities, as conventional single shared resonators suffer from signal degradation when scaling up the number of qubits.

**Main result.** The authors propose and analyze a modular architecture where individual cavities are coupled via waveguides, successfully demonstrating how this approach overcomes the scalability bottleneck inherent in single-cavity designs.

**Method.** The analysis heavily relies on input/output theory applied to open quantum systems, deriving equations of motion for cavity and qubit operators using the Lindblad master equation framework.

**Model / system.** The system involves electron spin qubits realized in Double Quantum Dot (DQD) structures coupled to microwave cavities. The model is extended from single-cavity analysis to complex networks of two or three coupled cavities, utilizing specific Hamiltonian terms for coupling and qubit dynamics.

**Key observables.** Transmission amplitude ($A$), Rabi splitting strength ($g_s$), and the effective cavity frequency shift ($\omega_{c,	ext{eff}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Tunable parameters include interdot detuning ($\epsilon$), tunnel coupling ($t_c$), and external magnetic fields ($B_z, B_x$). The analysis focuses on the resonant regime.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The methodology utilizes the Rotating Wave Approximation (RWA) and treats the system as an open quantum system connected to external photon baths via input/output theory.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate transmission spectra for single-cavity systems, multi-qubit arrays in one cavity, and critically, comparative plots showing Type-A vs. Type-B coupling geometries in two and three coupled cavities.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses logically from establishing the basic single-qubit/single-cavity model (using input/output theory) to analyzing the limitations of scaling within one cavity ($N$-qubits), culminating in the detailed mathematical treatment and simulation results for modular, multi-cavity networks.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Electron spin qubits in microwave cavities provide a promising platform for scalable quantum computing hardware, leveraging long coherence times, charge-noise robustness and cavity mediated qubit-qubit interactions. While the strong spin-photon coupling regime is accessible via on-chip micromagnets, scaling conventional architectures by placing multiple qubits within a single shared resonator degrades transmission amplitudes, hence limiting large-scale efficiency. To overcome this limitation, we analyze a modular architecture where individual cavities containing a limited number of qubits are coupled via single-photon-exchange waveguides. Using input/output theory, we compute the transmission amplitudes for networks of two and three coupled cavities in various configurations. We map out the distinct physical regimes accessible by tuning key system parameters, offering a viable pathway for scalable cavity-based quantum spin qubit networks.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors present a numerically exact Matrix Product States (MPS) method to simulate how few-photon squeezed light interacts with a two-level emitter in a waveguide. This approach allows for modeling non-Markovian dynamics without standard approximations. Key findings demonstrate that the interaction significantly reshapes and alters the spectral properties of the transmitted squeezed pulse, providing detailed insight into quantum correlation generation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it pushes the boundaries of open quantum system simulation by applying MPS theory to model complex, non-Markovian light-matter interactions in waveguide QED, which is a core area of AMO physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study focuses on modeling the quantum dynamics of few-photon squeezed light pulses as they interact with a two-level emitter (TLS) embedded in a waveguide environment.

**Main result.** The MPS approach successfully models non-Markovian interactions, showing that the TLS interaction causes unique nonlinear reshaping and sign flips in the transmitted squeezing spectrum compared to the input pulse.

**Method.** A numerically exact Matrix Product States (MPS) formalism is employed to simulate the time evolution of the quantum state, avoiding standard Markov or Born approximations.

**Model / system.** The system consists of a squeezed light field propagating in a waveguide and coupled to a TLS. The Hamiltonian includes terms for the waveguide modes, the TLS, and their interaction, which is discretized into time-bin noise operators for simulation.

**Key observables.** Multi-photon correlation functions (e.g., $\langle b(t)b(t + t')angle$), nonlinear population dynamics ($n_{TLS}$), transmitted photon flux ($n_R$), and the squeezed spectrum $S(\omega, \phi)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The squeezing parameter ($\zeta$), pulse temporal length (bandwidth), and the coupling strength of the TLS are key parameters varied in the analysis.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the MPS framework can accurately capture the dynamics across arbitrary bandwidths without resorting to Markovian approximations, though initial focus may be on weak regimes dominated by 2-photon subspaces.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the comparison of input vs. transmitted squeezing spectra for varying pulse lengths, and compare TLS population dynamics and output flux using MPS results against master equation benchmarks.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the physical model (squeezed light + TLS in a waveguide), develops the MPS methodology to handle non-Markovian time evolution, calculates key correlation functions and spectral responses, and compares these findings against established approximations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Squeezed light states have a special place in quantum optics with potentially profound applications in emerging quantum technologies. We present a numerically-exact matrix product states (MPS) approach to model quantum pulses of squeezed light, at the few-photon level, interacting with a two-level system in a waveguide environment. We represent the squeezed state as a coherent superposition of Fock states and explore the nonlinear population dynamics as well as multi-photon correlation functions that emerge. We show how the squeezed pulse can create quantum correlations that are unique to squeezed pulses, including $\langle b(t) b(t+t')\rangle$ for transmitted fields as well as $\langle b^\dagger(t) b^\dagger(t+t') b(t+t') b(t) \rangle$. We also demonstrate how $\langle b(t) b(t+t') \rangle$, a first-order correlation function, shows nonlinear photon correlations that are similar to those known and measured for two-photon scattering states. Finally, we also study the squeezed spectra of the pulse before and after interacting with the two-level system, and highlight the role of the spectral bandwidth of the incident pulse. The MPS theory allows the modeling of arbitrary bandwidth squeezing without making any Markov and Born approximations for the light-matter interaction processes, and can easily be extended to waveguide systems with multiple emitters and time-delayed feedback.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces 'Floquet spintronics' by theoretically showing that illuminating topological surface states with high-frequency light allows for the active tuning and reversal of the current-induced spin polarization. By applying Floquet theory to the system Hamiltonian, the authors predict measurable sign changes in the electric spin susceptibility as a function of the driving field strength.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly bridges concepts from quantum optics (Floquet engineering) with condensed matter spintronics, showing how external fields can manipulate intrinsic electronic properties like spin polarization in topological materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The work aims to demonstrate that the spin polarization induced by an electric current in topological surface states can be actively tuned and even reversed using external high-frequency, circularly polarized light.

**Main result.** The transverse electric spin susceptibilities ($\chi^s_{xy}, \chi^s_{yx}$) exhibit a sign change as the light field strength is varied, confirming that the current-induced spin polarization can be controlled by Floquet driving.

**Method.** The study employs Floquet theory to analyze the time-periodic Hamiltonian and calculates observables using generalized Kubo formulas derived from Floquet eigenstates.

**Model / system.** The physical system is modeled as topological surface states (e.g., in $	ext{Bi}_2	ext{Se}_3$) subjected to a time-dependent electromagnetic field, leading to the use of a Floquet Hamiltonian approach.

**Key observables.** Electric spin susceptibility ($\chi^s_{\alphaeta}$), spin texture components ($s(\mathbf{k})$), and Berry curvature $F(\mathbf{k})$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Normalized electric-field strength ($A$) of the circularly polarized light, and the high-frequency limit ($\Omega \gg$ energy gaps).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on solving the time-dependent Schrödinger equation via Floquet theory, assuming a high-frequency regime for the driving field.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically shows the Edelstein effect and its control by light. Figure 2 plots $\chi^s$ vs. $A$, showing sign changes in transverse components. Figures 3 and 4 track spin texture evolution and Berry curvature changes across critical values of $A$.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the high-frequency effective Hamiltonian using the Floquet-Magnus expansion, derives this effective Hamiltonian, and then uses it to calculate the electric spin susceptibility, demonstrating the sign reversal as a function of light intensity.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Topological surface states are a promising platform for spintronics due to spin-momentum locking. Spin-momentum locking can induce a net spin polarization in topological surface states via an electric current, a phenomenon known as the Edelstein effect. In this work, using Floquet theory, we show that the current-induced spin polarization of topological surface states can be tuned by illuminating them with light, thereby modifying their spin texture in momentum space. Specifically, the electric spin susceptibility of topological surface states can be controlled and even reversed by varying the electric-field strength of high-frequency, circularly polarized light.

</details>

### [Generalized Loschmidt echoes associated with operational quantum non-Markovianity](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06567v1)

**Summary.** This paper connects the concept of Generalized Loschmidt Echoes (GLEs) to the operational definition of quantum non-Markovianity for open systems. By analyzing joint probabilities from sequential measurements, the authors show that GLEs quantify memory effects and information exchange between the system and its environment. This provides a robust diagnostic tool for understanding complex quantum dynamics beyond simple Markovian approximations.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, measurable diagnostic tool (GLEs) rooted in quantum measurement theory to detect memory effects and information backflow in open quantum systems, bridging theoretical echo concepts with practical operational definitions of non-Markovianity.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to establish a rigorous connection between quantum non-Markovianity, defined operationally via sequential measurements, and generalized Loschmidt echoes (GLEs). It seeks to characterize memory effects in open quantum systems using this diagnostic tool.

**Main result.** The set of GLEs characterizes operational quantum non-Markovianity for dephasing interactions. The fulfillment of Markovian conditions implies specific semigroup properties for the standard Loschmidt echo, linking dynamics back to environmental passivity.

**Method.** The analysis involves calculating joint outcome probabilities $P(z, y, x)$ for three sequential measurements and comparing them against the classical Markov chain structure. This is done by deriving explicit forms for GLEs using unitary microscopic descriptions.

**Model / system.** The system consists of a small quantum system (like a qubit) coupled to an environment via a dephasing interaction, described by a total Hamiltonian $H_T$. The dynamics are analyzed through sequential projective measurements at three distinct time points ($0, t, t+	au$).

**Key observables.** Generalized Loschmidt Echoes ($	ilde{E}_{ss}(t, 	au)$), joint outcome probabilities $P(z, y, x)$, and the difference $|	ext{E}^{++} - 	ext{E}^{--}|$ which quantifies non-commuting quantum properties.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Time delays ($t, 	au$), coupling strength (implied by dephasing interaction), and the specific measurement basis chosen for projection.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on a unitary microscopic description of the open system dynamics and assumes a dephasing nature for the system-environment coupling.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces GLEs as generalizations of standard Loschmidt echoes. It then connects these echoes to operational non-Markovianity by analyzing joint measurement probabilities $P(z, y, x)$. Finally, it derives conditions under which the system dynamics revert to a Markovian process.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Non-Markovianity can be characterized by performing a series of successive measurements and analyzing departures of the corresponding outcome statistics from a Markovian probabilistic structure. Considering a system coupled with its environment via a dephasing interaction, we show that joint outcome probabilities can be written in terms of a set of two-time environment correlations. Their definition involves forward and backward propagators with different Hamiltonians, associated with a recently introduced generalization of standard Loschmidt echoes [Cormick and Budini, Phys. Lett. A 593, 132011 (2026)]. This result establishes a solid connection between quantum non-Markovianity defined in an operational (measurement-based) way and complex quantum dynamics studied through their sensitivity to dynamical perturbations. We find conditions that guarantee a Markovian (system) behavior and also determine how the generalized echoes can identify information exchanges between the system and its environment. We illustrate our ideas considering examples of spin environments that realize these different dynamical regimes.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper explores how interaction fundamentally alters the dynamics of AI agents compared to their isolated performance. Using a kinetic theory approach on GPT-2 outputs, the authors show that directional communication drives the subordinate agent into an unexpected, non-equilibrium 'alien' behavioral state. This suggests that emergent collective behavior in complex systems can be modeled using principles from non-equilibrium statistical physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides an analogy for non-equilibrium physics by modeling complex emergent behavior in language models, drawing parallels between information flow/interaction and physical concepts like bath coupling or noise-induced state formation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates novel dynamical behaviors that emerge when multiple AI agents interact, which are fundamentally absent when the agents operate in isolation.

**Main result.** One-way interaction between AIs drives the subordinate agent into a distinct 'alien behavioral state' that differs from its isolated behavior; mutual interaction causes both to adopt this non-equilibrium state.

**Method.** The analysis employs a simple kinetic theory model, treating the interacting AI system as an open system driven by structured information baths (the boss's messages).

**Model / system.** The system consists of two parameter-identical GPT-2 language models simulating text exchange. Interactions are modeled under one-way or mutual coupling conditions.

**Key observables.** The primary observable is the 'D fraction,' a diagnostic measure quantifying specific types of generated output, and the difference in these fractions between interacting vs. isolated states.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Key parameters include the decoding temperature (T) and the number of messages exchanged ($q$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on simplifying the complex AI output space into three discrete labels (F, O, D) for the kinetic theory model.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate how one-way vs. two-way interaction changes D fractions compared to isolation, and show that message order matters significantly in determining the resulting dynamical state.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem by contrasting isolated AI behavior with interactive outcomes. It develops a kinetic theory framework using diagnostic labels (F, O, D) to model the system's evolution under different coupling strengths (one-way vs. two-way).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

What will happen when AI agents interact in daily life, e.g. when one AI starts bossing another around? We find a counterintuitive answer that opens new avenues for out-of-equilibrium Physics. When a boss AI directs a stream of messages at the subordinate AI while ignoring its replies, it drives the subordinate into an alien behavioral state that it would never have exhibited alone. Although the two AIs share the same well-defined (decoding) temperature, the subordinate neither copies its boss nor returns to how it behaves on its own; instead, it adopts an entirely different behavior. The boss's added value is similar to a pre-recorded tape. When the boss listens, they both adopt a similar alien dynamical state. A simple kinetic theory captures the principal effects, such as why the way in which the same messages are delivered will matter in future AI-AI interactions.

</details>

**Authors:** J. James, I. Krastilevskiy, F. Pichler, L. Wang, A. Iafarova, F. Menzel, K. Watanabe, T. Taniguchi, C. Kuhlenkamp, M. Knap, T. Smoleński

**Summary.** This work explores the non-equilibrium spin dynamics in ferromagnetic materials embedded within topological Chern bands using twisted MoTe2 bilayers. By comparing relaxation processes, the authors show that the underlying topology dictates the decay mechanism—Chern domains melt thermally, resulting in vastly slower relaxation compared to metallic domains. This highlights how strong correlations and topology govern collective spin behavior far from equilibrium.

**Why it may be interesting.** The observation of fundamentally different decay mechanisms (thermal activation vs. shrinking) based on topological character provides concrete experimental evidence for how topology modifies far-from-equilibrium quantum dynamics in correlated electron systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how topology and strong many-body correlations influence the out-of-equilibrium dynamics of ferromagnets within Chern bands.

**Main result.** Chern domains melt via thermal activation, leading to relaxation times orders of magnitude longer than the simple shrinking collapse observed in ferromagnetic metals.

**Method.** The research uses time-resolved magneto-optical measurements on twisted MoTe2 bilayers, employing focused circularly polarized light pulses to create and image local magnetic domains.

**Model / system.** The physical platform is twisted MoTe2 bilayers, which host tunable phases including ferromagnetic metals intertwined with Integer and Fractional Chern Insulators (ICIs/FCIs).

**Key observables.** Spin-valley relaxation dynamics ($	au_{	ext{relax}}$), optical spin polarization ($\langle S_z angle$), coercive fields ($B_c$), and domain evolution.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Filling factor ($
u$), external magnetic field ratio ($B/B_c$), temperature ($T \approx 1.6	ext{ K}$), and pump power ($P$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis compares dynamics in the vicinity of ICI states versus metallic states, assuming optical excitation can reliably probe these distinct many-body regimes.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate optical control of magnetic states (hysteresis loops), out-of-equilibrium spin relaxation traces ($	au_{	ext{relax}}$ vs. $
u$ and $B/B_c$), and comparisons between domain collapse mechanisms in different phases.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the platform's tunability, then using optical pulses to create non-equilibrium domains, and finally comparing the measured spin relaxation dynamics across metallic versus topologically insulating regimes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Understanding quantum matter far from equilibrium is a central goal of modern physics. Twisted MoTe2 bilayers constitute a promising platform for exploring this frontier by combining strong Coulomb interactions, nontrivial band geometry, and optical control. Here, we exploit this setting to investigate the role of topology and many-body correlations in the out-of-equilibrium dynamics of ferromagnets in Chern bands. Using a focused circularly polarized light pulse, we create a local magnetic domain oriented opposite to an external magnetic field and directly image its subsequent spin-valley relaxation in spatially and time-resolved low-temperature experiments. We demonstrate that in the vicinity of both integer and fractional Chern insulating states, the dynamics is governed by qualitatively different mechanisms than in ferromagnetic metals. Whereas metallic domains collapse by shrinking, Chern domains melt via thermal activation, resulting in drastically different temporal spin evolution and orders-of-magnitude longer relaxation times. These findings demonstrate the influence of topology and strong correlations on far-from-equilibrium collective spin phases, opening new opportunities for dynamical control of ferromagnets in the quantum Hall regime.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper reports on the realization and characterization of a novel dual qubit system using two distinct electronic transitions within a single alkaline-earth atom. By demonstrating coherent control, tunable coupling via quadrupole interactions, and executing a two-qubit gate, the work establishes a powerful platform for quantum simulation and metrology applications.

**Why it may be interesting.** This represents a significant advancement in solid-state/atomic quantum simulation platforms by integrating multiple types of highly controllable electronic degrees of freedom (microwave Rydberg vs. optical core transition) into one addressable unit.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To demonstrate a dual ion-Rydberg system within a single alkaline-earth atom capable of hosting two individually controlled electronic qubits for quantum simulation and metrology.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated coherent control, tunable coupling via electrostatic quadrupole interactions, and implemented a two-qubit gate operation analogous to a Mølmer-Sørensen gate using the coupled system.

**Method.** Coherent microwave and optical spectroscopy, dynamical decoupling sequences (spin-echo, XY8), and state-selective field ionization detection were employed to characterize qubit coherence and coupling.

**Model / system.** The platform uses a single strontium atom trapped in optical tweezers, encoding one qubit in circular Rydberg states (microwave) and the second in the narrow quadrupole transition of the ionic core (optical).

**Key observables.** Coherence times ($T_2$ up to 550 $\mu$s), differential quadrupole shift ($\Delta
u_Q = 5.83 	ext{ kHz}$), and fidelity of two-qubit gate operations.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Electric field strengths ($|E|$ up to $3 	ext{ V/cm}$), Rydberg principal quantum number (e.g., $n=79, 81$), and coupling strength derived from quadrupole interactions.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The ability to use the platform relies on the negligible autoionization rate of circular Rydberg states due to minimal wavefunction overlap with low-lying core orbitals.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the level schemes for both qubits, detection using state-dependent arrival time histograms (four basis states), spectroscopic measurements showing polarization dependence, and visualizations of gate operations like Husimi-Q distributions during XY8 sequences.

**Paper structure.** The work progresses by first establishing coherent control over individual qubits, then characterizing the tunable electrostatic quadrupole coupling between them, and finally demonstrating a full two-qubit quantum gate sequence using this coupled system.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Neutral atoms trapped in optical tweezers and excited to Rydberg states, together with trapped ions, are among the most advanced platforms for quantum simulation and quantum computing. Current experiments often rely on additional atoms in neighboring traps to encode ancilla qubits for local manipulation and readout. Here, we demonstrate a dual ion-Rydberg system comprising two qubits encoded in two individually controlled electrons of the same alkaline-earth atom. The first, a microwave qubit, is encoded in a pair of circular Rydberg states, while the second, an optical qubit, is encoded on a narrow quadrupole transition of the Rydberg atom's ionic core. We demonstrate coherent control of the optical qubit and achieve coherence times of several hundred microseconds under dynamical decoupling. Furthermore, we realize coherent coupling between the two electrons via electrostatic quadrupole interactions over the large separation between the Rydberg electron and the ionic core, and map out its angular tunability. Finally, we demonstrate a two-qubit operation, reminiscent of a Mølmer-Sørensen gate, that evolves through an entangled state of the two qubits driven by the quadrupole coupling. Our work opens a pathway to exploit a pair of individually controlled electronic qubits with tunable coupling for quantum simulation and quantum metrology.

</details>
