**Summary.** This paper rigorously investigates the sources of heating in optical lattices trapping ultracold atoms by separating the contributions from intensity and phase fluctuations in the trapping lasers. The authors show that phase noise can dominate lattice heating under certain conditions, providing a predictive theoretical framework validated against experimental measurements using ${^6	ext{Li}}$ atoms.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant to AMO physics as it addresses a critical technical limitation (heating) in modern quantum simulation platforms. By quantifying phase noise, it guides experimentalists toward improving laser stability for realizing deeper and more complex quantum simulations.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the fundamental sources of heating experienced by ultracold atoms trapped in optical lattices, specifically disentangling the roles of intensity noise versus phase noise.

**Main result.** The research demonstrates that phase noise can become the dominant source of lattice heating, particularly when using light atoms or deep optical lattices, providing a quantitative method to predict this effect.

**Method.** The authors combine theoretical modeling (using fluctuating harmonic potentials and Fermi's Golden Rule) with experimental measurements of laser phase noise spectra via self-heterodyne interferometry.

**Model / system.** The system involves ultracold atoms trapped in optical lattices formed by multiple interfering laser beams. The analysis models the potential as a fluctuating harmonic oscillator whose fluctuations are driven by both intensity and relative phase noise from the lasers.

**Key observables.** Measured heating rates ($\dot{Q}$ or $T(	ext{thold})$) for atomic temperature evolution, and independently measured power spectral densities ($S_{\phi L}(\omega)$) of the laser phase noise.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Reduced lattice depth ($s$), time delay ($	au$) between interfering beams, and characteristic frequencies like $\omega_{	ext{lat}}$ and $\omega_R$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the potential can be approximated by a fluctuating harmonic oscillator near the lattice minima. Specific derivations rely on approximations for short time delays or symmetric geometry.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 compares normalized heating rates due to intensity versus phase noise in different lattice depths ($s=10$ vs $s=1000$). Figure 2 shows measured temperature decay curves used to extract total heating rates. Figure 5 displays the measured power spectral density of the laser phase fluctuations.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the theoretical framework by calculating heating rates from both noise sources using a fluctuating harmonic potential model. It then details the experimental measurement of these noise spectra and compares the resulting theoretical predictions for heating rates against direct measurements on ${^6	ext{Li}}$ atoms in a triangular lattice.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We experimentally and theoretically study the origin of heating in optical lattices by disentangling the respective roles of intensity and phase noise depending on the lattice parameters. While intensity noise is widely identified as a major limiting factor, we show that phase noise can become the dominant heating source, especially for light atoms and deep optical lattices. We provide a simple theoretical framework to predict the phase-noise-induced heating from the power spectral density of the laser phase noise, which can be measured experimentally. We show that such predictions can accurately reproduce the measured heating rates of lithium-6 atoms in a triangular lattice. Our approach is readily generalized to other lattice geometries and atomic species.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes Hawking evaporation in Horndeski gravity, showing that coupling corrections cause the black hole's temperature to dynamically quench. This process halts evaporation at a stable remnant mass, leading to an asymptotic logarithmic growth of entanglement entropy rather than complete disappearance.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on gravity, the analysis of entanglement entropy transitioning from linear to logarithmic growth near a saturation point is highly relevant to open quantum systems and information retention in non-equilibrium processes.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how Horndeski gravity corrections alter the semiclassical Hawking evaporation process of a black hole, specifically addressing the fate of quantum information during late-time evaporation.

**Main result.** The BH does not evaporate completely but halts at a stable, macroscopic cold remnant ($M_r$), and the entanglement entropy transitions to an asymptotic logarithmic regime, suggesting information is retained in this remnant.

**Method.** The analysis uses semiclassical methods involving evaluating curvature invariants, applying anomaly calculations (conformal trace and Robinson-Wilczek), and deriving resummed temperature/entropy equations.

**Model / system.** The system is a two-dimensional Callan-Giddings-Harvey-Strominger (CGHS) black hole geometry perturbed by first-order Horndeski scalar-tensor couplings. The analysis is performed in the context of dimensional reduction from 4D gravity.

**Key observables.** Hawking temperature ($T_H$), fine-grained entanglement entropy, surface gravity ($\kappa$), and the BH mass evolution $M(t)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The remnant mass scale ($M_r$) and coupling constants ($\gamma_3$, $\lambda$). The late-time behavior is characterized by a power-law freeze-out: $M(t) - M_r \propto 1/t$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on extrapolating resummed temperature profiles to the boundary of perturbative control, and certain constraints were imposed during dimensional reduction to maintain stability.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the power-law freeze-out of mass difference ($M(t) - M_r$) for G3 corrections, and show how luminosity vanishes smoothly at the remnant mass $M_r$.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing that coordinate singularities are artifacts, then deriving dynamical quenching of the Hawking temperature due to anomaly interactions. Finally, it analyzes the late-time evolution using entropy conservation laws to confirm the stable remnant state.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the semiclassical Hawking evaporation of a two-dimensional Callan-Giddings-Harvey-Strominger (CGHS) black hole perturbed by first-order Horndeski scalar-tensor couplings. By evaluating the exact scalar curvature invariants, we demonstrate that coordinate singularities near the apparent horizon parametrize a physical deformation of the surface gravity while preserving a strictly regular event horizon. In the linear-order $γ_3 ΦX$ coupling sector, the surface gravity and correspondingly the Hawking temperature decreases dynamically as the black hole loses mass. Extrapolating this cooling effect via a resummed temperature equation suggests that evaporation halts, potentially leaving behind a stable, macroscopic cold remnant at the boundary of perturbative control. Utilizing both the conformal trace anomaly and the Robinson-Wilczek gravitational anomaly, we establish that the asymptotic radiation flux identically reflects this geometric shift. Consequently, the fine-grained entanglement entropy of the Hawking radiation departs from linear growth and transitions to an asymptotic logarithmic regime. This indicates that quantum information may be permanently retained within the remnant, altering the standard semiclassical Page curve without requiring a breakdown of horizon regularity.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the critical dynamics of a scalar field near four dimensions, focusing on how inertia and dissipation compete. Using supersymmetric field theory and RG methods, the authors demonstrate that while different dynamic limits exist, the propagating regime is inherently unstable when subjected to local equilibrium dissipation. This highlights the fine-tuned nature required for sustained wave propagation at criticality.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous treatment of dynamic critical exponents and the stability analysis concerning dissipation provides deep insights into non-equilibrium dynamics relevant to phase transitions in many physical systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the critical dynamics of a non-conserved order parameter, specifically analyzing the competition between propagating (relativistic) and overdamped (relaxational) behavior near criticality.

**Main result.** The paper shows that while both dynamic limits share static fixed points, they possess distinct dynamical scaling regimes; furthermore, the propagating regime is found to be unstable to any local equilibrium dissipation perturbation.

**Method.** The analysis employs a supersymmetric field-theory formulation combined with two-loop Renormalization Group (RG) techniques applied near four spatial dimensions.

**Model / system.** The system is a scalar field theory ($\phi(t, x)$) in thermal equilibrium whose dynamics are governed by an action incorporating both inertial and dissipative terms. The analysis compares the strictly overdamped limit to the dissipationless propagating limit.

**Key observables.** Dynamic critical exponent (z), static Gaussian and Wilson–Fisher fixed points, and the stability of the RG flow concerning local dissipation.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The ratio controlling local friction ($X$), the dimensionality ($d=4$), and the presence/absence of local equilibrium dissipation.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theory is assumed to be in thermal equilibrium, requiring adherence to the fluctuation–dissipation relation (KMS symmetry).

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically builds the supersymmetric structure, derives the effective action, analyzes static corrections, and then treats the overdamped and dissipationless limits separately before concluding with a comprehensive RG flow analysis.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The critical dynamics of a non-conserved order parameter is generally expected to become overdamped at long distances, even when propagating modes occur at microscopic or intermediate scales. We investigate the critical dynamics of a scalar field theory in thermal equilibrium which, in addition to local friction and noise, also contains a time-dependent second-order kinetic term. We show how to build a supersymmetric field-theory formulation. Using a two-loop expansion about four spatial dimensions, we show that the propagating and strictly overdamped limits share the same static Gaussian and Wilson-Fisher fixed points but realize distinct dynamical scaling regimes. The overdamped limit reproduces Model A. On the surface where local friction and noise vanish, the theory instead supports an interacting propagating fixed point whose dynamic exponent receives corrections at two loops. We demonstrate that coarse-graining does not generate a local dissipative operator on this surface, which therefore remains invariant under the RG flow. Local dissipation is nevertheless relevant at the propagating fixed point: an arbitrarily small equilibrium friction-noise perturbation drives the flow away from propagating scaling. Propagating critical dynamics thus defines a consistent but fine-tuned regime that is unstable to local equilibrium dissipation.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces QCORE, a novel digital reference architecture designed to manage all necessary services—control, readout, calibration, and error correction—for scaling quantum processors. By partitioning tasks into dedicated hardware modules and enforcing strict versioning via safe-commit mechanisms, QCORE achieves superior real-time performance and reliability for complex quantum experiments.

**Why it may be interesting.** For AMO physicists working on open quantum systems, this work provides a crucial blueprint for the classical control infrastructure needed to reliably operate and characterize superconducting qubits in real-time, directly addressing hardware bottlenecks beyond the physics itself.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Scaling quantum processors requires integrating complex, concurrent control, readout, calibration, and error correction services under strict latency and shared-resource constraints.

**Main result.** The proposed QCORE architecture successfully unifies these disparate functions into a single digital reference boundary, demonstrating significant performance improvements in feedback latency and error reduction compared to existing systems.

**Method.** The authors designed QCORE as a hardware-partitioned, real-time execution architecture that uses transaction-level and event-driven modeling to manage task admission, resource scheduling, and atomic state commits.

**Model / system.** The system targets Quantum Processing Units (QPUs) utilizing transmon qubits. The control flow involves managing tasks through a Runtime Manager, executing sequences like Rabi-Ramsey calibration, and incorporating error correction codes (QEC).

**Key observables.** P99 latency of the feedback path ($\approx 1.984 L_{	ext{max}}$), reduction in mean frequency error ($83.2\%$), and state-assignment error reduction from $10.39\%$ to $5.37\%$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Background load (e.g., 0.8), maximum readout drift, and the number of configuration transactions (100,000).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Deterministic execution is defined behaviorally rather than by fixed RTL cycle latency; late or version-mismatched results cannot alter the current real-time state.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the QCORE architecture, comparing it to conventional control methods, and detail functional flows for calibration (Rabi-Ramsey) and Randomized Benchmarking (RB).

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the limitations of existing quantum control systems, proposes the modular QCORE reference architecture with its four hardware partitions, details the execution flow through task management and resource scheduling, and validates performance using multiple complex workloads.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Scalable quantum processors require control, readout, feedback, calibration, and error correction to coexist under bounded latency and shared-resource constraints, whereas existing platforms typically optimize only a subset of these capabilities. This article presents QCORE (Quantum-Control-Oriented Real-Time Execution), a QPU-side digital control reference architecture positioned between the Host and a platform-specific analog/mixed-signal front end. QCORE separates task management, shared resources, hard-real-time execution, and long-timescale services into four hardware partitions. A fast-result sideband closes same-round feedback, a Measurement Packet provides a traceable measurement and service interface, and a common service-control skeleton, Tile-local QEC, and versioned safe-point commit organize calibration, error correction, and long-term state updates. Transaction-level, event-driven, and quantum-behavioral models are used for evaluation. At a background load of 0.8, the $P_{99}$ latency of the shared Measurement Packet/Event feedback path is $(1.984\pm0.004)L_{\max}$. Closed-loop operation reduces the mean frequency error by $83.2\%\pm0.8\%$ and lowers the state-assignment error at maximum readout drift from $10.39\%\pm0.54\%$ to $5.37\%\pm0.29\%$. No unsafe acceptance or mixed-version observation is observed in 100,000 configuration transactions, and Tile-local QEC reduces modeled global-boundary demand and yields a $2.08\times$ capacity-normalized scaling estimate.

</details>

**Authors:** Benedikt M. Reible, Albert Much, Rainer Verch, Christof Schütte, Luigi Delle Site  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.07246v1>

**Summary.** This paper generalizes the two-sided Bogoliubov inequality from finite quantum systems to abstract von Neumann algebras. By employing relative entropy and unbounded perturbation theory, the authors establish rigorous variational bounds for the relative free energy. This provides a mathematically robust, thermodynamically grounded criterion for quantifying entanglement in complex, infinite-dimensional quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides rigorous mathematical tools (generalized inequalities) to quantify entanglement in complex, infinite-dimensional systems by framing it within established thermodynamic principles of statistical mechanics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The work aims to generalize the quantum-mechanical two-sided Bogoliubov inequality, which links free energy to quantum correlations, to the abstract setting of von Neumann algebras.

**Main result.** They establish computable bounds for the relative free energy using generalized inequalities and derive variational expressions that extend bounded-perturbation principles to unbounded systems.

**Method.** The methodology involves advanced mathematical tools from operator algebras, specifically utilizing Araki-Uhlmann relative entropy and the framework of unbounded perturbation theory for KMS states.

**Model / system.** The analysis considers general quantum systems described by a von Neumann algebra $M$ with a faithful KMS state $\omega$, modeling interactions via perturbations $U$. The initial physical context involves interacting particles in infinite-dimensional systems, such as those found in quantum field theory.

**Key observables.** Relative free energy ($F(\omega_U, \omega)$) and the entanglement quantification derived from thermodynamic measurements.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Inverse temperature ($eta$), interaction strength (related to $U$), and the structure of the von Neumann algebra.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The generalization relies on assuming the existence of a faithful KMS state $\omega$ and employing advanced mathematical machinery like modular theory.

**Paper structure.** The paper reviews the known quantum-mechanical inequality, then systematically generalizes it to von Neumann algebras using relative entropy and unbounded perturbation theory. Finally, variational expressions for the relative free energy are derived as a key result.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The quantum-mechanical two-sided Bogoliubov inequality provides upper and lower bounds for the free energy required to separate a system of interacting particles into independent subsystems. The bounds can be calculated straightforwardly from the ensemble average of the interface energy, bypassing the direct evaluation of the free energy. In this work, we generalize the two-sided Bogoliubov inequality to arbitrary von Neumann algebras by employing the Araki-Uhlmann relative entropy and the framework of unbounded perturbation theory of KMS states. Furthermore, we obtain variational expressions for the relative free energy that extend existing bounded-perturbation principles to the unbounded setting. Crucially, these mathematical developments yield a physically well-founded thermodynamic criterion for the quantification of entanglement in infinite-dimensional systems.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper experimentally and theoretically investigates how crystal thickness affects the dimensionality (Schmidt number) of OAM-entangled photon pairs generated via SPDC. The key finding is the first observation of non-monotonic behavior, which arises from spatial walk-off effects in anisotropic crystals. This work emphasizes that using the full phase-matching theory, rather than approximations, is essential for accurate quantum state characterization.

**Why it may be interesting.** This research provides a crucial refinement to the theoretical understanding of high-dimensional quantum state generation in optics, showing that neglecting geometric effects like walk-off can lead to fundamentally incorrect predictions about entanglement resource scaling with device size.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how the dimensionality of orbital angular momentum (OAM) entangled states, quantified by the Schmidt number, changes as a function of nonlinear crystal thickness.

**Main result.** Contrary to previous predictions, the authors experimentally and theoretically demonstrate that the Schmidt number exhibits non-monotonic behavior—increasing after an initial decrease—due to spatial walk-off effects in anisotropic crystals.

**Method.** The work combines experimental measurements using SPDC with theoretical modeling that incorporates the full phase-matching function, explicitly accounting for spatial walk-off terms neglected in prior approximations.

**Model / system.** The system involves generating OAM-entangled photon pairs via Spontaneous Parametric Down-Conversion (SPDC) within an anisotropic nonlinear crystal like BBO. The state is analyzed using the Schmidt decomposition formalism.

**Key observables.** Angular Schmidt spectrum ($S_l$) and the associated Schmidt number ($K$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Crystal thickness ($L$), phase-matching angle ($	heta_p$), and the spatial walk-off term ($\Delta k_{	ext{extra}}^z$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary limitation addressed is the failure of previous theoretical models that used approximate phase-matching conditions by neglecting spatial walk-off.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 compares the Schmidt number dependence on thickness, showing a monotonic decrease (approximate) versus a non-monotonic increase (exact/experimental). Appendix figures compare zero-radial-mode spectra using both approximate and exact formulations, highlighting divergence at larger thicknesses.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the theoretical framework for OAM entanglement via SPDC. It then contrasts previous approximate models with the full model incorporating spatial walk-off. The core of the work presents experimental measurements confirming the non-monotonic trend predicted by the exact theory, followed by detailed comparisons of spectral convergence across different approximations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The orbital angular momentum (OAM) of photons provides a high-dimensional resource for quantum information protocols. The dimensionality of OAM-entangled states generated via spontaneous parametric down-conversion (SPDC) is quantified by the angular Schmidt spectrum. Here, we experimentally investigate the dependence of the angular Schmidt spectrum on the thickness of the nonlinear crystal. Contrary to previous studies reporting a monotonic decrease in the Schmidt number with increasing crystal thickness, we report the first experimental observation of a nonmonotonic behavior, as we demonstrate an increase in the Schmidt number beyond a certain crystal thickness. We attribute this to the spatial walk-off effect in the anisotropic nonlinear crystal and explain it using a theoretical model that is devoid of standard phase-matching approximations. These findings can have important implications for high-dimensional entangled state generation.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper investigates the stabilizer Rényi entropy in critical quantum chains, particularly the TFI model. By introducing a fugacity-resolved partition function, the authors map the problem to discrete Selberg ensembles. They derive exact mathematical results for specific Rényi indices ($\alpha=1/2, 1, 2$), revealing deep connections between entanglement measures and advanced combinatorial identities.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection between quantum entanglement measures (Rényi entropy) in critical systems and advanced mathematical physics tools like Selberg integrals and Jack-Kostka representations provides deep insights into the structure of ground state correlations in 1D quantum matter.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is determining which Rényi indices allow for an exact finite-size compression of the all-minors sum derived from calculating stabilizer Rényi entropy in critical quantum chains.

**Main result.** The analysis yields explicit product formulas and connections to discrete Selberg ensembles for specific Rényi indices ($\alpha=1/2, 1, 2$), showing that the degree distribution converges to Gaussian limits under appropriate rescaling at these points.

**Method.** The authors introduce a fugacity-resolved partition function for the critical transverse-field Ising chain and map the resulting all-minors sum to a checkerboard-weighted discrete Selberg ensemble on a doubled root lattice.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on the critical transverse-field Ising (TFI) quantum chain, which serves as a common finite-size building block for calculating stabilizer and computational-basis Shannon--Rényi entropies. The analysis is generalized to other paired free-fermion chains.

**Key observables.** Stabilizer Rényi entropy ($M_\alpha$), the full counting statistics of the Pauli expectation-value distribution, and the degree distribution symmetry.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The Rényi index ($\alpha$), system size ($L$), and fugacity ($\mu$ or $	ext{unit } \mu$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on mapping the problem to known mathematical structures like discrete Selberg ensembles, which allows for exact evaluations at specific integer/half-integer indices.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in detail, but the notes mention Figure 1 illustrating entropy reductions via decimation, factorization, and escort relations, and Table I summarizing these reduction relations.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the physical context (TFI chain entropy), developing the mathematical machinery (fugacity-resolved partition function mapping to Selberg ensembles), deriving exact results for specific $\alpha$ values using determinant/Pfaffian compressions, and finally discussing the general behavior and open algebraic questions.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Stabilizer Rényi entropy quantifies the nonstabilizerness of a quantum state through Rényi moments of its Pauli expectation-value distribution. Its standard form sums over Pauli-string degrees and retains only the total Rényi weight. We introduce a fugacity-resolved partition function for the critical transverse-field Ising chain that resolves this degree in the balanced Majorana representation and generates its full counting statistics. This Ising problem extends beyond a single model: exact decimation identities and the correspondence with the half-filled \(XX\) chain establish it as a common finite-size building block for stabilizer and computational-basis Shannon--Rényi entropies. We map the fugacity-resolved all-minors sum, for every positive Rényi index, to a checkerboard-weighted discrete Selberg ensemble on a half-filled doubled root lattice. For positive integer indices, it reduces to a finite aliased Dyson constant term. At \(α=\tfrac12,1,2\), determinant and Pfaffian compressions yield product formulas. At \(α=4\), the generic-fugacity problem admits an exact inverse Jack--Kostka representation, while at unit fugacity a complementary middle-minor identity relates it to the square of the \(α=2\) result. These generating functions determine the balanced-degree statistics. Complement symmetry makes the distribution symmetric about \(k=L/2\), with an index-dependent width. It is exactly binomial at \(α=1\), has variance proportional to \(L\) at \(α=\tfrac12\), and develops an \(L\log L\) enhancement at \(α=2\). After variance rescaling, the centered distributions converge to Gaussian limits at all three indices. Beyond these solvable cases, finite-size numerics reveal an evolution from a central peak to a symmetric bimodal profile and eventually to endpoint dominance as the Rényi index increases.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors propose using van Hove singularities (vHSs) observed in the rf spectra of the 3D attractive Fermi Hubbard model as a highly robust method to determine the pairing gap ($\Delta$) and chemical potential ($\mu$). By showing that vHS positions depend only on these two parameters, measuring just two distinct features allows for parameter extraction without needing full spectral fitting. This establishes vHS analysis as a practical diagnostic tool for quantum simulation experiments.

**Why it may be interesting.** This provides a concrete, measurable spectroscopic diagnostic tool for characterizing superfluidity in quantum simulators, offering an alternative to complex full spectral fitting techniques.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to establish a robust spectroscopic method for extracting the pairing gap ($\Delta$) from radio-frequency (rf) spectra of the Fermi Hubbard model.

**Main result.** Van Hove singularities (vHSs) in the rf spectra depend only on $\Delta$ and chemical potential $\mu$, allowing both parameters to be determined by measuring just two well-resolved vHS positions.

**Method.** The analysis uses linear response theory applied to the low-order rf current, identifying characteristic detunings ($
u_i$) at vHS momenta derived from the Green's function formalism.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on the 3D attractive Fermi Hubbard model, relevant for ultracold Fermi gases in optical lattices. The theory extends beyond mean-field BCS by using pair-fluctuation theory within the T-matrix approximation.

**Key observables.** Van Hove singularities (vHSs) in the rf spectral function $A(\mathbf{k}, \omega)$, characterized by specific detuning values ($
u_i$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Pairing gap ($\Delta$), chemical potential ($\mu$), interaction strength ($|U|$), and lattice hopping ($t$). Special attention is paid to the half-filling case where $\mu$ is fixed.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that vHS features remain visible even when incorporating phenomenological lifetime and scattering broadenings, allowing for algebraic extraction of parameters from kinematic relations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate representative spectral functions showing particle/hole branches (Fig 1), the momentum-integrated rf current with labeled vHS features (Fig 2), and how these detunings ($
u_i$) vary systematically with interaction strength and filling (Fig 8).

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theoretical framework by deriving the dependence of vHS positions on $\Delta$ and $\mu$, demonstrating that two such measurements suffice for parameter extraction. It then analyzes the robustness of this method across different momentum resolutions and coupling strengths.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We show that van Hove singularities in rf spectra of the 3D attractive Fermi Hubbard model provide a robust route to extracting the pairing gap. Four types of singularities are classified, and their spectral positions are shown to depend solely on the pairing gap $Δ$ and chemical potential $μ$ through simple algebraic relations. Measuring two well-resolved singularities therefore determines both parameters without requiring full spectral fitting. Numerical simulations incorporating phenomenological lifetime and scattering broadenings confirm that these features remain visible in both momentum-integrated and $k_z$-integrated spectra, and become more pronounced at stronger coupling where conventional back-bending methods lose sensitivity. At half filling, particle-hole symmetry fixes $μ$, reducing the extraction to a single singularity measurement. These results establish vHS analysis as a practical spectroscopic diagnostic for pairing in quantum-simulated 3D Fermi Hubbard systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper analyzes sound wave excitations in 2D dipolar supersolids using Bogoliubov-de Gennes theory. It decomposes density fluctuations into contributions from crystal strain and particle transport, identifying three distinct acoustic modes. The results confirm that the microscopic quantum description correctly reproduces macroscopic hydrodynamic predictions based on elastic constants.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a detailed microscopic link between the quantum excitation spectrum (BdG) and macroscopic continuum properties (elasticity/hydrodynamics) in exotic quantum matter like supersolids, which is highly relevant to modern AMO research.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the microscopic structure of long-wavelength acoustic excitations in two-dimensional dipolar supersolids.

**Main result.** The analysis successfully connects Bogoliubov excitation modes to measurable crystalline and superfluid degrees of freedom, showing that fluctuation amplitudes match hydrodynamic theory based on elastic coefficients.

**Method.** Bogoliubov–de Gennes (BdG) calculations are used to determine quasiparticle excitations, followed by developing a procedure to extract the lattice displacement field from density perturbations.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled as a two-dimensional dipolar Bose-Einstein condensate exhibiting crystalline order (triangular or honeycomb). The dynamics are analyzed using linearizations of the extended Gross–Pitaevskii equation.

**Key observables.** Density ($\varrho$), phase ($\vartheta$), current ($J$), and lattice displacement fluctuations associated with three acoustic modes (one transverse, two longitudinal).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Long-wavelength limit ($|q|a \ll 1$); zero temperature; elastic coefficients of the supersolid.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes a uniform planar geometry and focuses on small distortions relative to the lattice constant.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate schematics of the three acoustic modes, phase diagrams showing triangular/honeycomb states, and spectra detailing density, strain, and defect density fluctuations for different excitation bands.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the physical system and governing equations (eGPE/BdG) to analyzing specific fluctuation components ($\delta	ilde{ho}, \delta	ilde{ho}_u, \delta	ilde{ho}_\Delta$) for each acoustic mode, culminating in comparisons with established hydrodynamic sum rules.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the microscopic structure of the long-wavelength acoustic excitations of two-dimensional dipolar supersolids with triangular and honeycomb crystal order. Using Bogoliubov--de Gennes calculations, we determine the density, phase, current, and lattice-displacement fluctuations associated with the three acoustic modes. We develop a procedure to extract the lattice displacement directly from the microscopic density perturbation, allowing the modes to be identified as one transverse and two longitudinal branches. The displacement field further separates the density fluctuations into contributions from crystal strain and particle transport relative to the lattice. This reveals that the lower longitudinal mode can have large strain and defect-density fluctuations that substantially cancel in the total density response. We show that the long-wavelength fluctuation amplitudes agree with hydrodynamic theory, which is specified entirely by the elastic coefficients of the supersolid. Our analysis provides a microscopic connection between Bogoliubov excitations and the crystalline and superfluid degrees of freedom of two-dimensional supersolids, and identifies their signatures in density- and current-sensitive probes.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a method for building scalable superconducting quantum processors using flip-chip integration via electroplated indium bumps. By characterizing the qubit coherence and systematically analyzing loss sources across different fabrication steps, the authors demonstrate compatibility with high-coherence circuits while identifying surface losses at the gold interface as the main limitation.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work directly addresses scalability challenges in superconducting quantum computing by providing a robust, high-coherence integration technique compatible with advanced 3D packaging methods.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing a scalable, high-coherence platform for superconducting quantum processors by integrating distinct material platforms using flip-chip bonding.

**Main result.** The electroplated indium bump bond technology is compatible with high-coherence circuits, achieving qubit quality factors around $10^6$. The primary loss mechanism identified is surface loss associated with the gold layer used for electrical contact.

**Method.** Fabrication and characterization of transmon qubits using a three-dimensional flip-chip architecture bonded via electroplated indium bumps. Loss mechanisms were systematically studied by comparing qubit lifetimes to resonator quality factors under controlled process variations.

**Model / system.** The system is a 3D transmon circuit utilizing two substrates connected by electroplated indium bumps. The design aims to share the electric field across both substrates, minimizing sensitivity to interface losses.

**Key observables.** Qubit quality factor ($Q_i$), $T_1$ relaxation time, and resonator quality factors measured under varying magnetic fields and process steps.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Achieved $Q_i \approx 10^6$; dominant loss tangent ($	an \delta_{	ext{MA}}$) associated with the Au-covered metal-air interface.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that surface losses at the gold/metal-air interface are the primary limiting factor, and that field sharing across substrates is effective for coherence preservation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the flip-transmon cross-section, measurement distributions of internal quality factors over time, and plots comparing $Q_i$ vs. simulated energy participation ratios ($	ext{PAu,MA}$) to confirm loss sources.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the 3D transmon architecture using electroplated indium. It then systematically measures qubit coherence ($T_1$) and compares these results with resonator measurements across controlled process variations (e.g., presence/absence of Au film) to pinpoint the dominant loss mechanism, concluding that surface losses govern performance.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Flip-chip integration offers a promising route toward scalable superconducting quantum processors and hybrid semiconductor-superconductor quantum devices. We develop a three-dimensional transmon architecture using electroplated indium in which the qubit electric field is shared nearly equally between two bump-bonded substrates while maintaining low participation at the indium-bump interface. The resulting geometry is well suited for future hybrid qubits, enabling the integration of distinct material platforms while minimizing sensitivity to bump-interface loss. Using this platform, we evaluate electroplated indium interconnects for superconducting quantum circuits. Flip-chip transmons incorporating electroplated indium bumps exhibit qubit quality factors around $10^6$. In addition, a systematic study of coplanar-waveguide resonators is used to identify losses associated with the electroplating process. In particular, we find that surface losses associated with the gold-layer, used to enable good electric contact with the indium, is likely the primary contributor to the qubit decay rate. These results demonstrate the compatibility of electroplated indium technology with high-coherence superconducting circuits and establish a promising platform for three-dimensional hybrid quantum integration.

</details>

### [From triangles to prisms: towards a geometric extension of concurrence fill for three-qubit mixed states](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07145v1)

**Authors:** Divyanshu K. Verma, Girish Kulkarni  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.07145v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** 🔥 `entanglement & information structure` **4/5**

**Summary.** This paper develops a geometric framework—the concurrence prism—to extend the measure of genuine tripartite entanglement (concurrence fill) from pure to mixed three-qubit states. By applying this formalism to mixtures of GHZ and W states, the authors derive an exact closed-form expression for the entanglement measure, providing both theoretical results and powerful visualization tools.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides advanced tools for quantifying multipartite entanglement in realistic, mixed quantum systems, which is crucial for understanding decoherence effects in quantum computation protocols.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is extending entanglement measures, specifically the concurrence fill, from three-qubit pure states to general mixed states.

**Main result.** The authors derived a closed-form expression for the concurrence fill of rank-2 mixtures of GHZ and W states, showing it depends on the mixing parameter $\lambda$ as $F(\lambda) = (5\lambda^2 - 4\lambda + 8)/9$.

**Method.** The study employs the convex-roof extension formalism, interpreting the optimization problem geometrically by minimizing the total 'prism fill area' over all pure state decompositions.

**Model / system.** The system under investigation is three-qubit quantum states ($ho$), with a specific focus on rank-2 mixed states formed as mixtures of GHZ and W states.

**Key observables.** Concurrence fill ($F_{ABC}$), Concurrence Triangle area, Total Prism Fill Area, Reduced density matrices for subsystems.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The mixing parameter $\lambda \in [0, 1]$ governing the mixture between GHZ and W states.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to rank-2 mixed states; the geometric interpretation relies on relating general decompositions to eigen-decompositions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the geometric connection between 'eigenprisms' (from eigendecomposition) and general prisms, showing the concurrence fill vs. $\lambda$ for the GHZ-W mixture, and visualizing the resulting prism structures.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the theoretical framework by generalizing the concurrence triangle to a 'concurrence prism' for mixed states. It then applies this formalism to rank-2 mixtures of specific entangled states (GHZ and W) to derive closed-form expressions and provide geometric visualizations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The quantification of multipartite entanglement remains a long-standing open challenge in quantum information theory with major implications for quantum foundations and quantum technologies. In the context of three-qubit pure states, the concurrence triangle underlies geometric interpretations for a variety of multipartite entanglement measures. In particular, the concurrence fill, which is proportional to the square root of the area of the concurrence triangle, has been shown to be a valid measure of genuine tripartite entanglement. As a result, there is now significant interest in extending the concurrence triangle and concurrence fill to three-qubit mixed states. In this work, we show that any rank-2 three-qubit mixed state can be visualized in terms of concurrence prisms associated with their pure state decompositions. Thus, the concurrence fill of the mixed state obtained through convex-roof extension is proportional to the total prism fill area, defined as the square root of the base area times the height, associated with the prisms minimized over all pure state decompositions. We then consider three-qubit mixed states that are incoherent mixtures of Greenberger-Horne-Zeilinger (GHZ) and W states and analytically perform their convex-roof extension to obtain a closed-form expression for the concurrence fill of these rank-2 mixtures. Finally, we apply the geometric picture of concurrence prisms to these mixtures and their pure state decomposition corresponding to minimum concurrence fill as a tool for visualization.

</details>

### [Consequences of a dynamical no-signaling condition for classical-quantum interactions](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07245v1)

**Authors:** S. Camalet

**Summary.** This paper rigorously extends the no-signaling principle to describe dynamical interactions between classical and quantum subsystems. It shows that maintaining this causality constraint forces the underlying evolution dynamics to be mathematically equivalent to convex-linear transformations. This has profound implications, restricting genuine non-trivial classical-quantum coupling in time evolution.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides fundamental constraints on how classical information can interact with quantum mechanics in a time-evolution setting, which is crucial for understanding open quantum systems modeling where classical parameters might govern environmental coupling or measurement outcomes.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a dynamical generalization of the no-signaling condition specifically for hybrid systems involving classical and quantum degrees of freedom.

**Main result.** The dynamical no-signaling condition imposes strong constraints on classical-quantum interactions, implying that such interactions must be described by convex-linear transformations equivalent to non-interacting dynamics in certain limits.

**Method.** The analysis involves formulating probability measures for hybrid states and testing the independence of outcome probabilities from ancillary measurements under time evolution operators T. Mathematical tools include information theory (mutual information) and analyzing linear/convex-linear maps.

**Model / system.** The system studied is a general hybrid model composed of classical degrees of freedom (defined by a sample space X) coupled to quantum degrees of freedom (described by a Hilbert space H). The dynamics are governed by finite-time evolution operators T acting on the joint probability measure.

**Key observables.** Outcome probabilities of measurements performed after ancillary pre-measurements; classical-quantum mutual information I(omega); and entanglement measures.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The nature of the hybrid state (pure vs. mixed), the type of evolution T (linear vs. non-linear), and whether all possible hybrid states are allowed.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis builds upon prior work defining probability measures for hybrid systems and assumes that deviations from no-signaling require non-linear transformations T.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in the notes, but the structure suggests mathematical derivations proving inequalities related to information loss under evolution.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the general framework of hybrid systems and the initial no-signaling condition. It then generalizes this concept dynamically for finite-time evolutions, deriving consequences regarding the directionality (classical influencing quantum but not vice versa) and ultimately proving equivalence to convex-linearity when all states are considered.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Hybrid classical-quantum approaches are instrumental in numerous fields, from condensed matter physics to quantum information science. We recently proposed to describe hybrid systems starting from a set of natural axioms for measurement probabilities without adding any underlying mathematical structure. The so defined probability measures fulfill a no-signaling condition that ensures that instantaneous communication is impossible. We formulate here a dynamical generalization of this condition. It means that, for two independent systems, the outcome probabilities of a measurement made on one of them are not affected by a measurement performed earlier on the other. Analogous requirements are satisfied for usual classical and quantum bipartite systems and violating them would make faster-than-light signaling possible. The dynamical no-signaling condition has important consequences for classical-quantum interactions that depend on the hybrid approach used. For no-signaling hybrid dynamics with classical trajectories, the classical degrees of freedom can influence the quantum ones but the latter cannot react on the former. If pure states of quantum systems remain pure then the dynamical no-signaling condition implies the absence of classical reaction. When all hybrid states are allowed, there are no genuine classical-quantum interactions for no-signaling hybrid dynamics that do not generate correlations between the classical and the quantum degrees of freedom. In all these cases, the proposed condition is equivalent to the convex-linearity of the probability measure transformations describing finite-time evolutions.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a rigorous theoretical framework to calculate spontaneous radiation rates induced by collapse models, incorporating detailed atomic structure effects through Radial Distribution Functions (RDFs). By applying this formalism to atoms like Germanium and Xenon, the authors demonstrate that different collapse mechanisms predict distinct, measurable spectral signatures at low energies. This provides a powerful tool for designing next-generation experiments to test fundamental physics beyond standard quantum mechanics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges fundamental quantum gravity concepts (collapse models) with highly precise atomic structure calculations. The explicit incorporation of material-dependent effects via RDFs provides a concrete pathway for experimental testing in AMO physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how atomic structure modifies the spontaneous radiation rate predicted by Spontaneous Collapse Models (SCMs). The goal is to use this modification to create material-dependent constraints on SCM parameters.

**Main result.** By incorporating realistic radial distribution functions (RDFs), the derived emission rate shows distinct model dependencies for CSL and DP models, particularly exhibiting a vanishing rate limit at low energies ($E 	o 0$) for neutral atoms.

**Method.** The authors derive a general expression for the spontaneous emission rate by calculating correlation functions of the radiation field, which are then expressed in terms of nuclear-electron and electron-electron RDFs.

**Model / system.** The study analyzes charged particles (e.g., Ge, Xe) whose dynamics are modified by SCMs, governed by a master Lindblad equation incorporating stochastic noise fields. The calculation relies on semi-classical approximations for the radiation field.

**Key observables.** Spontaneous emission rate ($	ext{d}\Gamma/	ext{d}\omega$ or $	ext{d}\Gamma/	ext{d}E$), and the modulation function $f(r_{ij})$ which distinguishes between CSL and DP models.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Collapse strength ($\lambda$) and spatial resolution ($\sigma$) of the SCMs; low-energy regime where photon wavelength $\sim$ atomic dimensions (1–100 keV).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis uses a semi-classical approach valid above 1 keV, assumes spherical symmetry for angular integration, and initially approximates interatomic correlations as subleading corrections.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare calculated RDFs ($P(r)$) for Si/Ar against literature; Figure 5 compares the predicted emission rates for Ge and Xe across different energy regimes for CSL vs. DP models, contrasting with simplified theoretical limits like $1/E$.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops a general formalism for the emission rate using arbitrary noise correlations, derives the expression in terms of RDFs, applies this framework to specific atoms (Ge, Xe) under CSL and DP assumptions, and concludes by comparing results across energy regimes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Collapse models introduce stochastic and nonlinear modifications in the quantum dynamics, predicting observable effects, such as spontaneous radiation from charged particles, which can be used to constrain their parameters. Recently, attention has focused on the 1-100 keV energy range, where the wavelength of the emitted photons becomes comparable to atomic dimensions, making the emission sensitive to atomic structure and leading to model-dependent behaviors that enable their discrimination. Here, we derive a general expression for the spontaneous emission rate for arbitrary noise, providing a framework that systematically incorporates the atomic structure through the radial distribution of the emitters, modulated by the specific collapse model. The formalism recovers previous results in the appropriate limits and naturally includes new low-energy effects, such as cancellation mechanisms arising from charge correlations. We evaluate the rates for germanium and xenon within the Diósi-Penrose and Continuous Spontaneous Localization models, showing how these correlations modify the predicted emission rates. This approach provides a unified framework to account for atomic effects and enables more robust, material-dependent experimental constraints on collapse model parameters.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper demonstrates that correlated noise can be actively utilized as a resource to overcome the thermal noise limitations plaguing quantum transduction between microwave and optical frequencies. By exploiting these correlations in both direct and entanglement-assisted protocols, the authors show significant improvements in channel fidelity and positive quantum capacity. This theoretical guidance suggests a pathway to realizing practical hybrid quantum networks with less stringent cryogenic requirements.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses fundamental bottlenecks in building practical quantum networks by transforming perceived noise sources into usable resources, which is highly relevant to open quantum systems theory and quantum optics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Quantum transduction, essential for hybrid quantum architectures, is fundamentally limited by thermal noise, which degrades state conversion fidelity.

**Main result.** The authors show that exploiting correlated noise as a resource significantly suppresses effective channel noise in direct transduction and enhances entanglement generation in entanglement-based transduction. This allows both protocols to achieve positive quantum capacity over relevant experimental ranges.

**Method.** The analysis uses the scattering picture framework applied to an electro-optomechanical system, modeling mode conversion via Gaussian processes and calculating quantum capacity bounds based on noise correlations.

**Model / system.** The study models a hybrid transduction platform involving optical ($\hat{a}$), mechanical ($\hat{b}$), and microwave ($\hat{c}$) modes, described by an effective Hamiltonian and analyzed using input-output relations incorporating correlated bath noise.

**Key observables.** Quantum Capacity ($Q_{N}^{LB}$), Logarithmic Negativity ($E_N$), Effective Channel Noise ($n_e$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Cooperativities ($C_{em}, C_{om}$), Transmissivity ($\eta$), Noise Correlation Parameter ($\chi$), Coupling Phase ($\phi$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis treats the EOM device as a linear Gaussian processor and assumes that noise correlations, rather than independent thermal noise, are the key resource.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the EOM system schematic and input-output relations with correlated bath noise. Figure 2 likely compares Logarithmic Negativity ($E_N$) versus coupling phase ($\phi$) under varying levels of noise correlation ($\chi$).

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the limitations of quantum transduction due to thermal noise, then introduces exploiting correlated noise as a resource for both direct and entanglement-based protocols. It develops the theoretical framework using scattering relations and finally presents results showing performance enhancement and proposes mechanisms for engineering these correlations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum transduction, which coherently converts quantum states between microwave and optical frequency domains, is a key technology for hybrid quantum architectures. Its performance, however, is fundamentally limited by thermal noise. Direct quantum transduction is particularly susceptible to noise and often fails to achieve positive quantum capacity. Entanglement-based quantum transduction, which realizes state conversion through quantum teleportation assisted by microwave-optical entanglement, is intrinsically more robust against thermal noise. However, generating sufficiently strong entanglement in a realistic thermal environment remains a major challenge. In this paper, we exploit correlated noise as a resource for quantum transduction. For direct quantum transduction, it is shown that the noise correlations give rise to controllable interference terms that substantially suppress the effective channel noise. For entanglement-based quantum transduction, the same correlations enhance the generation of microwave-optical entanglement, thereby improving the fidelity of teleportation-based conversion. As a result, both transduction protocols exhibit broad regions of positive quantum capacity over experimentally relevant ranges of cooperativity. We further discuss a possible physical mechanism for engineering the required noise correlations, providing theoretical guidance for experimental implementations. These results suggest that correlated noise can substantially relax the stringent cryogenic requirements for microwave-optical quantum transduction and facilitate the realization of practical hybrid quantum networks.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper explores using Bound States in the Continuum (BICs) within silicon nitride microresonators to control nonlinear photonics. By tuning one mode into a BIC state, researchers can profoundly alter spontaneous four-wave mixing and parametric amplification processes. This technique establishes BICs as a method for precise 'mode-selective lifetime engineering' of integrated optical devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it demonstrates a powerful tool—BICs—for engineering the decay rates and interaction strengths in open quantum optical systems, directly impacting nonlinear light-matter interactions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The work investigates utilizing Bound States in the Continuum (BICs) within integrated photonic structures to fundamentally reshape nonlinear optical dynamics, particularly through spontaneous four-wave mixing and parametric amplification.

**Main result.** Achieving BIC conditions allows for mode-selective lifetime engineering, enhancing parametric gain by 12 dB and lowering the oscillation threshold by 1.6 dB compared to standard resonances.

**Method.** The study combines theoretical modeling (extending field quantization to include discrete BIC modes) with experimental characterization of nonlinear processes like SFWM and OPA in silicon nitride microresonators.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a reconfigurable integrated photonic structure using silicon nitride microresonators, featuring an asymmetric Mach-Zehnder interferometer coupled to a bus waveguide. BICs are realized by tuning the coupling such that one mode becomes decoupled from the continuum modes.

**Key observables.** Signal-idler correlations in photon pairs, parametric gain enhancement (up to 12 dB), and reduction of oscillation threshold (by 1.6 dB).

**Important parameters / regimes.** High Quality Factor ($Q > 10^6$), low/high-gain regimes for nonlinear processes, and the ability to tune coupling coefficients via auxiliary resonators.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theoretical framework assumes orthogonality between BIC and asymptotic fields; experimentally, precise tuning of coupling is required to achieve near-zero radiative loss.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate mode solutions (propagating vs. confined BIC), the device structure showing how an auxiliary resonator creates BICs, and comparative plots demonstrating OPA gain in both BIC and standard resonance configurations.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the theoretical framework for incorporating discrete BIC modes into nonlinear Hamiltonians, to experimentally validating the selective creation of these BICs by tuning coupling, and finally applying this control to enhance parametric amplification processes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this work we theoretically and experimentally investigate bound states in the continuum (BICs) in a reconfigurable integrated photonic structure, focusing on spontaneous four-wave mixing. We consider a configuration in which only one mode, the idler, is tuned to the BIC condition, and show that suppressing the radiative loss of this single mode profoundly reshapes the overall nonlinear dynamics. In particularly, we realize electrically tunable Friedrich-Wintgen BICs with Q > 10^6 in an interferometrically coupled silicon-nitride microresonator. Although inaccessible through linear excitation, the BIC is populated by four-wave mixing and read out through its radiative photon partner. In the low-gain regime, we investigate the spectral, correlation, and coherence properties of the generated photon pairs, revealing distinctive signatures in the signal-idler correlations and an extracted signal whose coherence time approaches the intrinsic-loss-limited lifetime of the dark idler. In the high-gain regime, making one mode participating in optical parametric amplification a BIC enhances the parametric gain by 12 dB and lowers the oscillation threshold by 1.6 dB. These results establish BICs as a tool for mode-selective lifetime engineering, enabling control over both biphoton wavepackets and the threshold dynamics of integrated parametric devices.

</details>

### [Active Brownian motion in a single-relaxation viscoelastic fluid](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07059v1)

**Summary.** This work models Active Brownian Particles moving through a single-relaxation viscoelastic fluid, demonstrating that the resulting dynamics are dictated by the interplay between active self-propulsion and fluid memory effects. By analyzing the Mean Squared Displacement across different timescale orderings ($	au_R$, $	au_k$, $\lambda$), the authors provide a comprehensive theoretical framework validated by both simulation and experiment using an optical trap setup.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on soft matter dynamics rather than quantum phenomena, the study of non-equilibrium statistical mechanics in complex fluids (like viscoelastic media) shares conceptual links with open quantum systems where environmental coupling dictates system evolution. The concept of tunable 'effective environments' is relevant to designing quantum simulators.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates Active Brownian Motion (ABM) dynamics within viscoelastic (VE) media, focusing on how fluid memory effects alter particle movement compared to simple Newtonian solvents.

**Main result.** The Mean Squared Displacement (MSD) reveals distinct intermediate dynamic regimes—depending on the relative ordering of active persistence time ($	au_R$), VE equilibration time ($	au_k$), and relaxation time ($\lambda$)—validating the model against both simulation and experiment.

**Method.** The authors combine analytical derivations for the MSD with numerical simulations, validating the theoretical framework using an experimental setup involving a Janus colloid in a dynamic optical trap.

**Model / system.** The system models Active Brownian Particles (ABPs) moving in a single-relaxation viscoelastic fluid, mathematically described by combining contributions from active motion and harmonic/Maxwell-Voigt rheological elements. The experiment uses Pt-coated Janus colloids trapped optically to emulate the tunable VE environment.

**Key observables.** Mean Squared Displacement (MSD), which exhibits successive regimes including superdiffusive behavior, plateaus at characteristic timescales ($	au_k$ or $	au_R$), and long-time diffusive recovery.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dynamics are governed by the interplay of three competing timescales: ABP persistence time ($	au_R$), VE equilibration time ($	au_k$), and VE relaxation time ($\lambda$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The approach assumes linear viscoelasticity with a single relaxation time, and that the optical trap setup successfully emulates the required VE response without altering the medium due to activity.

**Figures summary.** Figure 2 shows simulated MSDs for persistence-dominated ($	au_R < 	au_k < \lambda$) and confinement-dominated ($	au_k < 	au_R < \lambda$) regimes. Figure 4 presents experimental MSD data matching these key theoretical predictions.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the rheological model, derives the governing Langevin equations for particle motion in the VE fluid, analyzes the resulting Mean Squared Displacement (MSD) across different timescale orderings ($	au_R$ vs $	au_k$ vs $\lambda$), and validates these theoretical predictions using both numerical simulations and physical experiments.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Active Brownian particles (ABPs) in viscoelastic (VE) media exhibit fascinating dynamical phenomena set by self-propulsion, thermal fluctuations, and fluid viscoelasticity. We extend our model, in which the Brownian dynamics within a slowly diffusing harmonic well emulates that in a single-relaxation VE fluid, to study active Brownian motion in such media. Consequently, the resultant dynamics is governed by the interplay of the characteristic timescales of the systems: the crossover and equilibration times of the VE fluid, $τ_k$ and $λ$, respectively, and the persistence time of the ABP, $τ_{\mathrm{R}}$. Following analytical predictions and simulations, we study two practically relevant regimes where the dynamics is dominated by the persistence of active motion and the elastic confinement of the VE fluid, with a phoretically active Pt-coated Janus colloid in a dynamic optical trap, and show quantitative agreement with the simulations. This approach provides a VE environment with tunable VE properties that remain unaffected by the strength of self-propulsion, allowing us to systematically investigate active Brownian motion in VE media in ways that are not otherwise possible with physical VE fluids.

</details>

### [Observation of metastable chiral domain walls in a topological magnet](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06569v1)

**Authors:** Richen Xiong, Chenxin Qin, Zhaoyu Han, Nisarg Chadha, Qiang Gao, William Holtzmann, Weijie Li, Jiaqi Cai, Yi Guo, Weihanzhang Guo, Qi Chen, Samuel L. Brantly, Sam Bonkowsky, Chen Huang, Kenji Watanabe, Takashi Taniguchi, Andrea F. Young, Xiaodong Xu, Eslam Khalaf, Chenhao Jin

**Summary.** This work experimentally identifies and characterizes metastable chiral domain walls in a QAH magnet using ultrafast spectroscopy. The authors demonstrate that these unique spin-valley textures are stabilized by the material's quantum geometry, allowing them to persist under extreme conditions unlike conventional magnetic excitations. This provides key insights into controlling topological protection in correlated electronic systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The mechanism of metastability arising from quantum geometry provides a concrete example of how fundamental band structure properties can stabilize exotic topological excitations relevant for spintronics and correlated electron systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Investigating exotic collective excitations, specifically metastable chiral domain walls, arising from the interplay between topology and correlation in topological magnets.

**Main result.** The authors observed a unique, long-lived spin-valley excitation that behaves as a chiral domain wall, whose metastability is governed by the quantum geometry of the parent bands. This mechanism distinguishes it sharply from conventional magnons or domain walls.

**Method.** Resonant ultrafast pump-probe spectroscopy and magnetic hysteresis measurements were used to probe the time and field dependence of spin-valley excitations in the material.

**Model / system.** The study uses a twisted MoTe2 moiré superlattice exhibiting Quantum Anomalous Hall (QAH) magnetism. The physics is governed by topological properties, spontaneous time-reversal symmetry breaking, and quantum geometric effects ($c_G$).

**Key observables.** Spin-valley excitation dynamics ($\Delta R/R$), magnetic hysteresis loops, temperature dependence of the slow component, and field dependence of domain wall stability.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Low temperatures ($T \sim 3.7	ext{ K}$), specific displacement fields ($D$), and large reverse magnetic fields ($	ext{Bz} = 200	ext{ mT}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The observed slow component is attributed to chiral domain walls stabilized by the interplay between real-space topology and momentum-space quantum geometry.

**Figures summary.** Figures show polarization-resolved contrast measurements confirming spontaneous symmetry breaking, hysteresis maps detailing stable regimes, and temperature/field dependence plots demonstrating the sharp onset and disappearance of the unique slow excitation component.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically presents evidence by first observing the novel metastable excitation via pump-probe spectroscopy, then characterizing its stability against magnetic fields and temperature, and finally linking its existence to theoretical predictions involving quantum geometry quantities ($c_G$).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The interplay between topology and correlation can give rise to exotic collective excitations. The integer and fractional quantum anomalous Hall (QAH) magnets recently discovered in two-dimensional (2D) flatband systems are predicted to host spin excitations distinct from those in conventional magnets. Experimentally, nevertheless, these new excitations remain largely unexplored. Here we investigate spin-valley excitations in a twisted MoTe2 moiré superlattice using resonant ultrafast pump-probe spectroscopy. We observe a metastable spin-valley excitation in the QAH magnet below T ~ 3.7 K that survives reverse magnetic field several times larger than the saturation field. The behavior of this excitation is sharply distinct from ordinary domain walls and magnons, indicating a new type of spin-valley textures unique to topological magnets. We propose that these textures are chiral domain walls with an in-plane winding of the pseudospin order parameter along the domain wall. Their metastability arises from the interplay between the topological winding in real space and the quantum geometry of the parent bands in momentum space through a universal mechanism. These chiral domain walls govern the nonequilibrium dynamics of QAH magnets and may play a central role in their stability. Our study highlights intrinsic quantum geometry effects on spin excitations in topological magnets; and provides key insights into the fundamental mechanism limiting stability of topological protection.

</details>

**Summary.** This study analyzes sea turtle foraging paths to model their anomalous movement. By analyzing velocity autocorrelation functions and mean-squared displacement, the authors demonstrate that the looping motion generates superdiffusion over intermediate timescales. They successfully construct a generalized Langevin equation that captures this loop-based, active search pattern.

**Why it may be interesting.** The methodology of extracting long-term memory effects from empirical time series (like animal movement) and mapping them onto a generalized stochastic differential equation is highly relevant to modeling non-equilibrium dynamics in complex physical systems, including active matter or biological transport processes.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To understand complex, non-Brownian movement patterns exhibited by foraging sea turtles by developing data-driven mathematical models that incorporate long-term memory.

**Main result.** The turtles' movement is characterized by large-scale loops generating superdiffusion over intermediate timescales, which can be modeled using a generalized Langevin equation incorporating oscillatory velocity autocorrelation functions.

**Method.** Analysis involves calculating Mean-Squared Displacement (MSD) and Velocity Autocorrelation Functions (VACFs), followed by constructing and fitting a stochastic generalized Langevin equation to the empirical data.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the satellite tracking data of loggerhead sea turtles foraging off West Africa. The model used is a stochastic generalized Langevin equation, which captures non-Markovian memory effects in the movement dynamics.

**Key observables.** Large-scale loops, superdiffusive MSD ($\alpha \approx 1.68$), persistent regular oscillations in VACFs (especially longitudinal), and confinement within a specific foraging region.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The characteristic time scale ($T_{x,1/2}$) derived from the oscillatory decay of the VACF, which is used to predict the superdiffusive length scale.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The movement dynamics are assumed to be governed by a generalized Langevin equation; simplifying assumptions were made in modeling (e.g., neglecting certain velocity distribution tails).

**Figures summary.** Figures show the bounded looping path compared to passive tracers, MSD curves exhibiting superdiffusion followed by localization, and VACFs displaying clear, persistent oscillations in the longitudinal direction.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying anomalous movement features (loops, superdiffusion) using diagnostics like MSD/VACF, to constructing a data-driven stochastic model (generalized Langevin equation), and finally validating this model against multiple species' trajectories by performing scale matching between oscillatory VACFs and the resulting superdiffusive MSD.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Animals inhabiting diverse environments by moving across different spatial scales, from insects to birds, marine predators, mammals and even humans, often display apparently random movement paths. Over the past decade, novel biologging technologies have recorded these patterns in increasing detail, generating a wealth of experimental data. A central challenge is to understand such complex patterns by constructing data-driven mathematical models. Many animal movements depart from Brownian motion, as described by correlated random walks, Lévy walks, or active particle dynamics. Yet, these movement models do not incorporate long-term non-Markovian memory extracted from experimental trajectories. Here, we construct a stochastic generalised Langevin equation from satellite tracking data for loggerhead sea turtles (Caretta caretta) foraging off the coast of West Africa. We find that these turtles exhibit active movement characterised by large-scale loops that are not explained by ocean currents or chirality. These loops maintain movement within a specific foraging region and, over intermediate timescales, generate superdiffusion similar to Lévy walks. We thus identify a loop-based form of active anomalous search related to foraging patterns observed across a wide range of animal species, which may inspire robotic search strategies and AI-based metaheuristic optimisation algorithms.

</details>
