**Summary.** This paper introduces a correlated-jamming protocol to enhance physical-layer security in communication channels. By utilizing entangled two-mode squeezed vacuum sources, the legitimate receiver can actively suppress self-interference noise below the standard quantum limit. This method preserves positive secrecy even against powerful eavesdroppers and is applicable to various real-world systems like optical or microwave links.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses practical limitations in quantum key distribution security by engineering active countermeasures (jamming) that leverage entanglement to surpass classical noise limits, which is highly relevant for physical implementations using superconducting circuits or optical links.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The goal is to improve physical-layer security in quantum communication by introducing correlated jamming noise that degrades an eavesdropper's channel while minimizing self-interference at the legitimate receiver.

**Main result.** Using entangled two-mode squeezed vacuum (TMSV) sources allows for sub-vacuum residual noise suppression, providing a measurable quantum advantage over classical jamming methods. This protocol maintains positive secrecy even when the eavesdropper has a stronger direct channel than the legitimate user.

**Method.** The analysis uses an optimized joint measurement technique on the received field and a retained local reference mode to cancel correlated fluctuations induced by the jamming source.

**Model / system.** The system models secure communication over lossy channels using a correlated-jamming protocol. It involves a bright coherent signal, a correlated two-mode source (classical or TMSV), and subsequent interference measurements at Bob's receiver.

**Key observables.** Self-interference noise variance ($\sigma_{B,J}$), achievable secrecy rate ($C$), and the quantum advantage quantified by residual noise below the vacuum floor.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Transmissivities of the channels ($\eta_{ch}, \eta_I$), signal power ($N_A$), and the squeezing parameter (for TMSV).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is explicitly treated as a collective-attack bound, not a coherent-attack bound. It assumes specific channel loss models and idler storage efficiencies.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates the setup: Alice sends signal $a_A$; Bob generates correlated source ($a_S, a_I$); $a_S$ is broadcast as jamming field $a_J$, and $a_I$ is retained for interference cancellation.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the protocol by first defining the physical setup (correlated jamming), then deriving the noise suppression mechanisms using quantum optics, followed by quantitative comparisons of secrecy rates across different source types (classical vs. entangled) and attack models.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Artificial-noise jamming improves physical-layer security by degrading an eavesdropper's channel, but the injected noise also interferes with the legitimate receiver. We introduce a correlated-jamming protocol based on classical and quantum correlations that suppresses this self-interference while preserving the jamming penalty experienced by the eavesdropper. Bob broadcasts one mode of a correlated two-mode source and retains the second as a local reference. Eve, who has no access to the retained mode, receives the full thermal jamming field, whereas Bob uses an optimized joint measurement to cancel its correlated fluctuations. The residual self-jamming noise is governed by the conditional variance of the transmitted jamming quadrature given the retained reference. Any classically correlated source is bounded by the vacuum-noise floor and therefore restores Bob, at best, to his unjammed receiver noise. An entangled two-mode squeezed source surpasses this limit and produces sub-vacuum residual noise, with the maximum quantum advantage set by the transmissivity of the jamming path back to Bob. In the bright-source regime, this advantage becomes a fixed reduction in Bob's noise floor, independent of the signal and jamming powers. Within a bounded-collection wiretap model, correlated jamming preserves positive secrecy even when Eve has a stronger direct channel than Bob and remains effective against collective measurements on Eve's Gaussian output states. The protocol supports bright classical message transmission and requires no end-to-end quantum channel. Possible applications include shot-noise-limited optical communication, cryogenic microwave networks, and covert or power-constrained secure links.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically analyzes how quantum matter systems lose coherence due to interaction with quantized gravitational waves (gravitons). By retaining the full quadratic tidal coupling, the authors derive master equations showing that different bath states yield distinct decoherence patterns. Notably, squeezed graviton baths can induce a protected subspace of coherent states, offering insights into fundamental limits for quantum measurements in gravitational environments.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous quantum treatment of gravitational decoherence, extending standard optomechanical models by retaining the full quadratic coupling. The identification of protected sectors via dressed states is highly relevant to understanding fundamental limits on quantum sensing.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how a nonrelativistic quantum matter system decoheres when coupled to quantized gravitational waves (gravitons). The focus is on retaining the full quadratic nature of the tidal interaction, which differs from simpler linear couplings.

**Main result.** The analysis shows that while number/thermal baths enhance decoherence by removing coherence protection, a squeezed graviton bath leads to a novel, coherence-protected sector spanned by dressed dark states. Furthermore, the structure of the matter Hilbert space decomposes into even- and odd-parity sectors regardless of the bath state.

**Method.** The authors use open quantum system techniques, deriving Lindblad master equations via Born-Markov and secular approximations. They employ renormalization procedures for divergent terms and analyze coherence decay using ladder operators and dark state analysis.

**Model / system.** The model consists of a nonrelativistic quantum harmonic oscillator (matter) coupled to a bath of quantized gravitational waves (gravitons). The interaction is fundamentally quadratic in the mechanical displacement, derived from the tidal coupling term $H_I \propto x^2 h_{xx}$.

**Key observables.** Decoherence rates ($\gamma$), coherence protection between lowest number states, and the structure/evolution of parity-separated sectors.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Bath state type (vacuum, coherent, number, thermal, squeezed), coupling strength, and mechanical frequency $\omega_m$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation relies on weak-field expansion, the Born approximation, and the Markovian assumption for deriving the Lindblad master equation.

**Figures summary.** Not specified by notes; however, the analysis involves solving coupled differential equations governing coherence decay in different bath regimes (e.g., $R_{01}(t)$ vs. time).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from deriving the general quadratic coupling and master equation structure to analyzing specific bath states (vacuum, coherent, number/thermal) and culminating in a detailed analysis of coherence protection using squeezed baths and dark state theory.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We derive and investigate the reduced dynamics of a nonrelativistic quantum matter system interacting with quantized gravitational waves. Because gravitational radiation couples tidally to the mass quadrupole moment, we emphasize that the leading interaction is quadratic in the mechanical displacement. Rather than treating it as a complication to be removed by linearization, we retain this full quadratic dependence, which gives rise to two-phonon rather single-phonon processes typical in dipolar interactions. We clarify its relation to the effective linear coupling that often appears in optomechanical literature, but which conceals the delicate quantum signatures of the full quadratic coupling. Using the Born-Markov and secular approximations, we microscopically derive Lindblad master equations for the matter dynamics induced by graviton baths in multi-mode vacuum, coherent, number, thermal, and squeezed states. We treat the ultraviolet-divergent Lamb-shift Hamiltonian by an explicit renormalization procedure, with the mechanical frequency identified as the physical frequency. Remarkably, owing to the nature of the interaction, the matter Hilbert space decomposes into even- and odd-parity sectors for all bath states considered. For a coherent graviton bath, the decoherence is identical to that of the vacuum, while the bath one-point function produces a coherent-shift Hamiltonian that exactly reproduces the tidal interaction with a classical gravitational wave. For a vacuum graviton bath, we recover the coherence protection between the two lowest mechanical number states, while number and thermal graviton baths remove this protection and enhance decoherence, although the effect remains small for realistic parameters. By contrast, a squeezed graviton bath enlarges the coupled coherence structure, and gives rise to a novel, coherence-protected sector spanned by dressed dark states.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a novel, device-independent protocol using entanglement-assisted QRACs to precisely measure both the sharpness and incompatibility of unsharp quantum measurements. By incorporating communication between decoding parties, the scheme significantly enhances the achievable bounds compared to noncommunicating methods. The work is validated experimentally using photon pairs in an interferometer setup.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work advances device-independent quantum characterization protocols, which are crucial for building trust in quantum devices where internal workings cannot be fully trusted. It provides concrete methods to quantify fundamental resource limitations like measurement incompatibility.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is developing a robust, device-independent protocol to simultaneously characterize both the sharpness ($\eta$) and the incompatibility ($D$) of unsharp quantum measurements.

**Main result.** The proposed communication-enhanced scheme allows for quantifying both parameters with greater precision than noncommunicating protocols, demonstrating that communication acts as a powerful operational resource in this certification process.

**Method.** The authors use an entanglement-assisted sequential Quantum Random Access Code (QRAC) protocol involving three parties and derive lower bounds on incompatibility using measured success probabilities ($P_{AB}, P_{AC}$).

**Model / system.** The system utilizes polarization-entangled photon pairs generated via type-II spontaneous parametric down-conversion in a PPKTP crystal within a Sagnac interferometer setup for experimental realization.

**Key observables.** Measurement sharpness ($\eta$), measurement incompatibility ($D$), and average decoding success probabilities ($P_{AB}, P_{AC}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The communication channel's allowance, which enhances the achievable success probabilities beyond classical bounds.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes statistical independence and uniform distribution of inputs; a limitation noted is that $D_C$ might not perfectly capture incompatibility as $\eta$ increases due to protocol structure.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the entanglement-assisted QRAC setup, showing how communication enhances success probabilities beyond classical limits, and quantifying the narrowing of sharpness intervals ($\Delta\eta$) and lower bounds on incompatibility ($D_B, D_C$).

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem using an entanglement-assisted sequential QRAC model. It derives mathematical relationships linking success probabilities to sharpness and incompatibility. The results demonstrate the advantage of communication, followed by experimental validation using tunable Mach-Zehnder interferometers.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Unsharp measurements are key resources for tasks that balance information gain and disturbance, but certifying them without device assumptions remains a challenge. We propose a fully device-independent protocol for characterizing unsharp instruments, based on an entanglement-assisted sequential quantum random access code, where the first decoder is allowed to communicate her measurement setting to the second. This communication-enhanced scheme creates a decoding regime in which both decoders surpass classical bounds, enabling tight quantification of sharpness and direct quantification of measurement incompatibility beyond noncommunicating protocols. Experimentally, we implement tunable unsharp measurements using a Mach-Zehnder interferometer, observing the predicted sequential enhancement in decoding probability. Additionally, we achieve significantly narrower sharpness intervals and incompatibility quantification across multiple target sharpness values. Our results show that communication is a powerful operational resource for certifying precisely unsharp instruments and advancing device-independent quantum information protocols.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper uses high-entropy spinel vanadates to study the competition between orbital ordering and disorder. Experimentally, they show that the random cation distribution suppresses long-range structural transitions associated with orbital order. Instead, the system settles into a complex, heterogeneous glassy magnetic state, highlighting the crucial role of quenched disorder in correlated oxides.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides experimental evidence of how quenched structural disorder can fundamentally alter the ground state by suppressing collective orbital ordering, leading to complex, frustrated magnetic dynamics relevant to correlated electron physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how high configurational disorder influences the interplay between orbital degrees of freedom, geometrical frustration, and magnetic ordering in spinel vanadates.

**Main result.** High entropy suppresses long-range orbital ordering, preventing structural symmetry lowering, while simultaneously stabilizing a heterogeneous glassy magnetic state characterized by complex relaxation dynamics.

**Method.** The research combines structural characterization using X-ray diffraction with detailed thermodynamic and magnetic measurements (AC/DC susceptibility, specific heat).

**Model / system.** The physical system is the high-entropy spinel vanadate $(	ext{Li}_{0.2}	ext{Mg}_{0.2}	ext{Mn}_{0.2}	ext{Co}_{0.2}	ext{Zn}_{0.2})	ext{V}_2	ext{O}_4$, where V ions form a geometrically frustrated pyrochlore sublattice.

**Key observables.** Structural symmetry (absence of peak splitting), magnetic susceptibility ($\chi'$ and $\chi''$), specific heat anomalies, and relaxation dynamics analyzed via Cole-Cole plots.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature regimes (down to 3 K), disorder concentration (high entropy), and characteristic freezing temperatures ($T_f \sim 17-22$ K).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the observed glassy dynamics are due to a spatially nonuniform spin-orbital state induced by random A-site cation distribution.

**Figures summary.** Figures show preserved cubic XRD structure down to low temperatures, ZFC/FC magnetic bifurcation below $\sim 17$ K, and AC susceptibility plots demonstrating frequency-dependent relaxation indicative of glassy dynamics.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by first establishing the structural stability (XRD) contrasting with orbital ordering expectations. It then measures bulk magnetism (M(T), C(T)) to rule out conventional long-range order, culminating in detailed AC susceptibility analysis using multiple models (Vogel–Fulcher, dynamic scaling) to characterize the glassy state.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

High-entropy oxides provide a unique platform for exploring the interplay between configurational disorder and correlated electronic states. We report on structural, thermodynamic, and magnetic properties of the high-entropy spinel vanadate (Li$_{0.2}$Mg$_{0.2}$Mn$_{0.2}$Co$_{0.2}$Zn$_{0.2}$)V$_2$O$_4$, in which orbital-active V ions occupy the pyrochlore sublattice. X-ray diffraction measurements reveal that the cubic spinel structure is preserved at temperatures down to 5 K without any detectable symmetry lowering. Unlike conventional spinel vanadates exhibiting a symmetry-lowering structural transition driven by orbital ordering, the compound that we study exhibits a suppression of long-range orbital ordering in the high-entropy state. Magnetic susceptibility measurements show glassy magnetic freezing at temperatures below $\sim$20 K, while specific-heat measurements reveal no anomaly associated with long-range ordering down to 3 K. Frequency-dependent ac susceptibility reveals pronounced glassy dynamics. Analyses based on the Mydosh parameter, dynamic scaling law, and Vogel--Fulcher law suggest an intermediate dynamical regime between canonical spin-glass and cluster-glass behavior. Furthermore, Cole--Cole analyses reveal systematic deviations from a single Debye relaxation process, indicating the presence of heterogeneous magnetic relaxation dynamics near the freezing regime. Our results demonstrate that, in a frustrated spinel vanadate, high configurational entropy suppresses long-range orbital ordering and stabilizes a heterogeneous glassy magnetic state, highlighting the combined roles of geometrical frustration, orbital degrees of freedom, and configurational disorder in high-entropy magnets.

</details>

**Summary.** The research reports on the use of superconducting elements to actively control spin-wave propagation within a YIG thin film. By tuning the local magnetic environment via superconductivity, the authors achieved temperature-tunable refraction and even negative refraction for spin waves. This opens pathways for developing novel microwave optical components for quantum or classical information processing.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work demonstrates using superconductivity to engineer wave properties (refraction/dispersion) in classical magnetic systems, which has parallels with controlling photonic or magnonic modes in quantum devices.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central challenge addressed is achieving low-damping, tunable control of spin-wave propagation, specifically realizing temperature-controlled refraction.

**Main result.** The study successfully demonstrated temperature-tunable spin-wave refraction and the observation of negative refraction using superconducting elements integrated into a thin-film magnetic insulator structure.

**Method.** Measurements involve exciting spin waves in YIG films and imaging their propagation characteristics (refraction, phase front shaping) using Nitrogen-Vacancy (NV) centers in diamond micromembranes as a sensitive detector.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a thin-film magnetic insulator (YIG) coupled with superconducting control elements (NbTiN). The theoretical analysis models the superconductor's diamagnetic screening effect, which modifies the spin-wave dispersion relation based on the temperature-dependent London penetration depth.

**Key observables.** Spin wave refractive index, refraction angle ($	heta_{	ext{YIG/SC}}$), and the superconducting London penetration depth ($\lambda_L$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature ($T$), magnetic field components ($\mathbf{B}_{	ext{ip}}$), excitation frequency ($f_{	ext{ESR}}$), and interface angles.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The small refraction observed above the superconducting critical temperature ($T_c$) is attributed to strain or damage-related anisotropy induced by deposition, rather than solely the superconducting effect.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate schematic setups (YIG/SC/NV), temperature dependence of refraction angles vs. setpoint temperature, and spatial maps showing phase front shaping and negative refraction regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically presents experimental measurements across various interface angles and temperatures, first demonstrating basic refraction, then quantifying the superconducting influence on dispersion ($\lambda_L$), and finally showcasing advanced control like negative refraction and beam translation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Spin waves are promising signal carriers for microwave control at the micrometer scale. However, realizing low-damping, tunable control of spin-wave propagation remains a central challenge. Here we use magnetic shielding by superconducting control elements to tune the local spin-wave dispersion and realize temperature-controlled refraction of spin waves in a thin-film magnetic insulator. Using magnetic imaging based on spins in diamond, we characterize the refractive index and demonstrate both positive and negative refraction as well as wavefront shaping by the superconductors. The observed refraction is explained by a geometrical analysis of the superconductivity-induced modification of the hyperbolic spin-wave dispersion. Our results demonstrate that superconductors enable tunable spin-wave optical elements, opening new opportunities for microwave control in classical or quantum information devices.

</details>

**Summary.** This work demonstrates that in layered electrides, the coupling between the anionic electron lattice (AEL) and the underlying metal cation framework dictates the electronic structure. By comparing YCl and LaCl, the authors show this coupling can fundamentally reshape band topology and connectivity, offering a powerful new knob for designing quantum materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept that an excess electron system can act as a tunable, reconfigurable degree of freedom to engineer band topology and geometry is highly relevant for designing novel quantum materials beyond conventional atomic constraints.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how controlling the lattice geometry, particularly through excess anionic electron layers (AELs), can actively dictate and reconstruct the electronic structure of crystalline solids.

**Main result.** The coupling between the AEL and the atomic cation framework fundamentally changes the effective lattice connectivity, transforming the electronic bands from a flat-band dice-lattice structure ($	ext{YCl}$) to a dispersive, reconstructed state ($	ext{LaCl}$).

**Method.** The research combines Angle-Resolved Photoemission Spectroscopy (ARPES) measurements with theoretical modeling using tight-binding Hamiltonians and DFT calculations.

**Model / system.** The system involves layered electrides ($	ext{RECl}$), comparing the 'standalone AEL' regime ($	ext{YCl}$) to the 'entwined lattice' regime ($	ext{LaCl}$). The electronic structure is governed by hopping channels between the excess electrons and the cation sites.

**Key observables.** Electronic band dispersion, Chern band topology ($|C|$), presence/absence of flat bands, and Van Hove singularities (vHS).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interlayer separation distance, degree of coupling between AEL and atomic lattice, and the specific cation element ($	ext{Y}$ vs. $	ext{La}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the electronic structure is highly sensitive to the hybridization strength between the anionic electron layer and the underlying atomic framework.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the standalone AEL in YCl versus the entwined lattice in LaCl, showing structural differences and band reconstructions. Another figure illustrates the general trend of crossover from standalone AEL to entwined lattices across the REX family.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical framework by contrasting $	ext{YCl}$ (standalone AEL) with $	ext{LaCl}$ (entwined lattice), using ARPES and tight-binding models to pinpoint the activation of direct hopping channels as the cause for the qualitative electronic divergence.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Controlling the lattice geometry that governs electronic structure is a central theme in condensed-matter physics, yet in crystalline solids this geometry is usually fixed by the atomic framework. Electrides offer an alternative route to electronic structure design in which their excess electrons can organize into anionic electron lattice (AEL) and provide a lattice-like degree of freedom. Recent work has highlighted the standalone limit, where the AEL in YCl yields bands well described by the dice-lattice model. Here, using angle-resolved photoemission spectroscopy (ARPES), we show that LaCl, although isostructural to YCl, realizes a qualitatively different regime where the AEL is entwined with the La cation framework, producing a fully reconstructed electronic structure. Combining the ARPES result with tight-binding model analysis, we demonstrate that this radical divergence stems from the activation of direct hopping channels between the AEL and the La atomic lattice. This coupling reshapes the effective lattice geometry, reconstructs the electronic states, and modifies the associated Chern band topology, transforming the bipartite dice-lattice network in YCl into a tripartite structure in LaCl. Our findings demonstrate that the coupling between the AEL and the atomic lattice can actively shape the effective lattice geometry that governs the electronic structure. This coupling can act as a powerful tuning knob for electronic structure design that is inaccessible in conventional materials.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper theoretically derives the existence and structure of long-lived nuclear spin states and coherences in aliphatic chains using Redfield relaxation theory. By identifying operators annihilated by the dominant dipole–dipole interaction, it provides a general framework to predict these protected quantum modes. This is crucial for applications ranging from hyperpolarization storage to quantum information processing.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous theoretical framework linking fundamental relaxation theory (Redfield) to observable quantum phenomena (LLCs/LLSs) in molecular spin systems, which is highly relevant for understanding coherence preservation in complex biological or chemical environments.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is determining the fundamental origin and general structure of delocalized long-lived states (LLSs) and collective zero-quantum long-lived coherences (LLCs) in aliphatic spin chains.

**Main result.** These protected modes are shown to arise directly from Redfield relaxation theory by finding operators annihilated by the dominant intra-pair dipole–dipole relaxation superoperator. The accessible number of such independent non-trivial operators depends on molecular chirality.

**Method.** The authors employ Redfield relaxation theory, constructing and analyzing the zero-eigenvalue subspace of the relaxation superoperator associated with the intra-pair dipole–dipole interaction.

**Model / system.** The system is an aliphatic spin chain composed of $N$ methylene groups ($-	ext{CH}_2-$). The analysis uses spin operators for pairs of spins and models the dynamics via the relaxation superoperator $\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{\text{intra}}$.

**Key observables.** Long-lived states (LLSs) and long-lived coherences (LLCs), which are characterized by their extended lifetimes exceeding conventional $T_1$ and $T_2$ times.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of methylene groups, $N$; the nature of the molecule (achiral vs. chiral); and the dominant relaxation mechanism (intra-pair dipole–dipole interaction).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Cross-correlations between different dipole–dipole interaction sites are neglected due to rapid averaging from fast molecular rotation.

**Figures summary.** Figures schematically illustrate the local LLO subspace, the construction of collective LLOs via product operators ($\prod \hat{P}(X)$), and the classification of these products into parity-defined subspaces ($P(MT)$).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the physical problem (LLSs/LLCs) to applying Redfield theory, deriving the mathematical condition for protection ($\hat{\mathcal{T}}^{(2)}_{2,m} \hat{L} = 0$), establishing the combinatorial structure of the zero-eigenvalue space ($2^N-1$ operators), and finally developing explicit construction methods using population projectors.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Delocalized long-lived states (LLSs) and collective zero-quantum long-lived coherences (LLCs) in aliphatic chains provide a promising route for preserving nuclear spin order with lifetimes that exceed the conventional $T_1$ and $T_2$ relaxation times, respectively. Their extended lifetimes make them attractive for applications including hyperpolarization storage, ligand-observed drug screening based on the loss of longevity upon binding to a target protein, and quantum information processing exploiting the collective properties of many-body spin systems. Although LLSs and LLCs have been observed experimentally in methylene networks, their origin and general structure in chains of arbitrary length have remained unclear. Here we show that these relaxation-protected modes follow directly from Redfield relaxation theory. Specifically, we construct the long-lived subspace, i.e., the zero-eigenvalue subspace of the relaxation superoperator associated with the dominant intra-pair dipole--dipole relaxation mechanism. The long-lived subspace contains $2^N-1$ independent non-trivial operators, which excludes the identity operator, where $N>1$ is the number of $-\mathrm{CH}_2-$ groups in the chain. In achiral molecules, conservation of the global intra-pair permutation parity restricts experimental access to at most $2^N-2$ of these operators, whereas in chiral molecules this parity is not conserved, making up to $2^N-1$ long-lived operators accessible. We further develop a general framework for constructing both LLSs and LLCs in aliphatic chains containing an arbitrary number of $-\mathrm{CH}_2-$ groups in achiral molecules, and illustrate the approach explicitly for chains with $N=2$, 3, and 4 methylene groups.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces an adaptive control protocol designed to optimize entanglement distribution rates over long-haul quantum fiber links plagued by polarization drift. By treating the problem as a joint optimization of rate versus fidelity, the authors developed a software controller that dynamically adjusts source power and compensation timing. This approach significantly boosts performance in near-term quantum networks without requiring new physical hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a practical, software-defined solution for mitigating environmental decoherence in quantum networks, which is highly relevant to open quantum systems and quantum optics research concerning physical implementation challenges.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The challenge is designing a control protocol for wide-area quantum links that maximizes entanglement distribution rate while ensuring the fidelity of transmitted quantum states remains above an application-specified threshold.

**Main result.** An adaptive, software-based controller was developed that improved the mean entanglement distribution rate by 14% over a 24-hour trace compared to optimized static policies.

**Method.** The authors formulated the problem as a joint control optimization over tunable source parameters (pump power) and polarization compensation timing, using trace-driven simulations based on real fiber data.

**Model / system.** The system models quantum communication over wide-area optical fiber links suffering from time-varying polarization drift. The control mechanism dynamically adjusts the entanglement source's pump power and applies polarization corrections to maintain high fidelity.

**Key observables.** Entanglement distribution rate, State Fidelity (F), Polarization State of Polarization (SOP).

**Important parameters / regimes.** 64 km deployed optical fiber length; minimum required fidelity threshold ($	ext{F}_{	ext{target}}$); pump laser power settings.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The control protocol assumes that the link degradation can be modeled or predicted sufficiently well to guide adaptive adjustments, and it relies on classical processing rather than new quantum hardware.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic of the quantum link setup involving entanglement generation and polarization compensation measurements, the metropolitan context of the 64km fiber link, and rate-fidelity trade-offs for different sources.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the physical limitations (polarization drift) to formulating the problem as a joint control optimization. It then details the adaptive protocol construction, followed by rigorous evaluation using long-term trace simulations against static benchmarks.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum network links must distribute entanglement at high rates while satisfying application-specified fidelity demands. However, wide-area deployed fiber links suffer from polarization drift which destabilizes end-to-end fidelity and forces periodic compensation. Current deployments often use active stabilization with fixed control policies, and improvements generally stem from advances in quantum hardware. Meanwhile, software control remains relatively underexplored.   Here, we formulate quantum link operation as a joint control problem over tunable rate-fidelity tradeoffs and uncontrollable link drift. From this framework, we construct a link control protocol that dynamically adapts source pump power and polarization compensation to maximize entanglement distribution rate subject to a minimum fidelity constraint. We evaluate the protocol through trace-driven simulations driven by data from a 64 km deployed optical fiber. Compared with optimized static policies, our adaptive controller improves mean entanglement distribution rate by 14% over a 24 hour trace, without requiring any offline policy optimization. Our results show that software-based physical layer control can provide a practical mechanism for improving near-term quantum link performance without requiring additional quantum hardware.

</details>

**Summary.** This work establishes $	ext{Eu}_3	ext{In}_2	ext{As}_4$ as a model system where strong exchange coupling allows systematic tuning of topological phases via external magnetic fields. By combining advanced magnetometry, spectroscopy, and transport measurements, the authors map out transitions from an antiferromagnetic insulator to ferromagnetic states hosting Weyl nodes. This demonstrates that magnetism can be used as a powerful knob to control electronic band topology in condensed matter systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The strong coupling mechanism provides a clear pathway for using magnetism (a many-body phenomenon) to engineer and control exotic quantum band structures like Weyl semimetals, which is highly relevant for designing next-generation spintronic or topological electronic devices.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research aims to control quantum states of matter by integrating intrinsic magnetism with band topology, overcoming limitations posed by weak exchange coupling in existing materials.

**Main result.** The material $	ext{Eu}_3	ext{In}_2	ext{As}_4$ exhibits a predictable sequence of magnetic phase transitions (AFM $ightarrow 2/3$-FiMa $ightarrow$ FM) driven by external fields, leading to topological phases like Weyl semimetals.

**Method.** The study combines theoretical calculations (DFT with SOC) predicting band structures for different magnetic states with multiple experimental probes including magneto-transport ($ho_{xx}$, AHE), magneto-infrared spectroscopy, and magnetization measurements.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the topological magnet $	ext{Eu}_3	ext{In}_2	ext{As}_4$. The model relies on identifying a 'giant exchange coupling' that causes large, magnetization-dependent band shifts (up to 300 meV) across various magnetic phases.

**Key observables.** Magnetization $M(H)$, anomalous Hall conductivity ($\sigma_{	ext{AHE}}$), magneto-infrared spectral features ($	ext{T}\alpha, 	ext{T}eta, 	ext{T}\gamma$), and Shubnikov-de Haas oscillation frequencies.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Exchange coupling strength ($J_{	ext{ex}}$) leading to $\sim 300	ext{ meV}$ band shifts; critical magnetic fields required for phase transitions (e.g., $1	ext{ T}$); temperature dependence near Néel temperatures ($\sim 4.5	ext{ K}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the observed electronic structure evolution and transport anomalies are directly governed by the strong, field-tunable exchange coupling mechanism.

**Figures summary.** Figures detail structural characterization (XRD), magnetic phase diagrams mapping transitions between AFM, $2/3$-FiMa, and FM states; magneto-IR spectra show evolving optical transitions correlated with magnetization; transport measurements map out angular dependence of AHE and $	ext{SdH}$ oscillations.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically maps the magneto-topological phase diagram by first characterizing the magnetic ground state (AFM) via susceptibility and $M(H)$ curves. It then uses spectroscopy and transport to link field-induced magnetic transitions ($	ext{AFMa} ightarrow 2/3	ext{-FiMa} ightarrow 	ext{FMa}$) to specific topological electronic states, culminating in the demonstration of Weyl node tuning.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The interplay between magnetism and band topology provides a route to controlling quantum states of matter, yet its realization in materials is often constrained by weak exchange coupling and complex electronic structures. Here, a giant exchange coupling is identified in the newly predicted topological magnet Eu3In2As4, giving rise to magnetization-dependent band shifts of up to 300 meV. Together with its intrinsically soft magnetic response, this strong cou-pling enables systematic tuning of topological phases by both the magnitude and orientation of applied magnetic fields. The magneto-topological phase diagram is mapped out in which an antiferromagnetic topological insulator ground state evolves, under modest fields, into a pro-posed intermediate 2/3-ferrimagnetic phase, and further into fully polarized ferromagnetic states predicted to host either Weyl or nodal-ring semimetals. Notably, the Weyl phase corresponds to a minimal model hosting a single pair of Weyl nodes. Quantum oscillations, anomalous Hall transport and magneto-infrared spectroscopy consistently reveal exchange-driven band recon-struction across these transitions. Rotation of the magnetization theoretically provides an effi-cient means to tune the momentum-space positions and separations of the Weyl nodes. These results establish Eu3In2As4 as a model system for exploring how strong exchange coupling can be used to control topological band structures with minimal complexity.

</details>

**Summary.** This work characterizes the temperature-dependent local magnetism in RMn2Ge2 intermetallics using muon spin spectroscopy. By analyzing magnetic transitions from conical to antiferromagnetic phases, the authors map out how rare-earth ion ordering and crystal symmetry dictate the material's exotic magnetic behavior. The combination of $\mu$SR measurements and DFT calculations provides a detailed picture of local spin dynamics in these correlated systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on magnetism, the use of $\mu$SR to probe local spin dynamics and symmetry breaking in correlated materials provides insights into complex many-body ground states relevant to strongly correlated electron physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the local magnetic properties of rare-earth intermetallic compounds RMn2Ge2, focusing on their temperature-dependent magnetic transitions and skyrmion hosting.

**Main result.** Muon spin spectroscopy reveals distinct magnetic phases (conical to collinear AFM) across the series, with disorder increasing from Ce to Nd. The local magnetism is strongly influenced by rare-earth ion ordering at low temperatures.

**Method.** The research combines experimental measurements using Muon Spin Rotation ($\mu$SR) with theoretical calculations employing Density Functional Theory (DFT) and Crystal Field Hamiltonian analysis.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the rare-earth intermetallic compounds RMn2Ge2 (R=Ce, Pr, Nd). The magnetism involves transitions between conical and collinear antiferromagnetic structures, which are probed by local magnetic field measurements.

**Key observables.** Muon asymmetry A(t), precession frequency $\nu(T)$, local magnetic fields $|B|$, and energy level splittings $\Delta \text{CEF}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature (T) regimes defining phase transitions ($T_c=318$ K, $T_N=417$ K), and the rare-earth element R.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the muon site is crystallographically unique for tracking changes across the series; DFT calculations use specific functionals (PBE).

**Figures summary.** Figures show rapid oscillatory $\mu$SR spectra below $T_c$, order-parameter-like behavior of frequency vs. temperature, and comparisons between calculated dipolar field distributions at candidate muon sites and measured local fields.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the magnetic transitions using $\mu$SR data across different R elements (Ce, Pr, Nd), correlating observed spectral changes with theoretical predictions derived from DFT calculations of local fields based on assumed magnetic structures.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present an investigation of the rare-earth intermetallic materials, $R$Mn$_2$Ge$_2$ ($R$=Ce, Pr, Nd), reported to host a lattice of skyrmionic bubbles at room temperature. The magnetism of all three materials is characterised by the onset of local fluctuations as the magnetic state changes from a conical magnetic structure to a collinear antiferromagnetic one as temperature $T$ is increased. In this $T$ regime, where skyrmion-bubble textures have been reported, we see dynamics similar to those observed in other skyrmion-hosting materials. The level of disorder above this transition increases across the series from $R$=Ce to Nd. At low temperatures the magnetism is affected by the ordering of the rare-earth ions, resulting in distinct behaviour for the different members of the series.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a rigorous method using Lefschetz thimbles to define the quantum state (Kodama wavefunctional) for gravity in de Sitter spacetime. By analytically continuing the Chern–Simons theory, the authors achieve an absolutely convergent semiclassical expansion. This successfully reproduces known results like the Hartle–Hawking wavefunction and demonstrates that Airy behavior arises naturally from the full dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on quantum gravity, the mathematical techniques—such as using Lefschetz thimbles to regularize path integrals and analyze gradient flows near saddle points—are highly relevant to non-equilibrium statistical mechanics or open quantum systems where complex analysis is used to define partition functions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the quantum description of de Sitter spacetime by defining the Chern–Simons–Kodama wavefunctional in Lorentzian self-dual gravity, overcoming issues with convergence and normalization.

**Main result.** Using a Lefschetz thimble definition allows for an absolutely convergent semiclassical expansion, reproducing the Hartle–Hawking Airy wavefunction structure even in anisotropic truncations like Bianchi IX.

**Method.** The authors employ complexification of the connection and utilize Picard–Lefschetz theory to define the functional integral along a specific Lefschetz thimble attached to the de Sitter saddle point.

**Model / system.** The system is quantum gravity in de Sitter spacetime ($\Lambda > 0$), modeled using self-dual Ashtekar variables and constrained by the Chern–Simons–Kodama functional. Analysis is performed via minisuperspace truncations (FRW, Bianchi IX).

**Key observables.** The resulting wavefunction structure, particularly its connection to the Airy function in homogeneous/isotropic reduction and Gaussian damping in shear directions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Positive cosmological constant ($\Lambda > 0$), coupling constants ($\kappa$), and the specific saddle point geometry of de Sitter space.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to minisuperspace truncations (FRW, Bianchi IX), and the Lefschetz thimble prescription is used to regularize the path integral.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the formalism by defining the wavefunctional via complexified connections and applying Picard–Lefschetz theory. It then applies this machinery to specific symmetry reductions (FRW, Bianchi IX) to derive explicit forms for the semiclassical wavefunction.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We give a Lefschetz thimble definition of the Chern--Simons--Kodama wavefunctional in Lorentzian self-dual gravity with positive cosmological constant. By complexifying the connection and choosing the Lefschetz thimble attached to the self-dual de~Sitter saddle, Witten's analytic continuation of Chern--Simons theory replaces the real contour, on which the defining integral is at best conditionally convergent, by an absolutely convergent integration cycle and yields a controlled semiclassical expansion. The relevant integrand is the full holomorphic exponential $e^{I}$, with $I=κY_{\rm CS}+(\text{source})$, and the steepest descent flow is governed by the Morse function $h=\operatorname{Re} I$. In the homogeneous isotropic de Sitter reduction, the construction reproduces the Hartle--Hawking Airy wavefunction. In the Bianchi~IX truncation, we take the Lefschetz thimble attached to the expanding de~Sitter saddle (the contracting branch defines a conjugate Lefschetz thimble); within this sector, the symmetric configuration is the only critical point for $Λ>0$. The corresponding Lefschetz thimble contains one trace direction with the FRW Airy structure and two anisotropic shear directions that are real-Gaussian damped at leading quadratic order. This gives a finite Bianchi~IX wavefunction which, to leading order, is the FRW Airy factor multiplied by a real anisotropy Gaussian. Thus, the Airy behavior is not an artifact of the one-dimensional minisuperspace reduction, but the leading form of the wavefunction on the Lefschetz thimble. We also discuss how the Lefschetz thimble prescription interfaces with Lorentzian reality conditions.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a microscopic theory for excitonic magnetism in ruthenium pyrochlores by analyzing the effective interactions within the low-energy singlet-triplet manifold. It shows that strong spin-orbit coupling can lead to an instability of the nonmagnetic ground state, causing condensation of magnetic excitations (triplons). The resulting phase diagram provides a unified framework connecting theory predictions to experimental measurements in ruthenates.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of excitonic magnetism—where magnetic order arises from bosonic condensation (triplons) rather than localized moments—provides a powerful theoretical lens for understanding correlated materials, linking electronic structure calculations directly to measurable spectroscopic excitations like Raman scattering.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the contradiction between strong spin-orbit coupling suggesting a nonmagnetic singlet ground state in Ru pyrochlores, while experimental evidence shows robust magnetic order.

**Main result.** The theory predicts that the nonmagnetic singlet state becomes unstable toward triplon condensation as hopping parameters increase, leading to magnetically ordered phases and identifying an additional phase unique to this excitonic description.

**Method.** A microscopic theory is developed using a multi-orbital Hubbard model, deriving effective superexchange interactions within the low-energy singlet--triplet manifold. The analysis employs both spectrum calculations (triplon excitations) and variational treatments of the condensed phase.

**Model / system.** The system studied is Ruthenium pyrochlores ($	ext{A}_2	ext{Ru}_2	ext{O}_7$), focusing on $	ext{Ru}^{4+}$ ($d^4$) ions. The model starts from a Hubbard Hamiltonian incorporating SOC and crystal field effects, projecting onto the low-energy singlet--triplet subspace.

**Key observables.** Triplon gap ($\Delta_{	ext{triplon}}$), magnetization ($\mu_B$), and the magnetic phase diagram as a function of hopping parameters.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Microscopic hopping parameters ($|t_{	ext{o}}/\lambda|$), which govern the transition between non-magnetic and magnetic states.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis focuses on the $t_{2g}$ manifold, and in some sections, a harmonic approximation is used by expanding around the singlet-condensed state.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1(b) plots the triplon gap and magnetization versus $|t_{	ext{o}}/\lambda|$, showing the transition point. Other figures illustrate the dispersion of triplon bands and lattice structure details.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the single-ion Hamiltonian (including SOC and crystal field), deriving the effective low-energy superexchange Hamiltonian, analyzing the stability via triplon excitation spectra, mapping out the phase diagram, and finally applying the theory to specific ruthenates like $	ext{Nd}_2	ext{Ru}_2	ext{O}_7$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Strong spin-orbit coupling in $d^4$ systems is expected to stabilize a nonmagnetic $J=0$ singlet ground state, yet many ruthenium pyrochlores exhibit robust long-range magnetic order. Motivated by this apparent contradiction, we develop a microscopic theory of Van Vleck excitonic magnetism on the pyrochlore lattice. Starting from a multi-orbital Hubbard model with spin-orbit coupling, we derive the effective superexchange interactions within the low-energy singlet--triplet manifold of Ru$^{4+}$ ions. We analyze the resulting excitonic Hamiltonian using both the spectrum of triplon excitations and a variational treatment of the condensed phase. We identify the instability of the nonmagnetic singlet state toward triplon condensation and determine the resulting magnetic phase diagram as a function of the microscopic hopping parameters. The phase diagram reproduces the magnetic orders known from conventional pyrochlore models while also predicting an additional magnetic phase unique to the singlet--triplet description. Finally, we apply the theory to the pyrochlore ruthenates, with particular emphasis on Nd$_2$Ru$_2$O$_7$, and show that it lies in close proximity to the excitonic quantum critical point. Our results establish a microscopic framework for understanding excitonic magnetism in pyrochlore ruthenates and their magnetic excitation spectrum, providing direct connections to spectroscopic probes, including Raman scattering.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors report an experimental demonstration of a secure Continuous-Variable Quantum Key Distribution (CV-QKD) system operating over 12 km of optical fiber. By implementing a Local-Local-Oscillator (LLO) scheme, they overcome security loopholes associated with shared local oscillators. The results establish practical key rates exceeding $5 	ext{ Mbit/s}$, paving the way for metropolitan quantum networking.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it pushes quantum communication technology into practical metropolitan deployment regimes by solving critical hardware/security issues inherent in previous CV-QKD implementations.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The goal was to demonstrate a secure Local-Local-Oscillator (LLO) Continuous-Variable Quantum Key Distribution (CV-QKD) system over a significant distance of 12 km optical fiber while mitigating security loopholes.

**Main result.** The experiment successfully achieved secret key rates of $5.11 	ext{ Mbit/s}$ in the asymptotic regime and $4.67 	ext{ Mbit/s}$ in the finite-size regime, validating metropolitan deployment potential.

**Method.** The system utilized Gaussian CV-QKD with fully independent transmitter and receiver lasers, followed by complex classical post-processing including information reconciliation (using MET-LDPC codes) and privacy amplification.

**Model / system.** The physical platform is an LLO CV-QKD setup implemented over a 12 km single-mode optical fiber. The security analysis was conducted under a trusted-device model, addressing the local oscillator loophole by using independent sources at both ends.

**Key observables.** Secret key rates ($5.11 	ext{ Mbit/s}$ asymptotic; $4.67 	ext{ Mbit/s}$ finite-size), measured average attenuation ($0.185 	ext{ dB/km}$), and excess noise ($\xi_B = 15.99 	ext{ mSNU}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Channel length of 12 km; processing large logical frames ($\approx 10^7$ states); asymptotic transmittance $	ext{T} = 0.6$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The security assessment is performed under a trusted-device model, which limits the scope of the security proof.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the experimental setup diagram for LLO CV-QKD components; Figure 2 plots the Secret Key Rate versus fiber length for both asymptotic and finite-size regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the experimental setup, describes the sophisticated digital signal processing (DSP) routine for synchronization and parameter recovery, outlines the classical post-processing steps (reconciliation, estimation, amplification), presents the measured key rates, and discusses future work.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Continuous-variable quantum key distribution (CV-QKD) promises high rates and seamless integration with classical beams within a single optical fiber. Over the years, implementations have been performed by transmitting a local oscillator reference along with the quantum channel, opening security loopholes for eavesdroppers and limiting potential applications. Here, we report on a Gaussian CV-QKD implementation using fully independent transmitter and receiver lasers (local-oscillator sources) over a 12 km fiber spool. The system was experimentally evaluated using logical frames containing approximately $10^7$ coherent states, each composed of ten independently processed subframes of approximately $10^6$ states, and security was assessed in both asymptotic and finite-size regimes under a trusted-device model. The full-fledged classical post-processing is capable of recovering the channel parameters and extracting secret key rates of 5.11 Mbit/s in the asymptotic regime and 4.67 Mbit/s in the finite-size regime, showing good agreement with theoretical predictions. This work establishes the foundation for metropolitan fiber deployment of CV-QKD under strict security constraints.

</details>

A full bulk gap is opened above the lowest exci- ton band. (b) Exciton band structure with the enlarged and more realistic 441 × 441 basis. Additional exciton continua emerge at higher energies, while the low-energy bands remain essentially unchanged. (c) Wilson-loop spectra of the low- est and second exciton bands in (b), corresponding to Chern numbers Cexc = +1 and −1, respectively. (d) Ribbon spec- trum with 20 layers along the open x direction. The chiral exciton edge states traversing the bulk gap are highlighted in red.</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This paper presents a theoretical framework demonstrating that strong electron-hole interactions can induce non-trivial Chern topology in excitons even if the constituent electronic bands are topologically trivial. By constructing an explicit 2D model and analyzing its band structure using symmetry constraints and Wilson loops, the authors provide a general recipe for designing interaction-driven topological materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, controllable mechanism (interaction tuning) to engineer topological properties in excitons, which are neutral quasiparticles relevant for quantum light sources and optoelectronics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To demonstrate that interaction-induced topological excitons (Chern excitons) can exist even when the underlying electronic bands are topologically trivial.

**Main result.** The authors constructed an explicit microscopic model showing that interactions alone can generate non-trivial exciton topology, independent of the band structure's intrinsic topology.

**Method.** The study combines real-space modeling with symmetry analysis (inversion eigenvalues) and momentum-space diagnostics using Wilson loops to calculate the Chern number.

**Model / system.** A two-dimensional exciton model is used, derived from exponentially localized Wannier states for conduction and valence bands. The system's Hamiltonian includes hopping terms and density-density interactions ($U$ terms).

**Key observables.** Chern exciton number ($C_{exc}$), Wilson loop spectra ($\arg W(p_y)$), and chiral edge states in ribbon geometry.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interaction strengths ($U_0, U_x, U_y, V_x$) and the small electron-hole separation $\Delta$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on restricting the Hilbert space to the active conduction-valence subspace; some calculations use a coincident embedding approximation for simplification.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the unit cell geometry, the effect of interactions leading to band inversion (Fig. 1c), and confirm the topology via bulk spectra showing gaps and ribbon spectra exhibiting chiral edge states (Fig. 2d).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the theoretical possibility of interaction-induced topology, constructing an explicit model using Wannier states, demonstrating band inversion via specific interactions ($V_x$), and finally confirming the non-trivial topology using momentum-space diagnostics like Wilson loops.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Exciton topology is commonly inherited from the topology of the underlying electronic bands. Recent theoretical work, however, has shown that the exciton Chern number can in general receive an additional contribution from the topology of the exciton envelope wave function, allowing, in principle, interaction-induced topological excitons even when the constituent electronic bands are topologically trivial. Here, we provide an explicit realization of this case by constructing a two-dimensional exciton model with topologically trivial conduction and valence bands that nevertheless hosts a Chern exciton diagnosed by inversion symmetry. Starting from a real-space limit of exponentially localized Wannier states for the conduction and valence bands, we identify the essential ingredients responsible for the emergent exciton topology and formulate a simple construction recipe. Our work demonstrates that interactions alone can generate nontrivial exciton topology, independent of the topology of the underlying electronic bands, and establishes a general framework for designing interaction-induced topological excitons.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces NS-RIS, a novel and scalable inference algorithm for training Hidden Quantum Markov Models (HQMMs). By employing Newton-Schulz orthogonalization on the Stiefel manifold, it achieves mathematically guaranteed convergence while demonstrating superior predictive performance over classical HMMs and existing quantum models on multiple benchmarks. This moves HQMMs from theoretical constructs to practical tools for sequence data analysis.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on machine learning inference, the rigorous mathematical treatment of optimization on constrained manifolds (Stiefel manifold) using advanced numerical techniques like Newton-Schulz retraction is highly relevant to developing robust parameter estimation methods for complex open quantum systems described by master equations or density matrices.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Existing Hidden Quantum Markov Models (HQMMs) lack practical applicability because their learning methods fail to consistently outperform classical HMMs on data not generated by quantum processes.

**Main result.** The proposed algorithm, NS-RIS, provides the first benchmark evidence that an HQMM can significantly outperform an EM-trained HMM even when trained on non-quantum data. It also establishes a finite-time stationarity guarantee for the inference process.

**Method.** The authors introduce NS-RIS, which uses Newton-Schulz orthogonalization twice to perform scalable, trace-preserving optimization of HQMM parameters constrained to the Stiefel manifold.

**Model / system.** The model is an extension of classical Hidden Markov Models (HMMs) to HQMMs, where probability vectors are replaced by density matrices and stochastic transitions by quantum operations (Kraus operators).

**Key observables.** Mean classification error, test metric improvement percentage, runtime comparison across synthetic and real-world benchmarks (e.g., Splice dataset).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The optimization is performed over the Stiefel manifold $	ext{St}(p, n)$, constrained by $\Gamma^\dagger\Gamma = I_n$. Convergence relies on step size ($\eta$) and momentum parameter ($eta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Theoretical guarantees assume standard conditions like smoothness of the objective function, existence of stochastic gradients, and finite accuracy in the Newton-Schulz approximation.

**Figures summary.** The results are shown across multiple benchmarks: synthetic HMM data (showing 38.5% average improvement), synthetic HQMM data (showing 18.9% metric improvement over COSM), and the real Splice classification benchmark (showing error reductions of up to 17.9%).

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the theoretical framework, deriving convergence bounds for NS-RIS on the Stiefel manifold using Riemannian geometry concepts. It then presents empirical results demonstrating superior performance over classical and state-of-the-art quantum models across various benchmarks.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Hidden Markov models (HMMs) are widely used probabilistic models for discrete sequential data but can be limited when hidden dynamics are complex. Hidden quantum Markov models (HQMMs) generalize HMMs by replacing probability vectors with density matrices and stochastic transitions with quantum operations, enabling richer latent representations. However, existing HQMM learning methods have not consistently outperformed Expectation--Maximization (EM)-trained HMMs on data not generated by quantum processes, limiting their practical applicability. We introduce NS-RIS, Newton--Schulz Retraction-based Inference on the Stiefel manifold, a scalable algorithm for learning trace-preserving HQMMs. NS-RIS uses Newton--Schulz orthogonalization to compute a polar-factor search direction while preserving Stiefel-manifold feasibility, avoiding costly matrix decompositions. We further establish a finite-time stationarity guarantee under standard assumptions on smoothness, stochastic gradients, and finite Newton--Schulz accuracy. Empirically, NS-RIS provides the first benchmark evidence that an HQMM can significantly outperform an EM-trained HMM on data not generated by a quantum model. On synthetic HMM-generated benchmarks, NS-RIS outperforms both EM and the state-of-the-art HQMM method COSM, improving the evaluation metric by an average of 38.5% and by up to 50.6%. On a synthetic HQMM benchmark, it improves the test metric over COSM by 18.9% while reducing runtime by 12.0%. On the real-world Splice classification benchmark, NS-RIS also surpasses both EM and COSM in higher-dimensional latent regimes, reducing mean classification error by 17.9% for latent dimension 6 and 14.9% for latent dimension 8 relative to COSM. These results move HQMMs beyond a theoretical generalization of HMMs and establish them as practical and expressive models for scientific sequence data.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces the Adiabatic Tangentially-Modulated (ATM) pulse as an advanced technique for single-shot qubit frequency tracking, overcoming limitations found in standard methods like Ramsey interferometry. By leveraging quantum adiabatic theory, the ATM pulse creates a highly sensitive, sigmoidal response that allows precise measurement of detuning with low noise. This makes it a promising tool for robust, real-time stabilization of qubits against environmental noise.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, theoretically grounded protocol for improving quantum measurement fidelity in noisy environments. The ability to engineer sensitivity and bandwidth independently is highly valuable for experimentalists working on solid-state qubits where noise mitigation is paramount.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to develop a single-shot, high-fidelity method for tracking frequency noise in two-level quantum systems that overcomes the fundamental trade-offs inherent in conventional measurement techniques like Ramsey interferometry.

**Main result.** The proposed Adiabatic Tangentially-Modulated (ATM) pulse achieves sensitivity comparable to multi-shot averaging while maintaining a lower closed-loop white-noise floor and exhibiting superior robustness against amplitude fluctuations compared to existing protocols.

**Method.** The authors derived the ATM pulse using quantum adiabatic theory, designing its time evolution ($\Omega(t)$ and $\omega_D(t)$) such that it maps continuous detuning values onto a highly selective, near-binary response.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is a two-level quantum system (qubit), whose dynamics are governed by a time-dependent Hamiltonian in the rotating frame. The protocol involves applying the specially shaped ATM pulse sequence to probe the qubit's detuning ($\Delta$).

**Key observables.** Detuning-dependent transition probability $P(\Delta)$, sensitivity ($S$), closed-loop noise floor ($P_{	ext{floor}, N}$), and operational detuning range ($\Delta_{	ext{range}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Pulse parameters include rise time ($	au_r$), constant time ($	au_c$), fall time ($	au_f$), and the final gradient $\dot{\Omega}(	au_T)$. Sensitivity scales inversely with the square root of the pulse's rate of change.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that the adiabatic condition can be maintained during most of the protocol while allowing controlled non-adiabatic transitions at specific points to achieve the desired readout contrast.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the temporal profiles of amplitude and frequency modulation for the ATM pulse, show the geometric evolution on the Bloch sphere corresponding to different detuning regimes, and compare the noise power spectral density (PSD) between ATM and Ramsey tracking methods.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem limitations with conventional techniques, introduces the ATM pulse derived from adiabatic theory, details its mathematical formulation and physical interpretation via Bloch sphere evolution, presents quantitative comparisons of sensitivity and noise floors against Ramsey methods, and concludes by summarizing the practical advantages for real-time stabilization.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Understanding and mitigating noise in two level quantum systems is essential for achieving high fidelity qubit control. Conventional frequency tracking techniques, such as Ramsey interferometry, are fundamentally limited by trade offs between sensitivity, bandwidth, and dynamic range. Here we introduce the adiabatic tangentially-modulated (ATM) pulse, a pulse derived from quantum adiabatic theory that maps qubit detuning onto a sigmoidal, near-binary response. Using numerical simulations supported by analytical modelling, we show that pulse sensitivity and detuning range can be independently engineered through simple design parameters. We derive scaling relations governing these quantities and demonstrate their agreement with simulation. A single shot ATM measurement achieves sensitivity comparable to that obtained from multi-shot Ramsey averaging, enabling tracking of substantially higher frequency noise components with a lower closed-loop white-noise floor. In addition, this pulse exhibits strong robustness to amplitude fluctuations compared with binary response pulses derived from the Shinnar-Le Roux formalism. Together, these properties establish the ATM pulse as a promising approach for robust qubit frequency tracking.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces Generalized Loschmidt Echoes (GLEs) as a powerful theoretical tool to quantify 'quantumness' by testing the non-commutativity of time evolution propagators. By comparing these echoes against predictions derived from classical random noise models, the authors successfully demonstrate a method to isolate and measure intrinsic quantum effects in systems like spin chains and harmonic oscillators.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous, measurable diagnostic tool rooted in echo physics to distinguish true quantum dynamics from classical noise or semiclassical approximations in complex many-body systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to develop a method using generalized Loschmidt echoes (GLEs) to detect intrinsic non-classical dynamical behavior, or 'quantumness,' in various quantum systems.

**Main result.** GLEs provide a diagnostic tool whose deviation from predictions based on classical random-frequency noise models quantifies the presence of genuine quantum features, even in regimes previously thought to be classical.

**Method.** The authors generalize Loschmidt echoes by involving four forward/backward time propagators. Quantumness is diagnosed by checking for non-commutativity among these evolution propagators.

**Model / system.** The analysis applies the method to two main models: the quantum Ising spin chain (critical dynamics) and non-interacting quantum harmonic oscillators, allowing comparison with classical Gaussian noise predictions.

**Key observables.** Generalized Loschmidt Echoes ($	ilde{E}^{ss}(t, 	au)$), and specific differences between echo combinations like $|E^{++}(t, 	au) - E^{--}(t, 	au)|$ which quantify non-classicality.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Time separation ($t, 	au$), coupling constants ($\lambda$, $J$), spectral density parameters ($A, \gamma$), and temperature/inverse temperature ($eta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The detection of quantumness relies on the assumption that classical dynamics correspond to commuting evolution propagators. Calculations often involve taking continuous limits or high-temperature approximations.

**Figures summary.** Figures plot non-classicality quantifiers for both Ising chain and bosonic dynamics, showing how these measures decay relative to standard Loschmidt echo decays.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces GLEs as an extension of LEs. It then applies this framework to calculate echoes for the Ising model (via Jordan-Wigner transformation) and harmonic oscillators (using spectral density methods), culminating in explicit formulas for quantum signatures.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Loschmidt echoes are a well-established method to characterize dynamical properties such as quantum chaos and sensitivity to perturbations. Here we present a straightforward generalization that identifies non-classical dynamical behavior. The generalized echoes involve four forward-backward time propagators. If these propagators do not commute, one can readily identify signatures of this property in the relations between echoes. As an example, detection of intrinsic non-commuting quantum features is analyzed in critical dynamics of a spin chain. Interestingly, this phenomenon is also observed for Gaussian dynamics and high temperature limits which are generally regarded as classical.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a statistical physics framework to understand deep learning by treating the regularization strength as an external field controlling phase transitions. Using analytically solvable linear neural networks, the authors rigorously connect observed feature detection cascades to the underlying geometry of the loss landscape via Hessian spectrum analysis. It provides powerful theoretical tools for interpreting network training dynamics through the lens of critical phenomena.

**Why it may be interesting.** The explicit mapping of deep learning phenomena (feature detection) onto established concepts like phase transitions and Hessian spectra provides a powerful theoretical analogy for understanding complex many-body systems or critical points in other physical models.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a scientific theory of deep learning by connecting its learning dynamics and statistical properties to concepts from statistical physics, specifically investigating how regularization induces phase transitions.

**Main result.** It establishes a rigorous link between cascades of phase transitions, the learnable features detected by the network, and the underlying geometry characterized by the Hessian spectrum. The analysis provides analytic predictions for these transitions using order parameters derived from an effective free energy description.

**Method.** The authors employ analytical techniques to study analytically solvable toy models (Deep Linear Neural Networks), treating the regularization strength as a tunable external field analogous to statistical physics Hamiltonians.

**Model / system.** The system is Deep Linear Neural Networks (DLN) trained with Mean Squared Error loss and $L_2$ weight decay regularization. The analysis focuses on the dynamics of the weights, relating macroscopic behavior to the microscopic geometry of the loss landscape via the Hessian spectrum.

**Key observables.** Phase transitions, order parameters related to learned features, and the eigenvalues/spectrum of the Jacobian/Hessian matrix.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Regularization strength ($eta$), network depth ($n$), and data covariance matrices ($\Sigma_{xx}, \Sigma_{yx}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to deep linear models (DLN) and often assumes zero mean for simplicity. The connection between the macroscopic effective description and microscopic Hessian spectrum provides the core theoretical framework.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the qualitative structure of the regularized loss landscape, showing level crossings indicative of first-order transitions, bifurcation points, and examples of the Hessian spectrum across different network depths.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the connection between learning dynamics and statistical physics. It analyzes the critical point structure for DLNs using regularization strength as a control parameter, deriving order parameters from the loss function's effective potential (Landau theory). Finally, it tackles the microscopic details by analyzing the Jacobian/Hessian spectrum to confirm these macroscopic predictions.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

A scientific theory of deep learning, comprising learning dynamics and statistical properties of learned models, is rapidly gaining attention. One of the corner stones of this development are analytically solvable toy models, allowing for the fully tractable analysis of the learning dynamics. Here we analytically investigate such a toy model using the regularization strength as a tunable external parameter - akin to external fields in statistical physics. In previous studies, (i) an onset of learning transition was predicted analytically and (ii) it was phenomenologically/numerically established that tuning the regularization strength can result in a cascade of phase transitions. The number of those transitions was linked to the geometry of the loss landscape determined by the model complexity. Setting up a rigorous framework underpinning the previous numerical observations, our investigation reveals a precise connection between those cascades of phase transitions, learnable features and the underlying geometry. We provide analytic predictions of these phase transitions as well as tractable order parameters related to learned features. At the level of the minimal model, we connect this macroscopic perspective (that can be condensed into an effective description) to the microscopic perspective in terms of the geometry of the loss landscape characterized by the Hessian spectrum. Thus, the presented model provides a platform to explore and sharpen advances made in the scientific theory of deep learning rooted in statistical physics concepts.

</details>
