**Summary.** This work introduces a novel reservoir computing framework combined with a cross-prediction scheme to quantitatively distinguish chaos from noise in time-series data. By training the model to predict future changes, it generates a correlation coefficient ($R^2$) that serves as a simple diagnostic tool. This method proves robust across synthetic and empirical datasets, offering a powerful tool for analyzing complex physical measurements.

**Why it may be interesting.** While not directly quantum, the methodology relies heavily on analyzing complex, non-equilibrium dynamics in observable time series. The use of advanced machine learning (RC) to extract underlying deterministic structure from noisy data has parallels with reconstructing quantum states or identifying hidden order parameters in many-body systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is developing a quantitative, data-driven method to reliably distinguish between deterministic chaotic time series and stochastic noise, as both exhibit irregular fluctuations.

**Main result.** The authors propose that using the squared Pearson correlation coefficient ($R^2$) derived from a cross-prediction scheme within reservoir computing provides a simple and robust criterion: $R^2 > 0.5$ suggests chaos, while $R^2 < 0.5$ suggests noise.

**Method.** A purely data-driven approach using Reservoir Computing (RC) trained to predict the *change* ($\Delta x_{t+1}$) of a variable from its current state ($x_t$). The cross-prediction scheme is key to suppressing spurious correlations inherent in noisy data.

**Model / system.** The method applies to general scalar time series data, tested on diverse synthetic chaotic systems (e.g., Lorenz, LogMap) and various noise processes (e.g., Gaussian, Flicker, fGn). Empirical tests include physiological signals (VOICE, RR intervals) and climate indices (NAO index).

**Key observables.** The squared Pearson correlation coefficient ($R^2$) between the true future change ($\Delta x_{t+1}$) and the predicted future change ($\hat{\Delta x}_{t+1}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Hyperparameters for RC (e.g., leak rate $a_{	ext{leak}}$) are optimized; classification thresholds include $R^2 > 0.5$ for chaos.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that deterministic chaotic dynamics impose a measurable, non-stochastic structure on the local changes ($\Delta x$), which can be captured by the RC framework better than random noise processes.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic of the RC prediction/classification process, show scatter plots comparing true vs. predicted values for various systems (Lorenz, Flicker), and summarize $R^2$ performance across multiple synthetic chaos and noise datasets.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem, details the reservoir computing framework with cross-prediction, presents results on diverse synthetic time series to establish the criterion, discusses robustness against real-world data limitations, and concludes by applying the method to a wide range of empirical physical and biological datasets.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Distinguishing chaos from noise using time-series data is fundamentally challenging because both exhibit irregular fluctuations and share many statistical and dynamical characteristics. Existing methods face two key limitations: temporally correlated noise can yield spurious signatures of chaos, and analyses of scalar time series often require explicit choices of embedding parameters. Here, we propose a purely data-driven method for distinguishing chaos and noise based on a reservoir-computing framework with a cross-prediction scheme. In the proposed approach, the model is trained to predict the future change of a variable from its current value, thereby combining a short-term predictability test with a test of the smoothness of deterministic flows. The recurrent structure of reservoir computing enables effective prediction of high-dimensional chaotic dynamics even from scalar time series without explicit delay-coordinate reconstruction, while the cross-prediction framework strongly suppresses spurious predictive correlations arising from noise. We apply the proposed method to diverse synthetic and empirical time series. Chaotic systems consistently yield strong correlations between the true and predicted future changes, whereas noise processes remain clearly separated in a low-correlation regime. The method also exhibits substantial robustness to practical limitations in empirical data, including measurement noise, limited data length, and increasing prediction lag. These results demonstrate that the squared Pearson correlation coefficient provides a simple quantitative criterion for distinguishing chaos from noise directly from observed time-series data.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a rigorous statistical theory to analyze how random potential landscapes affect particle dynamics via thermal activation or quantum tunneling. By mapping the problem onto integrals over Gaussian random coefficients, the authors derive formulas linking measurable escape rates directly back to the underlying statistical properties (like the Green's function) of the disorder itself.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful statistical framework for characterizing disordered media by measuring macroscopic dynamic responses, which is highly relevant for understanding energy barriers in complex quantum systems beyond simple mean-field treatments.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the statistical stability of complex, random potential energy landscapes (potential potentials) when subjected to thermal fluctuations or quantum tunneling.

**Main result.** The authors derive algebraic expressions for calculating the expectation values of escape rates (thermal and quantum) based on the statistical properties of the potential's local derivatives. They establish a direct link between these activation behaviors and the underlying Green's function characterizing the random potential.

**Method.** The core method involves reducing functional integrals over Gaussian random potentials to algebraic integrals over the expansion coefficients $\{u_n\}$ using derived probability density functions. Analysis includes calculating area fractions and asymptotic scaling laws for escape rates.

**Model / system.** The model is a point particle moving in a complex, disordered potential landscape $U(x)$, motivated by systems like superconducting vortices trapped by defects. The randomness of the potential is assumed to follow a Gaussian functional distribution characterized by its two-point correlation function.

**Key observables.** Statistical probability distributions of potential derivatives ($p(u_0, u_2)$), thermal activation rate ($\langle\Omegaangle$), and quantum tunneling rate ($\langle|T|^2angle$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature ($T$) or inverse temperature ($eta$), characteristic energy scale ($E_0$), and defect density parameters ($n_p\xi$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The potential is modeled by a Gaussian random functional distribution, allowing the reduction of complex integrals to algebraic forms. The analysis often truncates the potential expansion after low-order derivatives.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the probability density functions $p(u_0, u_2)$, $p(u_1, u_3)$, and $p(u_2, u_3)$ for various defect densities, showing convergence to analytic results in the Gaussian limit.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the statistical problem using potential expansions, deriving the algebraic tools via Gaussian statistics, applying these tools to calculate specific observables (area fractions, $\langle\Omegaangle$, $\langle|T|^2angle$), and finally analyzing the asymptotic behavior in different physical limits.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In numerous physical, chemical, and biological systems the dynamics can be reduced to the motion of state variables in a complex potential landscape. In case the manifold is known, the motion and response of the embedded object can be described deterministically up to stochastic effects usually associated with a noise. In contrast, if the manifold is unknown, the static and dynamic response of the state variable may be used as a spectroscopic tool to characterize the potential landscape. Inspired by a seminal work of L.\ Embon and co-workers, [Sci.\ Rep.\ \textbf{5}, 7598 (2015)] we investigate the statistical properties of potential minima, in particular, their stability to thermal and quantum activation. For Gaussian random manifolds, we derive an algebraic expression to evaluate the statistical probability of the potential character (value, slope, curvature, ...). With this tool, we compute the expectation value for the rate of thermal and quantum activation and link these findings to the principal characteristics of the Gaussian potential, i.e., its Green's function. This link provides the opportunity to access information on the potential's Green's function by studying the activation behavior of an object in this manifold.

</details>

**Summary.** This work theoretically investigates the Orbital Hall Effect in kagome metals by decomposing its contribution across different electronic orbital channels. It predicts distinct OHE signs for various compounds, showing that hybridization dictates the net sign. Furthermore, it demonstrates how loop-current phases can induce measurable local orbital angular momentum responses.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state transport, the detailed analysis of orbital degrees of freedom, Berry curvature contributions to transport coefficients, and emergent topological responses in correlated systems provides fundamental theoretical tools applicable to understanding exotic quantum states relevant to AMO physics (e.g., orbital entanglement).

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates electronic orbital degrees of freedom, specifically focusing on generating and controlling orbital currents via the Orbital Hall Effect (OHE) in kagome lattice metals.

**Main result.** The OHE sign depends critically on the competition between contributions from different orbital sectors ($|	ext{l}_z^d|=2$ vs. $|	ext{l}_z|=1$), and loop-current phases can induce finite local atomic orbital angular momentum.

**Method.** Calculations utilize a tight-binding model derived from first principles, employing the Kubo formula for transport properties and performing detailed orbital-sector decomposition analysis.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on transition metal kagome-lattice metals ($	ext{CsTi}_3	ext{Bi}_5$, $	ext{CsV}_3	ext{Sb}_5$, $	ext{CsCr}_3	ext{Sb}_5$) modeled by a 30-orbital tight-binding Hamiltonian. The analysis is restricted to a 2D approximation and neglects spin-orbit coupling.

**Key observables.** Orbital Hall Effect (OHE), local atomic orbital angular momentum, symmetric components of the orbital conductivity tensor, and orbital Berry curvature.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Electron filling ($n$), hybridization strength between $p$ and $d$ orbitals, and the presence/absence of loop currents.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Spin-orbit coupling (SOC) is neglected in this work, and the analysis relies on a 2D model approximation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate crystal structures, band dispersions showing element substitution effects, and momentum-space distributions ($k$-space) of the OHE Berry curvature for the three studied compounds.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the theoretical framework using orbital-sector decomposition to predict OHE signs across different kagome metals. It then analyzes how $p$-$d$ hybridization modulates this sign, concluding with an investigation into loop-current phases that induce local angular momentum and modify conductivity symmetry.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The study of electronic orbital degrees of freedom, including the generation and control of orbital currents and orbital angular momentum, has emerged as a vibrant research field. Here, we study the orbital Hall effect (OHE), one of the key mechanisms for orbital current generation, in transition-metal kagome-lattice metals. We propose a large positive OHE in CsTi$_3$Bi$_5$ and negative OHEs in CsV$_3$Sb$_5$ and CsCr$_3$Sb$_5$ models. Orbital-sector decomposition shows that the $\vert l^z_d \vert=2$ $d$-orbital channel gives a positive contribution, whereas the $\vert l^z \vert=1$ $d$- and $p$-orbital channels can give negative contributions. Control calculations suggest a persistent orbital-sector sign tendency near the actual filling, while strong $p$-$d$ hybridization modulates the quantitative balance and total OHE sign. Thus, the compound and filling dependence of the OHE reflects both the sign tendency of each orbital sector and the hybridization-controlled balance among them. Furthermore, we investigate the loop-current phase of CsV$_3$Sb$_5$ and show that it induces finite local atomic orbital angular momentum. We also show that loop-current-related symmetry lowering allows finite symmetric components of the orbital conductivity tensor. This study provides a basis for exploring orbital currents and local orbital-angular-momentum responses in strongly correlated kagome metals.

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces the Effective Quantum Fisher Information (EQFI) to quantify estimation precision in multi-parameter quantum sensor networks. It develops a phase map revealing that excessive quantum correlations can paradoxically degrade both local and global measurement capabilities. The methodology provides principled guidelines for engineering network states to achieve high global sensitivity while ensuring individual parameters remain locally private.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous geometric framework for understanding how quantum correlations can either enhance or actively degrade sensing performance, offering design principles for optimal network states.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper characterizes the trade-off between local and global estimation precision in quantum sensor networks while accounting for nuisance parameters.

**Main result.** It establishes a fundamental bottleneck, the 'barrel effect,' bounding global Fisher information by the weakest weighted local sensing capacity, and identifies conditions for achieving intrinsic local privacy.

**Method.** The authors develop an exact Effective Quantum Fisher Information (EQFI)-based phase map to geometrically describe how quantum correlations redistribute metrological information between local and global estimates.

**Model / system.** The system is a general N-node Quantum Sensor Network (QSN) whose estimation precision is governed by the multiparameter Quantum Fisher Information Matrix (QFIM).

**Key observables.** Effective Quantum Fisher Information (EQFI), Global EQFI ($E_w$), Local EQFI ($E_i$), and correlation parameters ($\mu_i, s_i, r_i$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The optimal correlation strength $\mu^*_i$ defining the transition from beneficial to detrimental correlations.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the QFIM is nonsingular for core derivations; local privacy is defined as a QFIM-level non-identifiability condition.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the information flow, mapping the trade-off between local and global EQFI across different correlation regimes, including an over-correlated penalty zone.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by introducing EQFI, developing the phase map to analyze the local-global trade-off (including the 'barrel effect'), and finally applying this geometry to derive conditions for intrinsic local privacy.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum sensor networks typically encode N unknown parameters while targeting a single linear combination, rendering the N-1 remaining parameters as nuisance directions. To rigorously quantify estimation precision under such nuisances, we introduce the concept of effective quantum Fisher information (EQFI) and develop an exact EQFI-based phase map that systematically describes the allocation between local and global EQFI. Leveraging this geometric framework, we identify a fundamental bottleneck termed the "barrel effect": the global EQFI is strictly bounded by the weakest weighted local sensing capacity among all nodes. We further establish concrete conditions for saturating this bound. Crucially, this geometric map delineates how the trade-off between local and global EQFI depends dynamically on quantum correlations, and uncovers a counterintuitive "overcorrelated" regime where excessive correlations actively degrade both local and global performance. Finally, we apply the phase map to intrinsic local privacy and identify the condition under which every local parameter is inaccessible while the desired global combination remains estimable. Overall, our work provides a principled methodology for engineering optimal network states in quantum sensing architectures.

</details>

**Summary.** This work characterizes Niobium Titanium Nitride (NbTiN) as a promising material for advanced nanomechanical resonators intended for use with superconducting circuits. By measuring resonance frequencies using optical interferometry, the authors determined that NbTiN possesses high tensile stress and good mechanical quality factors across relevant cryogenic temperatures. This establishes it as a viable platform for future cavity electro- and nanomechanics applications.

**Why it may be interesting.** While primarily mechanical/materials science, the focus on superconducting integration and high-quality factor resonators makes it relevant to quantum optomechanics and circuit QED platforms where material limitations are key bottlenecks.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The goal is to develop novel, high-performance material platforms for nanomechanical resonators that can be integrated with superconducting circuits.

**Main result.** Niobium Titanium Nitride (NbTiN) was characterized as a promising material due to its favorable mechanical properties, including high tensile stress ($\sim 0.81 	ext{ GPa}$) and a measurable intrinsic quality factor ($Q_{	ext{intr}}$ up to $850$).

**Method.** Mechanical properties were determined by fabricating doubly clamped nanostrings and measuring their resonance frequencies using optical interferometry across varying temperatures.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a thin film of NbTiN used as a nanostring resonator. Analysis involves fitting the measured resonance frequency ($\Omega$) dependence on string length ($l$) or harmonic order ($n$) to extract elastic constants and stress.

**Key observables.** Tensile stress ($\sigma$), Young's modulus ($E$), intrinsic mechanical quality factor ($Q_{	ext{intr}}$), and thermal expansion coefficient ($\alpha$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** High tensile stress up to $0.81 	ext{ GPa}$; Young's modulus of $\sim 181 	ext{ GPa}$; operating temperature range down to $13 	ext{ K}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis heavily relies on the high tensile stress approximation for relating resonance frequency to mechanical parameters, and comparisons between different fitting models (e.g., fundamental mode vs. higher harmonics) reveal discrepancies.

**Figures summary.** Figures show SEM images of nanostrings; characterization plots display resonance peaks ($\Omega$ vs $1/l$), the temperature dependence of $\sigma$, $E$, and $Q_{	ext{intr}}$, and comparisons between different fitting models for stress extraction.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the need for new superconducting nanomechanical materials to presenting experimental measurements using optical interferometry. It then details the analysis methods used to extract elastic constants ($\sigma, E$) and quality factors ($Q_{	ext{intr}}$) as functions of temperature and geometry.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Over the past decades, high-coherence mechanical resonators have been continuously pushed to new limits, using techniques such as dissipation dilution and clamp-tapering to enhance their quality factor beyond intrinsic material limitations. Today, these mechanical resonators are often fabricated from silicon-nitride, silicon-carbide or aluminum. Recently, however, interest in novel material platforms has grown, especially those allowing for the integration within superconducting circuits. Among these, superconducting nitrides stand out as particularly promising due to their high transition temperatures compared to elementary superconductors. Here, we introduce them as nanomechanical resonators and report on the fabrication and characterization of highly stressed, doubly clamped niobium titanium nitride (NbTiN) nanostring resonators. Using optical interferometry, we determine the elastic properties and mechanical quality factor from room temperature to 13 K. We observe high tensile stress up to 0.81 GPa along with a Young's modulus of 181 GPa and an intrinsic mechanical quality factor up to 850. With these favorable mechanical properties, NbTiN constitutes a promising material platform for future applications in cavity electro- and nanomechanics.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper develops a theory showing that band topology dictates a dipole moment associated with chiral domain walls in ferromagnets. Applied to twisted TMDs, this geometric coefficient ($c_G$) predicts that the DW metastability is highly sensitive to topological phase transitions, offering a new diagnostic tool for probing quantum geometry in moiré materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete example of how subtle quantum geometric invariants (like the second Chern form moment) can govern macroscopic stability properties (metastability) in condensed matter systems, linking topology directly to observable dynamics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how band topology, specifically through a geometric dipole moment, can stabilize chiral domain walls (DWs) in ferromagnets even when the net charge response vanishes.

**Main result.** A non-zero geometric dipole coefficient ($c_G$) leads to an energy cost that stabilizes DWs against collapse, and this coefficient is highly sensitive to topological phase transitions within twisted TMDs like tMoTe2.

**Method.** The authors use symmetry-constrained Hartree-Fock methods on the occupied projector to calculate the mixed real-space–momentum-space second Chern form, which yields $c_G$.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled using twisted transition-metal dichalcogenides (tTMDs) exhibiting conjugate Chern bands. The theory interpolates between Valence Polarized (VP) and Inter-Valley Coherent (IVC) states via a trial Hamiltonian $\hat{H}_{	ext{MF}}(\theta, \phi)$.

**Key observables.** Geometric dipole coefficient ($c_G$), transverse dipole density, domain wall energy $E(R)$, and the Chern number ($C$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Twist angle ($\theta_M$), layer bias ($u_D$), Zeeman field strength, and the competition between dipolar repulsion and surface tension.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes an internal relative U(1) spin symmetry and time-reversal symmetry. Calculations are performed in the weak coupling regime for some analyses.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the dependence of $c_G$ on system parameters, showing sharp drops across topological transitions (e.g., $C=1$ to $C=0$) that correlate with changes in domain wall stability.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes a theoretical framework linking band topology to dipole moments in DWs. It then applies this formalism to tTMDs, calculating $c_G$ using HF methods and demonstrating how its variation explains experimental observations of long-lived excitations near topological phase boundaries.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Band topology can have an imprint on the excitations of a ferromagnet. A known example is quantum Hall ferromagnets and their lattice analogs; when both flavors have the same Chern number $C$, a smooth skyrmion texture binds charge $-eC$ per unit winding. Here, we consider instead the case of conjugate Chern bands related by time-reversal. We show that, despite the vanishing net charge response, a smooth texture can be associated with a dipole response---a domain wall (DW) with an in-plane winding along its length can bind a nonzero dipole density transverse to the wall. The strength of this dipole density is controlled by a dimensionless coefficient $c_G$. Although not quantized, the geometric dipole coefficient $c_G$ is a moment of the second Chern form of the occupied projector in mixed (momentum and order-parameter) space and is generally nonzero. The dipole-decorated DW can thus become metastable at a finite radius due to the competition between dipolar repulsion and the usual surface tension, even at a finite Zeeman field. In a realistic model of twisted MoTe$_2$, we find that $c_G$ drops sharply across a transition within the valley-polarized (VP) phase from a $C=1$ to a $C=0$ ferromagnet. This naturally explains recent pump-probe experiments~\cite{exp} at hole filling $ν=1$, in which a long-lived excitation survives reverse fields far exceeding the saturation field but disappears at an intermediate displacement field despite only weak changes in conventional magnetic diagnostics. Metastable spin textures thus serve as a sensitive probe of band quantum geometry, and as an intrinsic bottleneck for fast optical control of moiré ferromagnets in Chern-conjugate bands.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces GALA codes, an algebraic framework for designing Quantum Error Correction codes tailored specifically for reconfigurable neutral-atom arrays. By constructing these codes using group lifts, the authors ensure that desirable properties like high rate and compatibility with physical gate operations are built in from the start. This yields several highly optimized code candidates suitable for near-term quantum computation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly bridges abstract quantum error correction theory with concrete physical hardware constraints (AODs, limited qubits) by embedding hardware requirements into the code design process itself.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is designing Quantum Error Correction (QEC) codes that are compact, self-dual, and maintain a high encoding rate ($\ge 1/2$) for implementation on near-term quantum hardware.

**Main result.** The authors introduce GALA codes, which provide an algebraically transparent construction method yielding several highly desirable instances, including a compact $[[132, 30, 12]]$ code with low logical error rates.

**Method.** They use Group-Action Lifts over product groups ($G = H_k 	imes C_m$) to build codes. This construction makes hardware compatibility and desired logical operations customizable inputs rather than post-hoc checks.

**Model / system.** The physical platform is reconfigurable neutral-atom arrays (RNAA) using qubits trapped in optical tweezers, with interactions controlled by acousto-optic deflectors (AODs). The code structure is defined algebraically via group theory lifts.

**Key observables.** Code parameters such as block length ($n$), logical qubit count ($k$), distance ($d$), encoding rate ($r$), and syndrome extraction cycle time (TQEC).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Rate $\ge 1/2$, girth $\ge 6$, low logical error rates ($	ext{LER} < 10^{-8}$), and compatibility with AOD-based physical gates.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the ability to implement specific group structures ($H_k$ non-abelian, $C_m$ abelian) and relies on the algebraic framework of lifts over product groups.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare GALA codes against baselines regarding overhead and cycle time; one figure illustrates the syndrome extraction schedule using AOD sweeps; another details the logical gates ($	ext{H}_	au, 	ext{S}_	au$) for a specific code instance $[[132, 30, 12]]$ via its Gram matrix.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces GALA codes by defining their algebraic construction using product groups. It then details the resulting properties, presents several optimized code instances in tables (e.g., TABLE S4), and finally analyzes the explicit implementation of logical gates on a representative code instance.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

High rate quantum low-density parity-check codes on reconfigurable neutral-atom arrays can reduce the overhead of quantum error correction, but near-term devices support only hundreds of qubits with limited reconfigurability from a few crossed acousto-optic deflectors (AOD). A practical code must be compact in addition to low-overhead, with checks and logical gates mapping onto hardware-compatible physical instructions. We introduce the GALA codes, or Group-Action Lifts with Active orthogonality, that lifts over a product group $G = H_k \times C_m$ (or $H_k \ltimes C_m^k$). The small non-abelian factor $H_k$ supplies active orthogonality, reaching $1/2$ rate with above-weight distance, while the large abelian factor $C_m$ supplies symmetries that give code automorphisms and explicit, simple AOD move schedules. Hardware compatibility and logical capability thereby become customizable inputs to a code search rather than properties verified post-hoc, making GALA designer codes by construction. The GALA family contains several previously discovered rate-1/2 Kasai codes of Ref. [arXiv:2601.08824, arXiv:2604.16209] while exposing simpler parameter bounds, logical operations, and ZX-dual variants with AOD-compatible fold-transversal Clifford gates. Our search yields a compact self-dual $[[132, 30, 12]]$ with a small number of $4$-cycles (almost girth-6) and below $10^{-8}$ logical error rate (LER) for memory at $10^{-3}$ physical error rate, 3.1ms syndrome-extraction cycle and transversal Clifford gates; a girth-6, rate-$1/2$ $[[672, 336, 12]]$ with a 6.76ms cycle with below $10^{-10}$ LER (extrapolated) and rate-$1/2$ barrier-breaking $[[1752, 880, 14]]$ and $[[2232, 1120, 16]]$ with exactly certified distances greater than check weights and all smaller than previously known hardware compatible rate-1/2 codes.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces the Kramers-Henneberger atom as a promising, ultrafast platform for quantum computing. By engineering the potential using intense laser fields, they demonstrated coherent single-qubit gates (Z and S) operating on femtosecond timescales. This work suggests a clear pathway toward attosecond-scale quantum operations limited by structured leakage rather than environmental decoherence.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work proposes an entirely new physical mechanism—utilizing strong field dressing to define a qubit basis—for achieving quantum gates at unprecedented speeds, bridging attosecond physics with quantum computation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The authors propose using the Kramers-Henneberger (KH) atom as a novel platform to achieve ultrafast single-qubit gate operations.

**Main result.** They demonstrated coherent Z and S gates on femtosecond timescales, which is six orders of magnitude faster than current superconducting qubit gates.

**Method.** The study relies on solving the time-dependent Schrödinger equation (TDSE) in both the length gauge and a transformed KH frame.

**Model / system.** The physical system is an electron governed by the dynamics within a strong, time-varying laser field, modeled using the KH potential. The qubit basis is defined by the two lowest eigenstates engineered by the strong driving field itself.

**Key observables.** Gate fidelity, gate operation timescales (femtoseconds), and decoherence times.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Strong field parameters ($\epsilon_{0s} = 5.0$ a.u., $\omega_s = 0.7$ a.u.) and weak control field frequencies ($\omega_w$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The fidelity is limited by structured leakage rather than stochastic decoherence, suggesting the process can be error-suppressed via pulse engineering.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the KH atom as a qubit platform, derives the Hamiltonian in different gauges (length and KH frame), simulates gate operations using TDSE solvers, and compares results between full time-dependent and time-averaged descriptions to confirm feasibility.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We propose and demonstrate the Kramers-Henneberger KH) atom as a novel qubit platform for ultrafast single-qubit gate operations. In the KH frame, the time-averaged strong laser field engineers a double-well potential whose two lowest eigenstates define the qubit basis, so that the computational structure is created and maintained by the driving field itself. A weak resonant control field drives coherent gate operations: full time-dependent Schrödinger equation simulations with the complete time-dependent KH potential confirm a Z gate and S gate of the order of femtoseconds, six orders of magnitude faster than laser-driven superconducting qubit gates. Decoherence characterisation gives longer decoherence times than those required for the gate operations. The complete six-gate single-qubit set is demonstrated in the time-averaged two-level limit, with strong agreement between the full and time-averaged descriptions confirming that fidelity is limited by structured leakage rather than stochastic decoherence. In principle, this is an error channel suppressible through pulse engineering. These results constitute the first demonstration of coherent single-qubit gates in a strong-field setting, with a clear pathway toward attosecond-scale operations.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper translates the classical Quantum Lattice Boltzmann method for solving the Dirac equation into an exact sequence of quantum gates. By mapping every physical step—including streaming and collision—to specific unitary circuits, the authors prove that this complex simulation can be run on a gate-model quantum computer. This work establishes a blueprint for simulating relativistic fermionic dynamics using quantum computation.

**Why it may be interesting.** It demonstrates a concrete pathway to implementing complex, non-equilibrium quantum dynamics (like Dirac evolution) directly using known quantum gate primitives, which is crucial for developing quantum simulation algorithms beyond simple Hamiltonians.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To determine if the Succi–Dellar Dirac Quantum Lattice Boltzmann (QLB) scheme can be written exactly as a quantum circuit.

**Main result.** The authors provide an exact, gate-level construction for every operation of the 3D Dirac QLB scheme, reproducing classical results to machine precision on state-vector emulators.

**Method.** They map each elementary physical operation (rotation, collision, streaming shift) into a sequence of fundamental quantum gates and circuits.

**Model / system.** The model is the Dirac equation solved via the QLB scheme on a lattice. The simulation uses amplitude encoding across separate spinor and position registers to represent the field state.

**Key observables.** Density deviation (reproduced to machine precision), particle velocity ($v_g$), group velocities, and mean speed relative to $c$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Lattice size ($N$), dimensionless mass ($	ilde{m}$), potential couplings, and the number of qubits required ($\propto \log N$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The scope is limited to establishing implementability on a gate-model quantum computer; state preparation and measurement costs are left as open questions.

**Figures summary.** References include comparisons of classical vs. qubit circuit implementations, and analysis showing how oblique motion slows massless particles below $c$ in 2D/3D lattices compared to continuum estimates.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the unitary nature of the QLB steps, then provides gate-level constructions for all components (rotation, collision, shift). It verifies these circuits on emulators across 1D, 2D, and 3D tests before discussing physical implications like velocity dispersion.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The quantum lattice Boltzmann (QLB) scheme of Succi and Dellar advances a four-component Dirac spinor on a lattice by a fixed sequence of local, exactly norm-preserving operations: a basis rotation, a collision, a streaming shift, and the inverse rotation. This unitarity is a structural property of the scheme, not an approximation, which suggests that a QLB time step should map onto a sequence of quantum gates. Here we make that mapping explicit. We give a gate-level construction of every operation of the three-dimensional Dirac QLB scheme: the fixed rotation gates, the collision gate, the streaming shift as a controlled increment on a position register, the position-dependent potential as a phase oracle, and periodic and reflecting (bounce-back) boundary conditions as unitary circuits. We then compose them into single-axis, two- and three-dimensional time steps. On a state-vector emulator the resulting circuits reproduce the classical QLB solver to machine precision (maximum density deviation between $3.7\times10^{-12}$ and $1.0\times10^{-17}$ across the one-, two-, and three-dimensional tests), so the circuits are the scheme rather than an approximation of it. The scope is narrow: we establish that the Succi-Dellar theory can be implemented on a (gate-model) quantum computer, and report the associated gate counts. We make no claim of computational advantage; state preparation, measurement, and asymptotic cost are discussed as open questions. All operators, circuits, tests, and figures are reproducible from the open-source quantumKineticMethods library.

</details>

**Summary.** This work tests a hybrid transformer model incorporating a Parameterized Quantum Circuit (PQC) for cross-modality classification. By performing controlled component attribution studies against classical baselines, the authors found no consistent evidence of a quantum advantage from the PQC layer. The study emphasizes that careful methodological controls are necessary before any performance gains can be attributed to the quantum components.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on ML classification, the rigorous testing of PQC components against classical counterparts provides valuable insights into the necessary conditions for demonstrating genuine quantum utility in hybrid models, which is relevant to understanding resource scaling in complex quantum simulations.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates whether integrating a Parameterized Quantum Circuit (PQC) into a hybrid quantum-classical transformer architecture improves performance on classical cross-modality classification tasks.

**Main result.** The controlled factorial analysis failed to show any consistent or statistically significant contribution from the PQC, concluding that no quantum advantage was established under the tested protocol.

**Method.** A rigorous component attribution study using an interface-matched $2 	imes 2$ factorial design was employed, systematically replacing the PQC with classical maps and the attention decoder with linear heads.

**Model / system.** The model is Quantum-Embedded Attention (QEA), which processes features through a learnable projector into an angle vector ($\xi$), feeds this into a shallow PQC acting on qubits to generate Pauli expectation values ($\mathbf{m}$), and finally uses a classical attention decoder.

**Key observables.** Paired performance differences ($\Delta Q-C$) derived from the factorial analysis, accuracy metrics across multiple datasets (e.g., Breast Cancer Wisconsin).

**Important parameters / regimes.** $n_q$ (number of qubits), $2 	imes 2$ factorial structure, and controlled comparison against classical baselines.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that component contributions can be isolated via interface-matched controls; the results are limited to ideal statevector simulations without hardware noise modeling.

**Figures summary.** Notes reference tables detailing forward costs (scaling with $n_q$) and parameter counts for the factorial analysis, but no specific figure summaries were extracted.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the QEA architecture, details the controlled component attribution methodology using a factorial design, presents results showing non-significant quantum contributions, discusses cross-modality performance descriptively, and concludes by emphasizing methodological rigor over claiming advantage.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We test whether a parameterized quantum circuit (PQC) improves a hybrid quantum-classical model's performance on classical datasets, using an interface-matched classical map as the control while holding all other components fixed. Our architecture, Quantum-Embedded Attention (QEA), uses a learnable projector to compress backbone features into an $n_q$-dimensional angle vector, a shallow PQC to map those angles to one- and two-qubit Pauli expectations, and a classical attention decoder to produce class logits. We hypothesized the PQC would improve accuracy or seed-to-seed stability over a classical map with matched input/output dimensions. We test this with an interface-matched $2\times2$ factorial on Breast Cancer Wisconsin at $n_q\in\{4,8\}$, independently swapping the PQC for a classical map and the attention decoder for a linear head, across five paired seeds per cell. Three of four paired quantum-minus-classical $95\%$ confidence intervals include zero; the fourth, a $+1.63$ percentage-point contrast for the attention decoder at $n_q=4$, reverses sign at $n_q=8$ and does not survive correction across the four contrasts. The experiment thus shows no consistent PQC contribution and cannot establish equivalence. A five-dataset cross-modality grid shows comparable accuracy on AG~News, Breast Cancer Wisconsin, and BirdCLEF but a large deficit on CIFAR-10; these cells are not interface-matched and are interpreted descriptively. We report all planned canonical runs, distinguish current Pauli-readout results from legacy probability-readout experiments, and analyze bottleneck, simulation, finite-shot, and noise limitations. The results do not establish a quantum advantage; they demonstrate why controlled component attribution is necessary before crediting a hybrid model's performance to its quantum layer.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously proves that for Ising models defined on sequences of weighted graphs, the free energy density remains stable (continuous) under a specific type of graph convergence called left convergence. This breakthrough relies on imposing a spectral condition on the graph's adjacency matrix and uses advanced techniques involving log-concavity and Langevin dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical statistical mechanics, the rigorous mathematical framework connecting local graph structure convergence to global thermodynamic quantities is highly relevant to understanding complex systems modeled by neural networks or random graphs in quantum information theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central problem is determining which 'global' graph properties of an Ising model are determined by local weak convergence when the underlying graph structure converges in a specific sense.

**Main result.** Under a spectral condition on the weighted adjacency matrix and sparsity assumptions, the free energy density of the Ising model is proven to be continuous with respect to the left convergence topology.

**Method.** The proof utilizes decomposing the Ising measure into log-concave combinations of product measures and leveraging the rapid mixing properties of Langevin dynamics for such measures.

**Model / system.** The system analyzed is the Ising Model on a weighted graph $G=(A, h)$, whose probability measure is defined by $\mu_G(\sigma) \propto \exp\left(rac{1}{2}\langle \sigma, A\sigmaangle + \langle h, \sigmaangleight)$. The analysis focuses on sequences of such graphs indexed by $N$.

**Key observables.** The normalized free energy density, $F(G) = rac{1}{N} \log Z(G)$, is the primary observable.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Spectral properties of the adjacency matrix (eigenvalues lying in an interval $I$ with $|I|<1$), graph sparsity ($\|A\|_{\infty}$ bounded), and external field strength ($h$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core result requires a spectral condition on $A_N$ and that the sequence of graphs must be sparse. The continuity proof relies on log-concavity and rapid mixing.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in the notes, but the results provide limit theorems for specific models like spin glasses and anti-ferromagnets.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes a general framework by relating graph convergence (left convergence) to physical properties. It then proves continuity of the free energy density under spectral conditions using advanced tools from stochastic processes (Langevin dynamics) before deriving explicit limit theorems for various disordered models.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

A sequence of sparse weighted graphs $(G_N:N\ge 1)$ indexed by the number of vertices $N$ is said to be left-convergent if all (suitably weighted) subgraph counts converge to a limit as $N\to\infty$. This notion generalizes in a natural way Benjamini-Schramm's definition of local weak convergence. A broad research agenda aims at determining which `global' graph properties are determined by left or local weak convergence (in other words, which of these properties are in fact local). We prove that, under a spectral condition on the weighted adjacency matrix ${\boldsymbol A}_N$ of graph $G_N$, the free energy density of the Ising model on this weighted graph is continuous in the left convergence topology (and hence is a local function). Our proof uses the decomposition of the Ising measure as a log-concave combination of product measures, and of the rapid mixing of Langevin dynamics for log-concave measures. As applications, we derive new limit theorems for the free energy density of spin glasses, antiferromagnets, and magnetization constrained ferromagnetic models, on locally tree-like graphs.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a theoretical framework to calculate the probability distribution of the radius of gyration for interacting polymer chains (ISAWs). By extending free energy calculations using cumulant expansions, the authors achieve an explicit formula that is validated against detailed Monte Carlo simulations. This allows them to map out the phase diagram governing the coil-globule transition in these polymeric systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on polymer physics, the rigorous application of scaling theories, critical phenomena analysis (coil-globule transition), and advanced statistical mechanics techniques for finite systems provides methodological insights applicable to analyzing complex many-body systems in AMO or quantum field theory contexts.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To derive an explicit, finite-size probability distribution for the radius of gyration ($R$) of an Interacting Self-Avoiding Walk (ISAW) as a function of chain length and interaction energy.

**Main result.** The derived free energy expression shows excellent agreement with extensive Monte Carlo simulations across various regimes, allowing for the construction of a phase diagram for the coil-globule transition.

**Method.** The authors extend existing work using a cumulant expansion approach to derive the free energy, which is then compared against numerical simulation results via Bayesian inference.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is an Interacting Self-Avoiding Walk (ISAW) on a lattice, representing self-attracting linear homopolymers. The interactions are governed by monomer-monomer contact energy ($\epsilon$).

**Key observables.** Radius of gyration ($R$), the probability distribution $P(R\mid \varepsilon, N)$, and the mean radius of gyration $ar{R}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Chain length ($N$), monomer-monomer interaction energy ($\epsilon$), and the critical temperature/point ($\Theta$-point).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation relies on extending free energy calculations from non-interacting SAWs using cumulant expansions, assuming analyticity in certain density variables.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare theoretical predictions (inferred free energy parameters) and histograms of $P(R^2|\epsilon, N)$ against Monte Carlo simulation results across different solvent conditions ($\epsilon$) and chain lengths ($N$).

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theory by first establishing the SAW case, then extending it to ISAW using cumulant expansions. The core is the comparison of the resulting free energy expression with extensive MC simulations to validate the model and map out the phase diagram.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We aim at calculating an explicit expression for the finite-size probability distribution of the radius of gyration $R$ of an Interacting Self-Avoiding Walk (ISAW), as a function of chain length $N$ and monomer-monomer interaction energy $\varepsilon$. We first derive the explicit free energy expression for a non-interacting Self-Avoiding Walk, introducing a new natural scale variable $t = ρ^g$ expressed as a power of the density $ρ$. Then, thanks to a cumulant expansion approach introduced by Lhuillier, Victor and coworkers, we extend it to the interacting case, capturing both the coil-globule transition and finite-size corrections to scaling, including entropic and surface-energy contributions. The radius of gyration distribution determined by the new free energy expression is then compared, using Bayesian inference to estimate the model parameters, to results from extensive Monte Carlo simulations of three-dimensional ISAWs, showing excellent agreement except very close to the $Θ$-point, where finite chain lengths limit the accessible scaling regime. This validates our resulting free energy expression and allows us, as an application, to construct a phase diagram of the coil-globule transition.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a constructive algorithm to find the Kraus decomposition for completely positive maps acting on infinite-dimensional quantum systems. By employing direct process tomography and leveraging strong convergence arguments, it overcomes limitations of previous non-constructive proofs. This provides a powerful, systematic tool for analyzing open quantum evolution in complex physical settings.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous, algorithmic approach to decomposing quantum operations in infinite dimensions, which is crucial for modeling open quantum systems where continuous variables or infinite degrees of freedom are involved.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to develop a constructive algorithm for finding the Kraus decomposition of a completely positive (CP) operator acting on separable, countably-infinite-dimensional Hilbert spaces.

**Main result.** The authors present an elementary and practical algorithm that successfully constructs the Kraus operators and proves that their sum converges in the strong-operator topology (SOT).

**Method.** The method combines direct process tomography with iterative reduction steps, systematically generating Kraus operators one by one while ensuring convergence properties.

**Model / system.** The system involves quantum channels or CP maps ($\Lambda$) acting between separable Hilbert spaces ($H$ and $K$). The decomposition seeks to express $\Lambda$ as a sum of processes generated by Kraus operators: $\Lambda = \sum_{i=1}^{\infty} \Theta(A_i)$.

**Key observables.** The CP map ($\Lambda$), the resulting Kraus operators ($A_i$), and the convergence of the partial sums in SOT.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Separable (countably-infinite-dimensional) Hilbert spaces, orthonormal bases for $H$ and $K$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The proof relies on extending finite-dimensional results using density arguments involving nested projections ($P_\sigma$) and convergence in the trace norm ($\|\cdot\|_{1,1}$).

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from establishing necessary tools (like the quasi-Cauchy-Schwarz inequality) to first solve the problem for finite dimensions before extending the constructive algorithm and proof of convergence to the infinite-dimensional case.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

An algorithm is presented for Kraus decomposition of a completely positive operator over separable (countably-infinite-dimensional) Hilbert spaces, together with an elementary proof that the generated sum convergences in strong-operator topology. This improves on the standard, nonconstructive, proof by fusing the abstract problem with practical process tomography. Kraus operators are generated one-by-one, each having one more guaranteed zero matrix entry than the previous one. In this way, the stream of outputs of the algorithm provides a coherent family of Kraus decompositions of restrictions of the target CP map to ever-larger subspaces.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work develops an analytic framework to understand 'spread complexity,' a measure used to probe quantum chaos. By analyzing the structure of the Krylov kernel in energy space, the authors derive a formula linking this complexity directly to the Fourier transforms of nearest-neighbor level spacing distributions. This successfully explains the spectral origin of the characteristic peak observed in chaotic systems while correctly predicting the monotonic behavior in integrable limits.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection drawn between dynamical measures like complexity and fundamental spectral statistics provides deep insight into quantum chaos, which is relevant for understanding energy level spacing in complex quantum systems studied in AMO physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to resolve the microscopic spectral origin of the characteristic finite-time peak observed in 'spread complexity,' a key diagnostic tool for quantum chaos.

**Main result.** It derives an analytic expression for spread complexity based on local spectral statistics, successfully describing both chaotic random-matrix ensembles and the integrable Poisson limit. This framework directly connects Krylov dynamics to spectral properties.

**Method.** The authors develop an analytic framework by analyzing the energy-space representation of the Krylov kernel, exploiting its approximate banded structure to relate complexity to Fourier transforms of nearest-neighbor spacing distributions.

**Model / system.** The analysis concerns time evolution governed by a time-independent Hermitian Hamiltonian H on a quantum many-body system. The core mathematical tool is the Krylov kernel derived from this energy spectrum.

**Key observables.** Spread complexity, $C(t)$; finite-time peak structure; local spectral statistics (nearest-neighbor spacing distributions).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis considers regimes ranging from chaotic random-matrix ensembles to the integrable Poisson limit. The large-$D$ limit is frequently employed.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Key assumptions include the approximate banded nature of the Krylov kernel and utilizing an 'approximate kernel-universality hypothesis' relating it to a uniform lattice.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the convergence of complexity approximations using successive diagonals of the kernel, comparing exact results against truncated series expansions for both chaotic and Poisson regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the energy-space representation of spread complexity; it then uses the banded structure to derive a master formula linking complexity to spectral statistics; finally, it applies this framework to analyze known limits (chaotic vs. integrable) to pinpoint the origin of the peak.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Spread complexity has emerged as a useful probe of quantum chaos, yet the microscopic spectral origin of its characteristic finite-time peak remains incompletely understood. We develop an analytic framework that relates spread complexity directly to local spectral statistics. Starting from an energy-space representation of the Krylov kernel, we show that the kernel is approximately banded, leading to a rapidly convergent diagonal expansion dominated by nearby levels in the ordered spectrum. Motivated by this structure, we propose an approximate kernel-universality hypothesis: after unfolding, the Krylov kernel is well approximated by that of a uniform lattice. Combining this universal kernel with local spectral statistics yields a simple analytic expression for spread complexity in terms of the Fourier transforms of the $k$-th nearest-neighbour spacing distributions. In particular, at leading order, the finite-time peak is controlled by the Fourier transform of the nearest-neighbour spacing distribution. The resulting framework describes both chaotic random-matrix ensembles and the integrable Poisson limit, identifies the spectral origin of the complexity peak and its late-time behaviour, and provides a direct connection between Krylov dynamics and spectral statistics.

</details>

**Summary.** This work theoretically predicts an emergent magnetic pseudogap in 2D superconductors arising purely from phase fluctuations. By analyzing the NMR relaxation rate (1/T1T), the authors show that the full temperature dependence is governed by a hierarchy of competing length scales: BKT correlations, BCS coherence, and background scattering. This provides a unified theoretical framework for understanding normal-state superconducting signatures.

**Why it may be interesting.** The detailed analysis of spectral function divergences (like the Hebel-Slichter peak) and their regularization via scattering lengths provides a rich theoretical playground for understanding how quantum fluctuations modify fundamental superconducting signatures, relevant to strongly correlated systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To theoretically identify an emergent magnetic pseudogap in two-dimensional superconductors arising purely from phase fluctuations, independent of invoking competing ordered states.

**Main result.** The paper establishes a unified picture where the normal-state spin relaxation rate (1/T1T) evolves based on the interplay between three characteristic length scales: the BKT correlation length ($\xi(T)$), the BCS coherence length ($\xi_{BCS}$), and an external scattering length ($\ell$).

**Method.** The analysis uses perturbation theory applied to the NMR relaxation rate (1/T1T), calculating contributions from both bubble diagrams and leading-order vertex corrections.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on 2D superconductors, modeled phenomenologically with a Hamiltonian incorporating spatially fluctuating superconducting order parameters ($\Delta(r, s)$). Comparisons are made between d-wave and s-wave pairing symmetries.

**Key observables.** NMR relaxation rate (1/T1T), charge pseudogap scale, magnetic pseudogap scale ($T_{mPG}$), and the coherence enhancement peak near $T_c$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** BKT correlation length $\xi(T)$, BCS coherence length $\xi_{BCS}$, background scattering length $\ell$, superconducting gap ($\Delta_{SC}$), and transition temperature ($T_c$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the validity of treating static phase fluctuations near the BKT point, allowing for a perturbative expansion in terms of weak pairing.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate how characteristic scales like $\xi(T_{mPG})$ and $\xi(T_{coh})$ are determined by analyzing the derivatives or maxima of the calculated 1/T1T contributions with respect to varying length scale ratios.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops a theoretical framework for phase fluctuations, calculates the leading-order corrections to $1/T_1T$ (bubble and vertex), distinguishes behavior between d-wave and s-wave systems, and finally unifies these results by showing how all scales ($\xi(T)$, $\xi_{BCS}$, $\ell$) govern the full temperature evolution of $1/T_1T$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Preformed pairs and phase fluctuations are believed to play a vital role in predicting the charge pseudogap in the normal state of two-dimensional superconductors. In this work, we extend this idea and further identify the emergent magnetic pseudogap from pure phase fluctuations without invoking any competing order. We examine the NMR relaxation rate $1/T_1T$ by evaluating the bubble contribution and leading-order vertex correction within perturbation theory. It is found that the magnetic pseudogap, manifesting as a smooth suppression of $1/T_1T$ in the normal state, is characterized by a temperature scale $T_\text{mPG}$ distinct from the superconducting gap $Δ_\text{SC}$ and transition temperature $T_c$. The onset scales of both charge and magnetic pseudogap are dominated by the competition of BKT correlation length $ξ(T)$ and BCS coherence length $ξ_\text{BCS}$. Moreover, the vertex correction is shown to be irrelevant for $d$-wave pairing, while it becomes prominent in $s$-wave systems and drives a coherent enhancement of $1/T_1T$ at lower temperatures just above $T_c$. We attribute this normal-state enhancement of $1/T_1T$ to the diverging coherence peak at the $s$-wave superconducting gap edge, which shares the same spirit as the celebrated Hebel-Slichter peak in the BCS theory. Analogous to the coherent Hebel-Slichter peak, regularization by Fermi-liquid-like scatterings is important and is characterized by a scattering length $\ell$. The normal-state coherent enhancement of $1/T_1T$ is hence described by the competition of $ξ(T)$ and $\ell$, through which the coherence scale $T_\text{coh}$ is determined. As a result, the complete evolution of $1/T_1T$ is understood quantitatively in a unified picture as the interplay among hierarchy of scales $ξ(T)$, $ξ_\text{BCS}$ and $\ell$.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper outlines a quantum computing approach to solve the intractable problem of determining the Equation of State (EoS) for matter inside neutron stars. By developing a theoretical framework based on QCD Hamiltonian simulation, they show how future quantum computers could drastically reduce uncertainties in key astrophysical observables like stellar radius and tidal deformability. This bridges high-energy nuclear theory with advanced quantum computation techniques.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on nuclear astrophysics, the core methodology involves mapping a complex field theory Hamiltonian onto a qubit register for variational energy minimization, which is highly relevant to simulating strongly correlated many-body systems in general.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is determining the Equation of State (EoS) for ultra-dense nuclear matter, specifically within neutron stars, which is intractable using standard Lattice QCD due to the sign problem.

**Main result.** The authors propose that future quantum computers can constrain the EoS by simulating QCD at finite chemical potential, leading to significantly reduced uncertainties in key observables like radius and tidal deformability.

**Method.** They develop a theoretical framework for solving the canonical Hamiltonian of QCD in Weyl gauge on a quantum computer architecture, using variational minimization techniques.

**Model / system.** The system is Quantum Chromodynamics (QCD) applied to neutron star cores. The theory focuses on developing the Hamiltonian formalism and encoding quark-gluon states onto qubit registers for simulation.

**Key observables.** Static observables of neutron stars: mass, radius ($R$), and Tidal deformability ($\Lambda$). Derived quantities include energy density ($ho$) and pressure ($p$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** High densities (intermediate regimes) where classical QCD fails; the coupling constant $g$; specific quark/gluon configurations (e.g., 3 quarks, 1 gluon).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The work is a proof-of-concept demonstration limited to small particle numbers (e.g., three quarks and one gluon) due to current computational constraints.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the narrowing of uncertainty bands for EoS observables when constrained by quantum computation, comparing theoretical predictions with existing bounds.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying the physical problem (EoS difficulty) to developing the necessary Hamiltonian theory in canonical gauge, detailing the quantum encoding and minimization procedure, and finally demonstrating the method via simulation results.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Equation of State (EoS) of Nuclear Matter at high densities, and particularly that of neutron stars, resists $\mathit{ab}$ $\mathit{initio}$ Quantum Chromodynamics (QCD) computations due to the notorious sign problem of Lattice Gauge Theory at finite chemical potential. A quantum computer deploying QCD in canonical quantization should be able to make substantial progress. We set some basic goals for a future quantum computer to predict the EoS, and thus the basic static observables of the star (mass, radius and Tidal deformability, for example). We then develop the basic theory to address the canonical Hamiltonian in Weyl (time-axial) gauge expressed in normal modes, together with the squared Gauss operator $\mathcal{G}^2$ necessary to execute energy minimization algorithms restricted to the physical Fock subspace. Finally, we deploy our particle-quantum register encoding of a generic field theory to demonstrate QCD at finite chemical potential for a few (three-four) particles with a modest number of momentum modes, by simulating the quantum computer on a classical cluster. This opens the possibility for effective quantum computers to constrain the microscopic physics of neutron stars simultaneously to the operation of third--generation gravitational wave detectors such as the Einstein Telescope, providing more detailed predictions than has been possible until now.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a hybrid framework for Quantum MIMO communication by modeling the channel with parameterized unitary transformations. It introduces a Variational Quantum Circuit receiver trained to decode transmitted information under realistic noise. The findings suggest VQCs provide robust performance stability in complex, strongly coupled quantum channels.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete, trainable quantum circuit approach (VQC) to solve an information processing task (MIMO equalization) that is highly relevant for near-term quantum hardware applications and understanding resource scaling.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates a quantum extension of classical Multiple-Input Multiple-Output (MIMO) communication, replacing linear channels with parameterized multi-qubit unitary transformations.

**Main result.** Variational Quantum Circuits (VQC) offer increased performance stability compared to classical detectors when channel complexity and interference are high, suggesting they are complementary rather than direct replacements.

**Method.** A Variational Quantum Circuit receiver is trained via supervised variational optimization to approximate the inverse quantum channel transformation under realistic noisy conditions.

**Model / system.** The system models quantum MIMO communication using an n-qubit Hilbert space where interference arises from coherent unitary evolution, $U_{ch}(\Theta)$.

**Key observables.** Bit-error rates (BER), Mutual Information, and resource requirements growth trends.

**Important parameters / regimes.** NISQ noise parameters (depolarizing noise, thermal relaxation) and system dimension ($n$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is performed under realistic CPTP noise modeling in quantum simulations; no claim of asymptotic advantage is made.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the circuit representation of the proposed quantum MIMO channel for $n=4$, involving Hadamard layers and nearest-neighbor entangling operations.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem gap by noting previous work focused on capacity limits, then details the hybrid framework combining parameterized unitary channels with VQC equalization, followed by performance evaluation under noise.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

This paper investigates a quantum extension of classical Multiple-Input Multiple-Output (MIMO) communication in which the conventional linear channel model is replaced by a parameterized multi-qubit unitary transformation. Within this framework, interference is represented through coherent quantum interactions rather than additive signal coupling. To recover transmitted information, a Variational Quantum Circuit (VQC) receiver is introduced that learns an approximate inverse channel transformation through supervised variational optimization. The proposed system is evaluated under realistic noisy intermediate-scale quantum (NISQ) conditions incorporating depolarizing noise, thermal relaxation, and measurement imperfections, and its performance is compared with that of standard classical detection methods. The results reveal a trade-off between the two approaches: classical detectors achieve substantially lower bit-error rates across much of the investigated parameter range but exhibit pronounced performance degradation for specific channel configurations, whereas the VQC receiver maintains a more uniform error profile as channel complexity increases, albeit at a higher average BER. These findings suggest that variational quantum receivers are not a direct replacement for classical detection methods, but rather a complementary approach that may offer increased performance stability in communication scenarios characterized by strong coupling and complex interference patterns.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates large magnetoresistance in layered Pd-based metals that possess complex electronic structures beyond simple semimetal models. By combining theory and experiment, the authors show that MR scales universally with carrier mobility, confirming Kohler's rule even in multiband systems. The study highlights how crystal symmetry influences this scaling, suggesting a new understanding of transport governed by band topology.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter transport, the concept of emergent single-parameter scaling governed by mobility in complex band structures provides a generalizable framework applicable to understanding quantum coherence or dissipation mechanisms in other strongly interacting systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To investigate universal magnetoresistance (MR) scaling in layered Pd-based multiband metals that operate beyond the simple compensated semimetal regime.

**Main result.** The MR scaling is governed by carrier mobility, exhibiting a systematic single-parameter dependence on field and RRR, establishing a new class of large MR in complex multiband metals. However, noncentrosymmetric systems show reduced MR compared to centrosymmetric ones at equal RRR due to spin-orbit coupling effects.

**Method.** The study combines first-principles electronic structure calculations (including SOC) with systematic experimental measurements of magnetotransport properties on single crystals.

**Model / system.** The physical systems are nonmagnetic, layered Pd-based metals ($	ext{PdTe}_2$, $	ext{PdPb}_2$, $eta	ext{-PdBi}_2$, and $\alpha	ext{-PdBi}$), which are characterized as high-carrier-density multiband conductors with complex Fermi surfaces.

**Key observables.** Magnetoresistance Ratio (MR), dependence on magnetic field ($H$), temperature ($T$), and Residual Resistivity Ratio ($	ext{RRR}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Carrier mobility, effective scattering time, RRR, and the degree of carrier compensation.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the observed scaling is governed by intrinsic electronic structure effects (mobility) rather than solely by disorder-induced impurity scattering mechanisms.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate crystal structures, calculated band/Fermi surfaces, and plots showing systematic correlations between MR magnitude and RRR across different materials.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically compares magnetotransport data across multiple PdX$_2$ compounds, using first-principles calculations to interpret the underlying electronic structure responsible for the observed universal scaling laws of the magnetoresistance.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We systematically investigated the magnetoresistance ratio (MR) of single-crystalline, nonmagnetic layered Pd-based metals, including centrosymmetric PdTe2, PdPb2, and beta-PdBi2 and noncentrosymmetric alpha-PdBi. Our study identifies a distinct class of large MR in multiband, high-carrier-density systems. Unlike well-studied extremely large MR materials, such as Dirac and Weyl semimetals described by simple compensated-carrier models, these compounds possess complex Fermi surfaces, as validated by our first-principles calculations. Nevertheless, they exhibit a remarkably simple MR scaling governed by carrier mobility, manifested in systematic dependencies on magnetic field, temperature, and the residual resistivity ratio (RRR). The validity of Kohler's rule in high-RRR crystals indicates that MR is governed by a single effective scattering time, even in these multiband systems. The field and RRR dependences of MR follow an intermediate power-law behavior between linear and quadratic, attributable to imperfect carrier compensation and a distribution of carrier mobilities. Among the studied compounds, alpha-PdBi exhibits the largest MR, reaching 1500% (2 K, 7 T), owing to its exceptionally high RRR (approximately 660). However, when compared on an equal-RRR basis, its MR is smaller than that of its centrosymmetric counterparts. This trend suggests that additional scattering channels arising from spin-orbit-induced band splitting in noncentrosymmetric systems reduce the effective carrier mobility. Our results establish a new class of large MR in clean multiband metals where complex electronic structures give rise to emergent single-parameter scaling, highlighting the interplay between disorder, mobility, and symmetry.

</details>

**Authors:** Chien-Tung Kuo, Felix Burt, Samuel Yen-Chi Chen, Kin K. Leung, Kuan-Cheng Chen  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.06892v1>
