**Summary.** This paper presents an RL framework to solve the complex problem of compiling quantum circuits across multiple, interconnected QPUs. It models this as an MDP, using compiler actions like 'Split' and 'Merge' to manage qubit placement dynamically. The approach shows promise for structured tasks but highlights that dense workloads still pose significant challenges.

**Why it may be interesting.** It tackles a critical resource management issue in scaling quantum computation—the communication bottleneck—by applying advanced AI techniques to hardware-aware scheduling.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is optimizing distributed quantum circuit compilation, which requires scheduling operations while minimizing expensive inter-QPU communication overhead (EPR-pair consumption) and latency.

**Main result.** The proposed reinforcement learning framework matches state-of-the-art heuristics on structured workloads but struggles with highly dense or unstructured circuits, indicating scalability challenges.

**Method.** The problem is formulated as a constrained Markov Decision Process (MDP), solved using an architecture-aware reinforcement learning policy trained via Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO).

**Model / system.** The system involves multiple cooperating Quantum Processing Units (QPUs) connected by communication links, modeling the compilation process over a heterogeneous graph structure.

**Key observables.** EPR-pair consumption (communication volume), communication makespan (latency), and circuit progress.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Qubit placement ($	ext{Loct}(q)$), QPU topology ($H=(P, E)$), and resource capacities.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The RL framework abstracts compiler-level actions like 'Split' and 'Merge'; scalability remains a key bottleneck for practical use.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the Split/Merge communication primitives and the overall pipeline of the proposed RL framework, showing state building through to reward calculation.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem as an MDP, details the heterogeneous graph RL framework (State Builder $	o$ Policy $	o$ Transition Engine), compares performance against established heuristics on various benchmarks (QAOA, QFT, Random, QV), and discusses limitations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Distributed quantum computing provides a scalable route for executing quantum circuits beyond the capacity limits of a single quantum processing unit (QPU), but it introduces a communication-aware compilation problem involving strict hardware constraints and circuit dependencies. This paper presents an architecture-aware reinforcement-learning framework that formulates distributed quantum compilation as a constrained Markov Decision Process (MDP). The compiler-level communication actions dynamically update logical-qubit placement and enable subsequent gate execution. A heterogeneous graph model represents interactions among hardware, logical qubits, and circuit operations, while a policy trained via Proximal Policy Optimization optimizes EPR-pair consumption and communication makespan. Evaluation across benchmark circuits shows that our policy matches state-of-the-art heuristics on structured workloads, with lookahead reward shaping yielding modest improvements on unstructured circuits. These results demonstrate that reinforcement learning is a flexible alternative to manual heuristics, though scalability remains a key bottleneck for practical use.

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces an advanced machine learning framework to overcome the computational bottleneck of calculating vacancy formation energies in complex High-Entropy Alloys. By incorporating both local chemical disorder and mechanical strain into a unified, permutation invariant model, it achieves predictions orders of magnitude faster than atomistic simulations while accurately capturing site-specific defect behavior.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter defects rather than quantum information, the core methodology—using permutation invariant neural networks (Deep Sets) to encode complex local structural/chemical environments—is highly relevant for developing ML potentials or predictive models in quantum materials simulation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Vacancy formation energies in High-Entropy Alloys (HEAs) are crucial for understanding material failure, but their dependence on local chemical environments and mechanical deformation makes atomistic calculation prohibitively expensive.

**Main result.** The proposed ML framework accurately predicts strain-dependent vacancy formation energies orders of magnitude faster than direct simulations, confirming that local chemistry dictates defect energetics beyond simple averaging.

**Method.** A permutation invariant machine learning framework (Deep Sets architecture) is developed to map local atomic environments and deformation gradients to vacancy formation energy.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on Face-Centered Cubic (FCC) High-Entropy Alloys (HEAs), modeling the interaction between vacancies, local chemical disorder, and applied mechanical strain/stress.

**Key observables.** Vacancy formation energies ($	ext{E}_{	ext{form}}$), site-to-site variability of $	ext{E}_{	ext{form}}$, and dependence on volumetric vs. shear deformation.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Local chemical environment, applied strain (tensile/compressive), and the specific composition of the HEA.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework assumes that local geometric descriptors and invariants of the deformation gradient are sufficient to capture the complex coupling between chemistry and mechanics.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates an atomic supercell of FCC CoCrFeNi HEA showing a vacancy. Figure 2 likely shows the variation of average $	ext{E}_{	ext{v}}^{	ext{f}}$ under different strain states.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the physical problem (costly atomistic calculation of defect energies in HEAs) and then introduces the ML framework, detailing the use of objective geometric descriptors and deformation invariants to learn the coupled effects. Results confirm the model's accuracy and efficiency compared to direct simulations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Vacancy formation energies govern diffusion, irradiation damage, phase stability, and dynamic failure in high-entropy alloys (HEAs), yet their strong dependence on local chemical environments and mechanical deformation makes atomistic calculations prohibitively expensive for large-scale studies. Here, we develop an atomistically informed permutation invariant machine learning framework for predicting strain dependent vacancy formation energies in FCC HEAs from local atomic environments. The model employs a vacancy-centered representation constructed from objective geometric descriptors together with invariants of the local deformation gradient, enabling the coupled effects of chemical disorder and finite deformation to be learned within a unified framework. Atomistic simulations reveal that volumetric deformation is the dominant factor controlling the average variation in vacancy formation energy, whereas shear deformation has a comparatively minor influence. At the same time, substantial site to site variability persists under identical macroscopic loading, demonstrating that local chemical environments govern the statistical distribution of vacancy energetics beyond species-averaged trends. The proposed framework accurately predicts vacancy formation energies across diverse deformation states while providing orders-of-magnitude faster evaluation than direct atomistic simulations. These results establish an efficient route for incorporating stress-dependent defect energetics into multiscale models of diffusion, irradiation damage, and dynamic failure in chemically complex alloys.

</details>

**Summary.** The research reports on using time-resolved resonant X-ray scattering ($	ext{tr-RXS}$) to overcome the challenge of resolving sublattice-specific phonon motions in charge density wave materials. By exploiting element selectivity via tunable X-ray energies, the authors successfully decoupled and identified three distinct coherent phonon modes in $	ext{EuTe}_4$, assigning them definitively to either Te or Eu lattice vibrations.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work demonstrates an advanced spectroscopic technique ($	ext{tr-RXS}$) capable of providing element-specific structural dynamics information in correlated materials, which is highly relevant for understanding lattice coupling mechanisms central to many quantum phenomena.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is experimentally resolving sublattice-specific phonon motions within multi-element materials that exhibit charge density waves (CDWs).

**Main result.** The authors successfully disentangled three distinct coherent phonon modes ($\sim 0.85, 1.3, 	ext{ and } 1.6 	ext{ THz}$) in $	ext{EuTe}_4$, assigning them to specific Eu- or Te-dominated lattice dynamics.

**Method.** They utilized time-resolved resonant X-ray scattering ($	ext{tr-RXS}$), a time-domain approach, which surpasses the energy resolution limits of conventional inelastic scattering techniques.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on the CDW material $	ext{EuTe}_4$, which has alternating Te monolayers and EuTe slabs. The analysis models the measured intensity as a weighted sum of contributions from the two distinct sublattices (Te and Eu).

**Key observables.** Coherent phonon frequencies ($\sim 0.85, 1.3, 	ext{ and } 1.6 	ext{ THz}$), element-resolved lattice dynamics, and CDW diffraction intensity oscillations.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The technique relies on tuning the X-ray probe energy near specific elemental absorption edges (e.g., Eu $M_{5,4}$ edges) to achieve elemental selectivity.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the measured signal can be accurately modeled as a linear combination of independent sublattice contributions whose weights depend strongly on the probe energy.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the $	ext{tr-RXS}$ experimental setup, showing time evolution and FFTs of CDW intensity oscillations at different probe energies. Subsequent figures detail the quantitative decoupling process using the two-sublattice model to assign specific phonon modes to Eu or Te motion.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem (lack of sublattice resolution), details the $	ext{tr-RXS}$ methodology, presents time-domain measurements showing oscillations, and finally uses FFT analysis combined with a multi-energy fitting model to decouple and assign specific phonon modes to their respective sublattices.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Phonons govern fundamental material properties and play a central role in various electronic phase transitions. Coherent driving of specific phonon modes enables on-demand phase control, motivating sublattice-resolved identification of real-space phonon motions. Yet experimentally resolving these motions remains challenging, limiting precise phonon-based control. Here, we introduce a dynamical protocol to track element-resolved phonon dynamics in the charge density wave material EuTe4, in which the dominant Te-sublattice charge order is accompanied by a previously unreported Eu-sublattice component. We leverage the elemental selectivity of time-resolved resonant X-ray scattering to reveal three coherent phonon modes with distinct sublattice character, thereby disentangling Eu- and Te-dominated lattice dynamics, in good agreement with theoretical calculations of the phonon eigenvectors. This time-domain approach, which surpasses the energy-resolution limits of conventional frequency-domain inelastic scattering, provides a broadly applicable framework for decomposing coherent phonons in multi-element materials, which is crucial for the targeted control of phases of matter.

</details>

### [An Exploratory Evaluation of LLM-Assisted Rewriting of Moderate-Complexity Financial Sentences for DisCoCat-Based Sentiment Analysis](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07439v1)

**Authors:** Brian Llinas, Nikos Chrisochoides  
**Type:** both · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.07439v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

**Summary.** This paper proposes using Large Language Models (LLMs) to rewrite complex financial sentences into simplified forms suitable for DisCoCat-based sentiment analysis within a Quantum NLP framework. The method successfully reduces the computational complexity of the resulting quantum circuits while maintaining high classification accuracy, demonstrating a potential pathway for applying quantum models to real-world language tasks.

**Why it may be interesting.** While not focused on core quantum hardware physics, the work explores using advanced AI (LLMs) to preprocess inputs for quantum algorithms, which is relevant for developing practical applications in quantum machine learning.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses practical limitations of using DisCoCat for financial sentiment analysis when processing complex, long sentences due to issues like parser sensitivity and high computational cost.

**Main result.** LLM-assisted rewriting successfully compresses moderate-complexity inputs into circuit-efficient variants, achieving higher mean accuracy (0.550) than the low-complexity baseline using GPT-4.1-mini with Prompt B.

**Method.** A novel preprocessing workflow is proposed where Large Language Models (LLMs) are used to rewrite and simplify complex financial sentences into forms compatible with the DisCoCat framework, which maps grammar to quantum circuits.

**Model / system.** The core model is Quantum Natural Language Processing (QNLP) utilizing Distributional Compositional Categorical (DisCoCat). The computational object derived from the text structure is a parameterized quantum circuit.

**Key observables.** Mean classification accuracy, average qubit and gate counts on the resulting quantum circuits, and linguistic complexity metrics.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Prompting strategies (e.g., Prompt B), specific LLMs (GPT-4.1-mini), and filtering configurations are key parameters being compared.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The study assumes that controlled rewriting can preserve the sentiment-bearing meaning of the original complex text while reducing computational overhead for quantum circuit mapping.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the pipeline, showing how a parser-derived diagram (Figure 1) from a sentence like 'Amazon profits soar' is reduced and mapped to a parameterized ansatz quantum circuit (Figure 2).

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem limitations of DisCoCat for complex text, details the LLM-assisted rewriting workflow involving multiple prompting strategies, presents quantitative comparisons against a low-complexity baseline, and concludes with an assessment of the feasibility.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum natural language processing (QNLP) provides a grammar-aware framework for text modeling, and Distributional Compositional Categorical (DisCoCat) is one of its theoretically grounded formulations. Prior work on financial sentiment analysis has identified practical limitations of DisCoCat, including parser sensitivity, high simulation cost, and difficulty handling longer sentences. We study an LLM-assisted preprocessing workflow that uses controlled rewriting to compress, simplify, or decompose moderate-complexity financial sentiment sentences into parser-compatible, circuit-efficient variants while preserving sentiment-bearing meaning. We compare prompting strategies, language models, and filtering configurations with the low-complexity-only DisCoCat baseline of Stein et al. At the circuit level, the strongest compression variants reduce average qubit and gate counts by more than 70 percent relative to the raw moderate-complexity subset. Across repeated training runs, GPT-4.1-mini with Prompt B achieves the highest observed mean accuracy, $0.550 \pm 0.035$, compared with $0.521 \pm 0.050$ for the baseline. Larger training splits do not necessarily improve downstream performance; across evaluated configurations, training-split size has a moderately negative association with accuracy (Pearson $r=-0.446$). These results provide exploratory evidence that LLM-assisted rewriting can make some moderate-complexity inputs usable within the evaluated DisCoCat configuration, while highlighting prompt design, filtering, and circuit-aware preprocessing as considerations for more scalable QNLP-based financial sentiment analysis.

</details>

### [Conditions for Quantum Advantage in AC Power Flow](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06711v1)

**Authors:** Parikshit Pareek, Abhijith Jayakumar, Carleton Coffrin, Sidhant Misra  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.06711v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>

<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 1.</b> Fig. 1. Condition number (κ) variations for the PGLib [13] power transmis- sion network datasets reveal potential numerical challenges in solving ACPF. Left: Variation in the Jacobian condition number in converging instances for medium-scale systems. It is clear that the condition number increases with each iteration before converging to the final value. Right: Variation in the Jacobian condition number across 20 NRLF iterations for large-size systems in non-converging instances. It shows that the Jacobian condition number varies significantly between iterations and increases rapidly for non- converging instances, thus necessitating use of κ = maxi κi in (2). Note that, condition number of...</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically analyzes whether Quantum Computing can solve the Alternating Current Power Flow (ACPF) problem faster than classical methods. It derives complexity bounds for both quantum and classical solvers, concluding that while a lower bound exists for QC, practical quantum advantage is unlikely due to unfavorable scaling with required error tolerance.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the application is in power systems (not core condensed matter physics), the rigorous comparative analysis of algorithmic scaling ($O(\cdot)$ vs $\Omega(\cdot)$) based on system parameters ($\kappa, N, \epsilon$) provides a valuable theoretical framework for assessing quantum computational feasibility across diverse scientific domains.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the theoretical conditions under which Quantum Computing (QC) algorithms can achieve a demonstrable quantum advantage over classical methods when solving the Alternating Current Power Flow (ACPF) problem.

**Main result.** The analysis establishes an optimistic lower bound for any Quantum-ACPF solver as $\Omega(N\kappa/\epsilon)$, but subsequent comparison with classical complexity suggests that achieving a practical quantum advantage is highly challenging due to the $1/\epsilon$ dependence.

**Method.** The authors perform rigorous complexity benchmarking by deriving baseline runtime expressions for both classical (Newton-Raphson Load Flow) and theoretical gate-based QC solvers, comparing their scaling with system size ($N$), condition number ($\kappa$), and error tolerance ($\epsilon$).

**Model / system.** The physical model is the electrical energy system analyzed via the nonlinear ACPF equations. The computational task involves iteratively solving linear systems derived from the Jacobian matrix of this power flow problem.

**Key observables.** Quantum/Classical runtime complexity, Quantum-to-Classical cost ratio ($Q/C$), and the condition number ($\kappa$) of the Jacobian matrix.

**Important parameters / regimes.** System size $N$, Condition Number $\kappa$, Error Tolerance $\epsilon$ (measured in $\ell_2$-norm).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The quantum complexity analysis optimistically assumes access to Quantum Random Access Memory (QRAM) and that the total time is dominated by a lower bound of $\Omega(N\kappa/\epsilon)$.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 shows variations in the Jacobian condition number ($\kappa$) across different datasets. Figure 2 compares the complexity bounds: top panel shows the classical NRLF bound, while the bottom panel illustrates the derived quantum lower bound.

**Paper structure.** The paper first benchmarks QC solvers against the classical NRLF algorithm. It then derives a general baseline runtime complexity for gate-based QC algorithms ($\Omega(N\kappa/\epsilon)$). Finally, it compares this theoretical quantum cost to the established classical benchmark to determine feasibility of advantage.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

This paper aims to contextualize the requirements for Quantum Computing (QC) algorithms to achieve a quantum advantage in solving the alternating current power flow (ACPF) problem, with a focus on runtime complexity. First, we establish a benchmark for a QC iterative solver to demonstrate an advantage over the classical Newton-Raphson Load Flow (NRLF) algorithm. Next, we derive a baseline expression for the end-to-end runtime complexity of any Gate-based QC algorithm as $Ω(N κ/\varepsilon),$ reflecting dependence on system size $N$, condition number $κ$, and error tolerance $\varepsilon$. Finally, we highlight key areas where QC algorithms may offer potential benefits over NRLF in addressing the standard ACPF problem.

</details>

<a id="paper-2608.06777"></a>
### [Quantum-State Projectors on Grassmannian: Geometry, Holonomy, and Topology](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06777v1)

**Authors:** Shin-Ming Huang  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** strongly correlated electrons · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.06777v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>
<div class="paper-figures-scroll">
<figure class="paper-figure-card">
<img src="2608.06777_figures/2608.06777_fig1.jpg" alt="Figure 1">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 1.</b> FIG. 1. Triangulation of a six-point Bargmann invariant into four third-order invariants. Arrows indicate the orientation of each overlap product.</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** The paper introduces a rigorous geometric framework using the Grassmannian manifold to describe quantum subspaces defined by spectral projectors. It shows that physical observables like Berry curvature and topological invariants can be derived from the projector's tangent field ($\dd P$) in a way that is inherently gauge-invariant. This unifies local geometry, distance measures, and global topology for gapped electronic systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful geometric language (Grassmannian geometry) to analyze topological phases in condensed matter systems, directly linking band structure topology to fundamental concepts of differential geometry, which is highly relevant for understanding robust electronic properties.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a unified, gauge-invariant geometric framework for characterizing quantum subspaces defined by spectral projectors on the Grassmannian manifold.

**Main result.** The formalism successfully unifies local quantum geometry (metric/curvature), finite subspace distance (geodesics via principal angles), holonomy, and global topology (Chern characters) using only the projector map $P$.

**Method.** The analysis employs differential geometry on the Grassmannian $	ext{Gr}(k, N)$, utilizing the spectral projector $P$ as a map from parameter space ($\mathbf{k}$) to the manifold. Key tools include tangent-vector calculus and Lie algebra decomposition.

**Model / system.** The system involves an isolated group of $k$ bands within an $N$-level quantum system, whose band structure is parameterized by momentum $\mathbf{k}$ on a torus (Brillouin zone). The dynamics are governed by a smooth gapped Hamiltonian.

**Key observables.** Quantum metric tensor, Berry curvature, principal angles between subspaces, Wilson loop determinant, Chern characters, and the $\mathbb{Z}_2$ index.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of bands ($k$), total levels ($N$), and the spectral gap ensuring global smoothness of the projector $P$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The existence of a spectral gap is crucial to ensure that the spectral projector $P(k)$ remains globally smooth and periodic, circumventing issues related to gauge fixing.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the spectral projector on $	ext{Gr}(k, N)$, deriving the tangent field $\dd P$, establishing that its interband block yields both metric and curvature information. It then applies this to calculate geodesic distances and derives basis-independent formulas for topological invariants like Wilson loops.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

An isolated group of $k$ bands in an $N$-level quantum system defines a rank-$k$ spectral projector and hence a map into the complex Grassmannian $\mathrm{Gr}(k,N)$. For a smooth gapped Hamiltonian, this projector is globally smooth and periodic even when band topology obstructs a globally smooth periodic, or symmetry-compatible, Bloch frame. We take the globally defined differential $\dd P$ as the central object: it is the tangent field of the Grassmannian map, removes unphysical rotations within the selected subspace, and retains the physical interband transition. The interband block of this tangent data simultaneously determines the quantum metric and Berry curvature; the associated horizontal generator produces finite Grassmannian motion, while wedge products of $\dd P$ enter topological forms. We construct a shortest path between two projectors in the ambient Grassmannian and show that the singular values of its horizontal generator block are the principal angles between the endpoint subspaces. This construction provides a piecewise-geodesic interpretation of discrete geometric phases. In a separate development, we derive a basis-independent expression for the determinant of a multiband Wilson loop from traces of powers of an ordered projector product, without decomposing a degenerate band multiplet into individual bands. Finally, the same tangent-vector calculus organizes Chern characters, chiral winding numbers, and the time-reversal $\mathbb Z_2$ index. The resulting framework unifies local quantum geometry, finite subspace distance, holonomy, and topology while avoiding global gauge fixing.

</details>

**Authors:** Lucas Gretta, Malvika Raj Joshi  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.06703v1>

**Summary.** This paper tackles a fundamental problem in quantum circuit complexity by proving that approximating the Quantum Fourier Transform (QFT) to constant depth requires the Fanout operation. The main result establishes an equivalence between the implementability of QFT and Fanout, thereby providing a rigorous constraint on the resources needed for realizing algorithms like Shor's algorithm on current noisy hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly theoretical but directly impacts the feasibility assessment of quantum algorithms like Shor's on near-term, limited hardware (NISQ), which often struggle with resource overheads like fanout.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to resolve an open question regarding quantum circuit complexity by proving that approximating the Quantum Fourier Transform (QFT) in constant depth requires the Fanout operation.

**Main result.** It is proven that $	ext{QFT}_q \in 	ext{AC}^0$ if and only if $	ext{FANOUT}_n \in 	ext{AC}^0$, directly linking the feasibility of Shor's algorithm on NISQ devices to the availability of a Fanout gate.

**Method.** The proof uses a complexity reduction, constructing a state of 'non-negligible felinity' via the QFT gate to demonstrate that if $	ext{QFT}_q$ is implementable in constant depth, so must be $	ext{FANOUT}_n$.

**Model / system.** The analysis operates within quantum circuit complexity theory, focusing on the computational power of gates like $	ext{QFT}_q$ and $	ext{FANOUT}_n$ when restricted to constant circuit depth ($	ext{AC}^0$).

**Key observables.** Felinity ($F_n(ho)$), which is a diagnostic measure quantifying state overlap, and the fidelity of approximated gates.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The modulus $q$, the number of qubits $n = \lceil\log qceil$, and the circuit depth restriction ($	ext{AC}^0$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The proof relies on prior results concerning state preparation in $	ext{AC}^0$ and assumes a framework where fidelity bounds can establish computational equivalence.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the necessary conditions for $	ext{QFT}_q \in 	ext{AC}^0$ by proving the equivalence to $	ext{FANOUT}_n \in 	ext{AC}^0$, culminating in a specific construction for the $q=2^n$ case relevant to Shor's algorithm.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Shor's algorithm is a canonical quantum supremacy target whose core operation relies on the Quantum Fourier Transform (QFT). We resolve an open question of Fang, Fenner, Green, Homer and Zhang from 2006 by showing that approximating QFT in constant depth, for any $n$-qubit modulus, necessarily requires the $n$-qubit Fanout operation.   Formally, let $\mathsf{QFT}_q$ be the gate acting on $n = \lceil \log q \rceil$ qubits that computes the QFT under modulus $q$. It is known that any $n$-qubit $\mathsf{QFT}_q$ can be implemented in constant depth using $\mathsf{FANOUT}_n$, i.e. $\mathsf{QFT}_q \in \mathsf{QAC}^0_f$. We prove the converse by using a $\mathsf{QFT}_q$ gate to construct a state of ``non-negligible felinity". Consequently, $\mathsf{QFT}_q \in \mathsf{QAC}^0 \iff \mathsf{FANOUT}_n \in \mathsf{QAC^0}$.   In the case of $q = 2^n$, such as in Shor's, we approximate $\mathsf{FANOUT}_n$ using a single $\mathsf{QFT}_{2^n}$ gate and $O(1)$ two-qubit local gates, thus tying the feasibility of realizing Shor's algorithm with NISQ circuits to that of Fanout.

</details>

### [Anderson Orthogonality as Measurement Backaction in Coupled Quantum Dots](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06550v1)

**Authors:** Will Grant, Sarath Sankar, Elena Cornick, Vahid Movahed, Johann Drayne, Silvia Lüscher, Saeed Fallahi, Geoffrey C. Gardner, Michael J. Manfra, Eran Sela, Yigal Meir, Joshua Folk

**Summary.** The authors investigate how measurement itself can fundamentally alter quantum transport by inducing Anderson Orthogonality Catastrophe (AOC) backaction in coupled quantum dots. By measuring the detector's response as a function of coupling strength, they demonstrate that this many-body correlation acts as a tunable knob to modify tunneling rates. This work establishes a controllable mechanism for engineering non-trivial dynamics in solid-state quantum devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a clean, solid-state platform to observe fundamental many-body physics (AOC) in an open quantum system setting. The ability to tune this correlation via gate voltages makes it highly relevant for controlling quantum dynamics in nanoscale devices.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to quantify detector-induced many-body correlations, specifically the Anderson Orthogonality Catastrophe (AOC), as a controllable source of backaction on quantum tunneling dynamics.

**Main result.** The experiment successfully demonstrates that tuning the energy level of a coupled quantum dot detector can modulate the tunneling rates from ordinary resonant transport to regimes dominated by AOC-induced many-body correlations.

**Method.** The research combines mesoscopic electrical measurements (measuring current and occupation in charge stability diagrams) with theoretical analysis using derived exponents ($\alpha_{est}$) relating transport signatures to the AOC parameter.

**Model / system.** The system is a coupled quantum dot circuit where one dot acts as the measured system, weakly coupled to another dot detector. The detector is strongly coupled to reservoirs and is held near equilibrium, allowing measurement of its many-body response.

**Key observables.** Average occupation ($\langle N_d angle$), detector current ($I_m$), dot current ($I_d$), and the derived AOC exponent ($\alpha$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Detector energy level detuning ($\epsilon_m$), bias voltage ($V_d$), temperature ($T$), and the ratio of coupling strengths ($\lambda/\Gamma_m$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The validity of mapping average current to occupation is assumed, and theoretical derivations rely on approximations valid in specific low-bias or weak-coupling limits.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate charge stability diagrams showing simultaneous measurements ($I_d$ vs $I_m$), time-resolved data confirming AOC effects on tunneling rates, and plots demonstrating the extraction of $\alpha_{est}$ from various transport signatures.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the physical setup and mechanism (AOC backaction), presenting multiple measurement techniques ($\langle N_d angle$ vs. $I_m$), deriving quantitative estimates for the AOC exponent ($\alpha_{est}$) from these measurements, and finally testing the robustness of $\alpha_{est}$ against experimental variations like temperature and bias.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Measurement perturbs a quantum system by coupling it to external degrees of freedom, but detector backaction depends on the physical mechanism of measurement itself. In solid-state devices, detectors driven far from equilibrium to enable faster measurements produce backaction that can often be understood as classical noise. However, a strong measurement can also induce backaction from quantum many-body correlations in the detector that are intrinsic to the measurement, even without shot noise. Here, we probe this near-equilibrium backaction through the effect of a quantum-dot charge sensor on tunnelling between a second quantum dot and its reservoirs. The measurement realizes the Anderson Orthogonality Catastrophe (AOC): electrons in the detector leads reorganize in response to an abrupt change in local scattering potential, suppressing resonant tunnelling while enabling inelastic processes that exchange energy with the detector. Changing the detector energy level tunes the AOC backaction from negligible to dominant in the tunnelling dynamics. More broadly, these results establish detector-induced many-body correlations as a controllable influence on quantum dynamics.

</details>

### [Tunable g-Factors of Hybridized Orbitals in a Quantum Dot Molecule](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06647v1)

**Summary.** This work demonstrates the ability to electrically tune the electron and hole g-factors within a quantum dot molecule using magneto-photoluminescence spectroscopy. By observing distinct changes in $g_e$ at tunneling resonances, the authors confirm that electric fields provide precise control over spin properties. This capability opens new pathways for deterministically generating complex entangled photonic states.

**Why it may be interesting.** The direct electrical tuning of fundamental parameters like the g-factor in solid-state quantum systems provides a powerful platform for engineering qubit interactions and deterministic entanglement generation protocols relevant to quantum computing architectures.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Controlling the g-factors of orbital spin states in quantum dot molecules (QDMs) is essential for achieving high-fidelity generation of multi-photonic cluster states with higher-dimensional entanglement.

**Main result.** The electron g-factor shows a pronounced step-like change at the tunneling resonance, while the hole g-factor remains nearly constant, demonstrating electric-field control over trion g-factors.

**Method.** Polarization-resolved magneto-photoluminescence spectroscopy was used experimentally, and the results were quantitatively reproduced using an eight-band k.p model theoretically.

**Model / system.** The study investigates negatively charged trions (X-) in a single InGaAs QDM, which is composed of two vertically stacked quantum dots separated by about 10 nm, allowing for gate voltage tuning.

**Key observables.** Electron ($g_e$) and hole ($g_h$) g-factors measured as a function of gate voltage; photoluminescence spectra under magnetic fields.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Gate voltage (electric field), applied magnetic field strength, tunneling resonance voltages, and the trion charge state ($	ext{X}^-$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theoretical model relies on an eight-band k.p approach combined with Configuration Interaction for calculating trion states.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates voltage-dependent PL spectra showing multiple charge states, and a schematic depicts the trion level structure under an in-plane magnetic field (Voigt geometry).

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the necessity of g-factor control for quantum state generation. It presents experimental measurements on $	ext{X}^-$ in the QDM, detailing the voltage dependence of $g_e$ and $g_h$. Finally, it validates these findings using an eight-band k.p model to confirm electric-field tunability.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The ability to control the $g$-factors of orbital spin states in optically active quantum dot molecules (QDMs) is a prerequisite for the high-fidelity generation of multi-photonic cluster states with higher-dimensional entanglement structure. Protocols that rely on two coupled spins require knowledge of the $g$-factor and its dependence on external control parameters. Mismatches in the $g$-factor between tunnel-coupled dots introduce unwanted dephasing of coupled spin-states, making precise characterization and voltage control essential. Here, we measure the gate voltage dependence of the electron and hole $g$-factors of negatively charged trions $X^{-}$ in a single InGaAs QDM using polarization-resolved magneto-photoluminescence spectroscopy. The electron $g$-factor exhibits a pronounced step-like change at the tunneling resonance, shifting from $g_\mathrm{e} = -0.336\pm 0.008$ to $g_\mathrm{e} = -0.389\pm 0.003$, providing a direct spectroscopic fingerprint of molecular orbital formation and a shift of the wavefunction localization from the lower to the upper dot. In contrast, the hole $g$-factor remains nearly constant at $g_\mathrm{h} \approx 0.094 \pm 0.007$, exhibiting a weak modulation near the anticrossing voltages attributed to Coulomb-mediated deformation of the wavefunction by the tunneling electron. Our results are quantitatively reproduced by an eight-band $\mathbf{k}{\cdot}\mathbf{p}$ model, establishing electric-field control of the trion $g$-factors as a practical tool for independently tuning the Zeeman splitting of individual dots and opening new pathways towards the deterministic generation of two-dimensional photonic cluster states.

</details>

**Summary.** This work experimentally investigates how modifying the thermal interface conductance in bulk NbTi superconductors alters the temperature dependence of flux avalanches. By introducing silver into the sample interface, the researchers induced a unique non-monotonic behavior in the threshold field $H_{th}(T)$. This signature is identified as direct evidence for entering a transitional regime where dynamic heat removal plays a crucial role.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on superconductivity, the core concept of identifying a critical parameter ($h_c$) that dictates a qualitative phase/regime crossover based on thermal coupling is highly relevant to open quantum systems where environmental coupling drives non-equilibrium dynamics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the onset of a transitional flux-avalanche regime in bulk Type-II superconductors, determining how thermal boundary conductance controls the superconducting instability.

**Main result.** Incorporating silver into the thermal interface layer causes the threshold field $H_{th}(T)$ to exhibit non-monotonic behavior, including an interval where $	ext{dH}_{th}/	ext{dT} > 0$, which signals entry into this transitional regime.

**Method.** Measurements were performed using magneto-optical imaging (MOI) on bulk NbTi disks with interfaces modified by varying concentrations of silver powder.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a bulk Type-II superconductor (NbTi) undergoing thermomagnetic avalanches. The key control parameter is the thermal boundary conductance ($h$) at the sample interface, which dictates the transition between electromagnetically and thermally controlled regimes.

**Key observables.** $H_{th}(T)$ (threshold field vs. temperature), $	ext{dH}_{th}/	ext{dT}$ (slope of $H_{th}$), and avalanche morphology (fingers vs. fronts).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Critical thermal boundary conductance ($h_c$), silver loading concentration ($	ext{wt.}\%$ Ag), and the characteristic temperature $T^* \approx 6.1-6.4$ K.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the observed non-monotonicity is a direct experimental signature of dynamic heat removal becoming relevant during the instability process, distinguishing it from purely quasi-static predictions.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 shows $H_{th}(T)$ for various Ag concentrations, highlighting the positive slope region ($	ext{dH}_{th}/	ext{dT} > 0$) in silver-filled interfaces. Figure 2 relates this to avalanche morphology changes (fingers to fronts).

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the background of flux avalanche regimes, details the experimental modification using Ag-filled interfaces, presents the key results showing non-monotonic $H_{th}(T)$ behavior, and correlates this change with both the transition into the transitional regime and changes in avalanche morphology.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Thermomagnetic avalanches in type-II superconductors occur in two qualitatively different regimes, electromagnetically controlled in thin films and thermally limited in bulk samples, distinguished by the sign of the temperature derivative of the threshold field $H_\text{th}(T)$. The two regimes are separated by a critical thermal boundary conductance $h_c$, and a non-monotonic $H_\text{th}(T)$ has been predicted in the transitional region where the interface conductance $h$ approaches $h_c$. We approach this region in a bulk NbTi disk by raising the interface coupling above the pure-nonadecane baseline with a silver-filled interface layer. Whereas the pure interface gives a monotonically decreasing $H_\text{th}(T)$, the silver-filled interface produces a non-monotonic dependence not previously realized in a bulk superconductor: a temperature interval of positive slope, $dH_\text{th}/dT > 0$, terminating in a maximum at $T^* \approx 6.1$--$6.4$~K. The effect is reproduced for two independent silver-filled compositions; in a third, with the highest loading, the low-temperature decrease is absent altogether and $H_\text{th}(T)$ is flat up to the same $T^*$, the evolution expected for stronger coupling. The position of the maximum is set by the intrinsic properties of NbTi, independent of the silver content. The onset of the positive-slope interval coincides in temperature with a change of the avalanche morphology from narrow channeled fingers to broad fronts. The reversal of the sign of $dH_\text{th}/dT$ is a direct experimental signature of the onset of the transitional regime, in which heat removal during the instability becomes dynamically relevant.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates the superconductor-insulator transition in 2D films by analyzing how disorder affects superconducting properties. The authors show that while the finite-temperature transition follows BKT physics, the zero-temperature transition is governed by quantum phase fluctuations causing $J_s(0)$ to vanish rapidly. By comparing transport measurements with theory, they clarify the role of these fluctuations in driving the system into an insulating state.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a detailed experimental realization of a quantum critical point driven by fluctuations in an ordered state (superconductivity), which is highly relevant for understanding emergent quantum phases in disordered materials, linking concepts from localization theory and superconductivity.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the origin of the superconductor-insulator transition (SIT) in disordered two-dimensional superconducting films, specifically determining whether it is driven by bosonic or fermionic quantum fluctuations.

**Main result.** The finite-temperature transition remains BKT type near the SIT, but the zero-temperature superfluid stiffness ($J_s(0)$) vanishes rapidly while $T_{c0}$ stays finite, indicating that quantum phase fluctuations drive the zero-temperature transition.

**Method.** The authors combine experimental measurements of DC resistance ($R_\Box(T)$) and superfluid stiffness ($J_s(T)$) with theoretical fitting techniques (e.g., BKT analysis, Finkel'stein theory) to map out energy scales across varying disorder strengths.

**Model / system.** The system under study is disordered two-dimensional superconducting films (e.g., NbN), characterized by a complex order parameter $\Delta(\mathbf{r})$. The physics involves the competition between Anderson localization and superconductivity, analyzed through various theoretical regimes (BCS-like suppression to insulating phase).

**Key observables.** Sheet resistance $R_\Box(T)$, superfluid stiffness $J_s(T)$, mean-field transition temperature ($T_{c0}$), BKT transition temperature ($T_{BKT}$), and zero-temperature superfluid stiffness ($J_s(0)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Disorder strength ($W$), critical resistance ($R_c$), characteristic energy scales like activation energy ($E^*$) and quasiparticle excitation energy ($E_g$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on established theories like the BKT mechanism for finite-temperature transitions, while addressing discrepancies between mean-field predictions and experimental observations due to quantum fluctuations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate $R_\Box(T)$ vs. T measurements, $J_s(T)$ evolution across different disorder levels, phase diagrams in the $(T-R_N)$-plane showing transition regions, and plots comparing theoretical energy scales ($E^*$, $\mu$, $E_g$) versus disorder strength.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes transport measurements to extract key parameters like $T_{c0}$ (via inflection points) and $T_{BKT}$ (via fitting), compares these results across multiple disorder regimes, and uses the separation of energy scales ($J_s(0)$ vs. $T_{c0}$) to pinpoint the nature of the quantum phase transition.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Theory predicts the superconductor-to-insulator transition (SIT) to emerge from the competition between Anderson localization, which tends to localize single-particle wavefunctions, and superconductivity, which establishes long-range correlations in the superconducting order parameter. In two-dimensional (2D) superconducting films, the transition temperature $T_\text{c}$ at which resistance vanishes, $R_\Box(T_\text{BKT}){=}0$, is set by the Berezinskii-Kosterlitz-Thouless (BKT) mechanism and satisfies $T_\text{BKT}< T_{c0}$, where $T_{c0}$ is the mean-field transition temperature. In weakly disordered samples $T_\text{BKT}\lesssim T_{c0}$, whereas increasing disorder drives $T_\text{BKT}\ll T_{c0}$ near the SIT. Whether the finite-temperature transition retains its BKT character throughout this crossover remains an open question. Here, we investigate the evolution of both sheet resistance $R_\Box(T)$ and superfluid stiffness $J_s(T)$ over a wide range of disorder strength $W$. We establish that even near the SIT, the finite-temperature transition from the superconducting to the resistive state remains of BKT type. However, as disorder approaches the critical value, the zero temperature superfluid phase stiffness, $J_s(0)$, is found to vanish rapidly while $T_{c0}$ remains finite, which we attribute to quantum phase fluctuations as the drive for the zero-temperature transition. Three decades after its experimental discovery by Haviland, Liu, and Goldman, our measurements clarify the origin of the SIT in 2D films.

</details>

For convenience, we have introduced the dimensionless frequencies ¯ω(k) = [ω(k)−γB0ξ]/(γµ0MS) and ¯ω0(k) = [ω0(k) −γB0ξ]/(γµ0MS). The red arrows indicate the direction of...</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This theoretical work investigates spin-wave transport in ferromagnet/superconductor heterostructures, establishing a framework based on self-consistent field calculations. The key finding is that superconducting screening induces unconventional wave phenomena, such as negative refraction and flat dispersion contours. This positions FMI-SC systems as highly versatile platforms for temperature-tunable magnonic devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on magnonics, the concept of using superconducting screening to engineer wave propagation (e.g., achieving flat isofrequency contours) shares conceptual parallels with engineered metamaterials used in quantum optics or cavity QED for controlling light-matter interactions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study develops a general theoretical framework for spin-wave optics in Ferromagnet-Superconductor (FMI-SC) heterostructures to understand unconventional wave phenomena.

**Main result.** The superconducting screening leads to negative refraction and nearly straight isofrequency contours, enabling functionalities like perfect imaging and temperature-tunable magnonic devices.

**Method.** The authors derived self-consistent equations for the total magnetic field by coupling Maxwell's equations with London equations, solving for the spin-wave dispersion relation $\omega(k)$.

**Model / system.** The system is an FMI-SC heterostructure where spin waves propagate. The superconducting response modifies the effective magnetic field via Meissner screening currents.

**Key observables.** Negative phase/group-velocity refraction, reflection/transmission coefficients ($R_{ij}, T_{ij}$), and temperature dependence of scattering properties.

**Important parameters / regimes.** London penetration depth ($\lambda_L$), spin-wave wavelength ($\lambda$), film thickness ($d$), and operating temperature (near $T_c$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the quasi-static approximation, assuming thin magnetic films ($kd \ll 1$), and decomposing the total field into dipolar and screening components.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the FMI-SC heterostructure setup involving spin wave propagation and Meissner screening effects.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the general theoretical framework, deriving self-consistent equations for the magnetic field, solving for the dispersion relation, and finally analyzing boundary conditions to find reflection/transmission coefficients in different limits.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate spin-wave transport in ferromagnetic insulator-superconductor (FMI-SC) heterostructures and develop a general theoretical framework for spin-wave optics in these hybrid systems. We demonstrate that Meissner screening by the superconductor gives rise to a range of unconventional wave phenomena, including negative phase- and group-velocity refraction, and reflection and refraction laws that differ fundamentally from their optical counterparts. Within this framework, we derive the spin-wave Fresnel equations governing reflection and transmission at FMI-SC interfaces and show that the scattering properties exhibit a pronounced temperature dependence, enabling tunable spin-wave mirrors and refractive elements. Most strikingly, we find that superconducting screening can produce nearly straight isofrequency contours, far flatter than the kinked, intrinsically curved contours attainable in conventional dipolar spin-wave systems. We show that these straight contours enable functionalities such as perfect spin-wave imaging, efficient waveguiding, and interferometric elements, such as phase shifters and beam splitters, with unconventional properties. Our results establish FMI-SC heterostructures as a versatile platform for temperature-tunable spin-wave optics and interferometric magnonic devices.

</details>

**Summary.** The research experimentally investigates electron temperature and electron-electron scattering length in high-mobility GaAs/AlGaAs 2DES using a mesoscopic aperture geometry. By measuring magnetoresistance oscillations as a function of temperature and applied bias current, the authors successfully determined the electron-electron scattering length and quantified how Joule heating elevates the electron temperature above the lattice temperature.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a highly controlled experimental platform for studying non-equilibrium electron dynamics in low dimensions. The ability to deterministically control $T_e$ via current injection allows for the direct measurement of scattering mechanisms, which is crucial for understanding energy relaxation in quantum devices.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to quantify electron temperatures ($T_e$) and the electron-electron scattering length ($\ell_{ee}$) in two-dimensional electron systems (2DES) under non-equilibrium conditions.

**Main result.** The authors established a method to determine $\ell_{ee}$ by analyzing the exponential decay of magnetoresistance peaks with temperature, and they quantified $T_e$ via its quadratic dependence on applied DC bias current ($I_{DC}$).

**Method.** Experimental measurements were performed using low-temperature ballistic transport in a mesoscopic multiparallel aperture geometry fabricated on GaAs/AlGaAs heterostructures.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a high-mobility 2DES confined in a perforated barrier structure. The analysis uses semiclassical cyclotron orbits and models the magnetoresistance amplitude decay based on electron-electron scattering theory.

**Key observables.** Magnetoresistance $R(B)$ peaks, which are analyzed for their amplitude dependence on temperature ($T$) and DC bias current ($I_{DC}$); also measured is $R(B=0)$ vs $T$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Electron temperature ($T_e$), electron-electron scattering length ($\ell_{ee}$), cyclotron orbit diameter, and the applied DC heating current ($I_{DC}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The amplitude of magnetoresistance maxima is assumed to correlate with the number of electrons reaching the collector; non-equilibrium heating implies $T_e > T$ when $I_{DC} 
eq 0$.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the mesoscopic geometry and cyclotron orbits, showing how magnetoresistance peaks vary with temperature. Other figures plot measured amplitudes vs $T$, fitting these to extract $\ell_{ee}(T)$, and show $R(B=0)$ vs $T$ to identify superballistic conductance regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the necessity of quantifying $T_e$ and $\ell_{ee}$ in 2DES, detailing the measurement technique using mesoscopic apertures, deriving relationships between peak amplitudes and scattering lengths, and finally applying this framework to study temperature dependence via both thermal cooling and bias-induced Joule heating.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantifying electron temperatures and electron-electron scattering lengths is essential for understanding electron transport regimes in two-dimensional systems. To that end, this work experimentally investigates low-temperature ballistic transport using semiclassical cyclotron orbits in a multiparallel aperture geometry. The mesoscopic geometry is fabricated on a GaAs/AlGaAs heterostructure of very high electron mobility. The amplitudes of maxima in magnetoresistance due to ballistic transport are observed to decay exponentially with temperature, allowing determination of the electron-electron scattering length from the data. A quantitative relation between electron temperature and an applied dc bias heating current is established by comparing magnetoresistance peak amplitudes measured as a function of temperature and dc bias, reflecting bias-induced Joule heating. In addition, the resistance measured near zero magnetic field decreases with increasing temperature, providing signatures of superballistic conductance.

</details>

**Summary.** This work demonstrates that nonreciprocity in magnetoacoustically coupled thin films can be used as a highly sensitive, nanoscale diagnostic tool. By observing the sign reversal of this asymmetry across different alloy compositions, the authors successfully linked the phenomenon to the underlying change in the magnetoelastic coupling coefficient $b$. This establishes a new method for characterizing fundamental magnetic material properties.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter magnetism and acoustics, the ability to use an electrical/acoustic measurement ($\chi_P$) to precisely determine the sign of a fundamental material parameter ($b$) where direct strain measurements fail is analogous to using quantum signatures (like entanglement measures) to probe inaccessible aspects of many-body Hamiltonians.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to understand and control the sign of nonreciprocity arising from magnetoacoustic attenuation in ferromagnet thin films.

**Main result.** A small compositional change near Permalloy's zero-magnetostriction point causes a measurable sign reversal of the nonreciprocity, confirming that this effect can serve as a nanoscale probe for the magnetoelastic coupling coefficient $b$.

**Method.** The research uses Surface Acoustic Waves (SAWs) propagating through $	ext{Ni}_x	ext{Fe}_{100-x}$ thin films deposited on a piezoelectric substrate to measure nonreciprocal attenuation ratios ($\chi_P$).

**Model / system.** The physical system is a hybrid device structure involving an $	ext{LiNbO}_3$ substrate, Interdigital Transducers (IDTs), and the magnetic trilayer. The underlying physics involves magnetoacoustic coupling between SAWs and the ferromagnet's magnetization.

**Key observables.** Nonreciprocity ratio ($\chi_P$), which measures the asymmetry of SAW attenuation for counter-propagating waves; the sign and magnitude of the magnetoelastic coefficient ($b$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Compositional concentration $x$ (near 80 at.% Ni), magnetic field angle $\phi$, and SAW frequency ($f \approx 2-9 	ext{ GHz}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The nonreciprocity is attributed to a rotation-based mechanism (MR or Barnett) rather than the shear-strain mechanism, which fails to explain the observed sign reversal.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the device setup and show clear evidence of nonreciprocity reversal ($\chi_P$) as the composition $x$ crosses the point where the magnetoelastic coefficient $b$ changes sign. Frequency dependence analysis is also presented.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical mechanism by analyzing how compositional tuning controls the sign of $\chi_P$. It then uses theoretical modeling (comparing shear-strain vs. rotation mechanisms) to pinpoint the origin, culminating in demonstrating that nonreciprocity acts as a sensitive probe for $b$ near zero.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Nonreciprocity, the asymmetry of transport, underlies technologies from the diode to the microwave isolator. In a ferromagnet, a surface acoustic wave generates an elliptical effective field with propagation-locked handedness, breaking the reciprocity of its propagation. Despite decades of study on this phenomenon, a method for controlling the sign of the nonreciprocity has remained elusive. Here we observe a sign reversal in Ni$_x$Fe$_{100-x}$ films. A 0.8 at.% change across Permalloy's zero-magnetostriction composition, where the magnetoelastic coefficient $b$ changes sign, reverses the handedness of the elliptical effective field and thereby the nonreciprocity, from 78.6% to -61.8%. Angle-dependent measurements and spin-wave-ellipticity modelling show that reversing the sign of $b$ reverses the handedness of the elliptically polarized effective field. Aided by cubic frequency scaling, we resolve the sign of $b$ down to -0.05 MPa in a 10-nm film, establishing nonreciprocity as a nanoscale probe of magnetoelastic coupling.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work demonstrates how stacking magnetic layers in an SSH pattern can create topological surface altermagnetism. By breaking local symmetry at the boundary of a bulk antiferromagnet, robust spin-polarized surface states emerge within the energy gap. These exotic surface properties are predicted to be detectable via a measurable net spin polarization induced by an external perpendicular electric field.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter magnetism, the concept of engineering robust, symmetry-protected electronic states at boundaries using lattice models is highly relevant to understanding engineered quantum materials for spintronics applications.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The work proposes a method to engineer topological surface altermagnetism by stacking magnetic layers using a Su-Schrieffer-Heeger (SSH) pattern.

**Main result.** Breaking the local symmetry at the boundary of an antiferromagnetic SSH stack generates robust, topologically protected surface altermagnetism within the bulk's topological gap.

**Method.** Theoretical analysis involving tight-binding models, calculation of spin-resolved LDOS, and characterization using the Zak phase associated with chiral symmetry.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled by SSH stacking of 2D magnetic layers. The bulk behaves as a PT-symmetric antiferromagnet, while surface states emerge due to boundary symmetry breaking.

**Key observables.** Topological gap containing surface states; spin separation in LDOS; layer-resolved and total spin splitter angle ($\alpha$); response to perpendicular electric field ($V$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The coupling strengths ($t_1, t_2$) defining the SSH pattern; the applied perpendicular electric field ($V$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The approach is generalized to different types of surface altermagnetism (p-wave, f-wave); the measurement relies on detecting transport signatures induced by $V$.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate energy spectra showing topological surface states; Fig. 2 shows how applying an electric field ($V$) induces a non-zero total spin splitter angle ($\alpha$), which is zero when $V=0$ due to symmetry.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the SSH model Hamiltonian, analyzes the bulk PT-symmetric antiferromagnetism, demonstrates that boundary breaking creates surface altermagnetism, and finally uses transport signatures (spin splitter angle) under an electric field to confirm this topological effect.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Surface altermagnetism opens new avenues in spintronics by unlocking altermagnetic spin-splitting at the boundaries of conventional antiferromagnets, bypassing the strict symmetry requirements of bulk altermagnets. In this work, we propose creating topological surface altermagnet by stacking magnetic layers in a Su-Schrieffer-Heeger pattern. We show that while the bulk of the system is a standard antiferromagnet with degenerate bands protected by $PT$ symmetry, breaking the local symmetry at the boundary gives rise to a topologically protected surface altermagnetic state residing within the topological gap. Furthermore, we propose that this effect can be experimentally detected by applying a perpendicular electric field. Besides, this approach can be readily generalized to surface altermagnetism of different types. Our work establishes topological boundaries as a natural platform for surface altermagnetism, offering a distinct route for realizing and manipulating topological surface altermagnets.

</details>

**Summary.** This work addresses the critical issue of high contact resistance limiting 2D transistor performance. By developing advanced self-consistent models for both vertical and edge contacts, the authors predict that current saturation limits downscaling below $10 	ext{ nm}$. They propose using specific topological semi-metals as electrodes to achieve near-quantum limit contact resistances.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on semiconductor physics rather than core quantum information protocols, the detailed modeling of interface transport phenomena, tunneling probabilities, and scaling laws is highly relevant to understanding energy dissipation in nanoscale quantum devices.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** High contact resistance remains a major bottleneck for realizing practical, ultra-nanoscaled (sub-10 nm) two-dimensional (2D) materials transistors, particularly for p-type operation.

**Main result.** The authors developed self-consistent models identifying that source-limited current saturation is the key challenge for scaling down 2D FETs. They propose using topological semi-metals as electrodes to achieve predicted contact resistances approaching the quantum limit ($< 100 \, \Omega\mu	ext{m}$).

**Method.** The study employs self-consistent theoretical modeling, developing separate frameworks for vertical and edge contacts by solving coupled differential equations based on tunneling current calculations.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a Metal-2D semiconductor-Metal (MSM) device, focusing on p-type 2D semiconductors like WSe2. The model incorporates concepts like Schottky barriers, generalized Transmission Line Models (TLM), and analyzes scaling laws based on channel length and doping.

**Key observables.** Contact Resistance ($R_c$), Total Resistance ($R_{	ext{tot}}$), Channel Resistance ($R_{	ext{ch}}$), Current density ($J$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Channel length ($	ext{L}_{	ext{ch}}$) down to sub-10 nm, doping concentration ($N_p$), applied voltage ($V_{	ext{DS}}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The model assumes metal electrodes have negligible resistance and that the analysis can be extended from computationally feasible few-nanometer scales.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate schematics for vertical and edge contacts, showing $R_c$ scaling with $	ext{L}_{	ext{ch}}$ and $V_{	ext{DS}}$, and comparing model predictions against experimental data using various electrode materials.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically develops contact resistance models for both vertical and edge geometries. It analyzes the scaling behavior of $R_c$ with device parameters, identifies current saturation as a limiting factor, and proposes new material combinations (e.g., topological semi-metals) to overcome this bottleneck.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

High contact resistance is one of the main bottlenecks for practical two-dimensional (2D) materials transistors, especially for p-type transistors and future 2D ultra-nanoscaled (sub-10 nm) FETs (PMOS + CMOS). We develop self-consistent contact resistance models for metal-2D semiconductor-metal devices to capture the essential interface physics for both vertical and edge configurations. Our calculations have been verified with various recent experiments of p-type and n-type contacts. For a given set of materials, the model determines the scaling of contact resistance over a wide range of device parameters including channel length (100s nm down to sub-10 nm), doping and mobility of the 2D materials, contact length of the electrodes, and applied voltages. These results identify the key factors in order to reduce the contact resistance for p-type 2D semiconductor WSe$_2$ towards the sub-10 nm channel length scale that are readily to be realized by future experiments. It is found that the effect of source-limited current saturation is the key challenge for down scaling 2D FET to sub-10 nm channel length. Two topological semi-metals as potential electrodes are proposed for 2D p-type semiconducting WSe$_2$ with our predicted contact resistance $R_c<$ 100 $Ω\; {\rm μm}$ approaching the quantum limit. Our model is also verified with the computational expensive full quantum atomistic model that is currently limited to a few nm scale.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically demonstrates how to achieve near-perfect absorption of electromagnetic waves using a periodic metasurface composed of 2D electron systems coupled with metal contacts. By incorporating a perfect reflector, the authors boost absorbance to $100\%$ and reveal that optimal performance can occur at distances much smaller than conventionally expected. This work is significant for designing high-efficiency optical detectors based on advanced condensed matter structures.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides advanced theoretical tools for engineering light-matter interaction in low-dimensional materials, which is highly relevant to quantum optics and open quantum systems where engineered cavities or plasmonic structures are used to enhance coupling rates.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is that electromagnetic absorbance by typical two-dimensional electron systems (2DES) is significantly less than unity, limiting their use in photodetection and optical studies.

**Main result.** By engineering a periodic structure of 2D sections connected by metal contacts and placing it above a perfect reflector, the authors demonstrate achieving near-perfect ($\approx 100\%$) absorbance. Furthermore, they show that optimal performance can occur at extremely small distances ($d 	o 0$) to the reflector.

**Method.** The analysis employs semi-analytical solutions of Maxwell's equations, utilizing Fourier transforms and Green's functions derived from solving the scattering problem for the engineered metasurface structure.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is a periodic array (metasurface) consisting of narrow 2D electron sections separated by wide, perfectly conducting metal contacts. This entire unit cell can be placed above a perfect electromagnetic reflector separated by a dielectric substrate.

**Key observables.** Absorbance ($\alpha$), which is the primary diagnostic quantity; it is related to the material conductivity and geometric parameters.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Filling factor ($f = l/L$), dimensionless conductivity ($\eta$), optimal distance to reflector ($d$), and substrate dielectric constant ($n_{	ext{sub}}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on linking the local electric field via Green's functions, and later approximations use a lumped circuit model for simplicity.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the physical structures (with/without reflector) and show calculated absorbance ($\alpha$) versus filling factor ($f$) or conductivity ($\eta$), demonstrating the enhancement provided by the back reflector.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the low baseline absorbance of 2DES, to introducing the periodic structure with metal contacts for initial enhancement. It then analyzes the inclusion of a perfect reflector, deriving conditions for $100\%$ absorption and exploring the non-intuitive dependence on the separation distance $d$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Electromagnetic absorbance by most two-dimensional electron systems is typically well below unity, which hinders both practical applications in photodetection and fundamental studies of their optical properties. Here, we show that a periodic structure comprised of narrow two-dimensional sections connected with wide perfectly conducting metal sections enables large absorbance. It reaches 50 \% provided the filling factor by the two-dimensional system $f$ equals its dimensionless conductivity $η=σZ_0/2$, where $Z_0$ is the free-space impedance. The absorbance is further raised to 100 \% if the periodic structure is placed above a perfectly conducting electromagnetic reflector, and provided $f=2η$. Surprisingly, the optimal distance between two-dimensional system and reflector may fall well below the quarter of incident wavelength $λ_0/4$, which was assumed as conventional absorption enhancement condition in optics. For low filling factors $f\ll1$, large dielectric constants of the substrate, and grating periods comparable with $λ_0$, the optimal distance to reflector tends to zero. Above the critical values of the grating geometrical parameters, the absorbance maximum ceases to exist. The critical behavior manifests as a large-amplitude resonance in 'dirty' two-dimensional system with purely real conductivity, while enhancement of carrier momentum relaxation time lowers the resonant peak. Such resonance mimics the plasmonic one, but does not rely on high electron mobility.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates the magneto-optical properties of $\mathrm{Co_2Mn}$-based Heusler thin films using both MOKE and BLS. The key finding is that the distinct spectral behavior observed in MOKE (especially quadratic contributions) directly correlates with the intensity variations seen in BLS measurements. This establishes a powerful experimental methodology for selecting optimal wavelengths when probing magnetic excitations.

**Why it may be interesting.** While primarily experimental condensed matter physics, the detailed study of spin wave dynamics and magneto-optical response provides fundamental insights into magnetic order parameters and their coupling to optical probes, which is relevant for understanding quantum magnetodynamics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To investigate the magneto-optical response of $\mathrm{Co_2Mn}$-based Heusler thin films using both Magneto-Optical Kerr Effect (MOKE) and Brillouin Light Scattering (BLS).

**Main result.** The study establishes a direct experimental link between MOKE spectroscopy and BLS measurements, showing that the spectral dependence observed in one technique can guide the optimal probing wavelength for the other.

**Method.** Measurements involve angle-resolved MOKE spectroscopy to separate linear and quadratic magneto-optical contributions, complemented by BLS intensity measurements of thermal magnons at multiple wavelengths.

**Model / system.** The physical system is epitaxial $\mathrm{Co_2MnX}$ Heusler thin films ($\mathrm{X} = \{\mathrm{Al}_x\mathrm{Si}_{1-x}, \mathrm{Ga}_x\mathrm{Ge}_{1-x}, \mathrm{Sn}\}$), which are studied for their spintronic and magnonic properties.

**Key observables.** Linear MOKE ($	heta_{	ext{linMOKE}}$), Quadratic MOKE ($	heta_{	ext{QMOKE}}$), BLS intensity of thermal magnons, and the spectral dependence of these signals across different wavelengths.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Wavelengths used for probing (e.g., $457\,\mathrm{nm}$ vs. $532\,\mathrm{nm}$); material composition ($\mathrm{Co_2MnX}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The simple symmetry-based separation approach used in MOKE cannot fully distinguish between all possible symmetries of the quadratic magneto-optical contributions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the MOKE setup, the method for separating hysteresis loops into odd (linear) and even (quadratic) components, and comparisons of BLS intensities at different wavelengths.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically compares MOKE results across various $\mathrm{Co_2MnX}$ films to identify characteristic responses ($\mathrm{Co_2MnSi}$ showing strong QMOKE vs. others being linear). It then correlates these findings with wavelength-dependent BLS measurements, culminating in the conclusion that MOKE guides BLS probing.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

$\mathrm{Co_2Mn}$-based Heusler compounds offer a versatile, composition-tunable platform for magnonics and spintronics. Among them, the half-metallic $\mathrm{Co_2MnSi}$ is of particular interest for magnonics owing to its ultralow Gilbert damping, yet its weak magneto-optical response in the visible challenges optical probing such as Brillouin light scattering (BLS). We study the magneto-optical response of epitaxial $\mathrm{Co_2Mn}X$ films ($X = \{\mathrm{Al}_x\mathrm{Si}_{1-x}, \mathrm{Ga}_x\mathrm{Ge}_{1-x}, \mathrm{Sn}\}$) by magneto-optical Kerr effect (MOKE) spectroscopy and BLS. Angle-resolved MOKE resolves a significant, wavelength-dependent quadratic MOKE (QMOKE) only for $\mathrm{Co_2MnSi}$, whereas $\mathrm{Co_2MnAl}$, $\mathrm{Co_2MnGa}$ and $\mathrm{Co_2MnSn}$ respond dominantly linearly. Comparing BLS intensities of thermal magnons at two wavelengths, $\mathrm{Co_2MnSi}$ gives the weakest signal at $532\,\mathrm{nm}$ yet among the strongest at $457\,\mathrm{nm}$, tracking the spectral dependence of its Kerr angle. These results emphasise the relation between the two magneto-optical techniques, guiding the choice of probing wavelength for $\mathrm{Co_2Mn}$-based Heusler compounds.

</details>
