**Summary.** The paper reports on experimental and theoretical investigations into superconductivity in Al/InAs hybrid structures. By measuring the microwave electrodynamics, the authors detect an anisotropic suppression of superfluid stiffness that signals the emergence of Bogoliubov Fermi Surfaces. This work establishes microwave measurements as a sensitive tool for probing gapless superconducting phases.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete platform for observing exotic superconducting states predicted in proximity systems, linking macroscopic transport measurements (microwave response) directly to microscopic band structure features like BFS.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the emergence of Bogoliubov Fermi Surfaces (BFS) and characterizes anisotropic gapless superconductivity in hybrid superconductor/semiconductor heterostructures.

**Main result.** The observed non-monotonic and strongly anisotropic evolution of the superfluid stiffness, measured via microwave electrodynamics, is consistent with the formation of BFS due to the interplay of Zeeman and orbital Fulde-Ferrell effects.

**Method.** The research combines microwave electrodynamic measurements (probing kinetic inductance) with microscopic theoretical calculations based on the Bogoliubov-de Gennes framework.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a hybrid Al/InAs heterostructure containing a 2DEG proximitized by an Al superconductor. The theory models the superconducting response using the superfluid stiffness tensor derived from the free energy density.

**Key observables.** Superfluid stiffness tensor components ($D_{ij}$), relative frequency shift ($\Delta f_0 / f_0$), and internal quality factor change ($Q_i$) as functions of in-plane magnetic field and angle.

**Important parameters / regimes.** In-plane magnetic field ($B$), Zeeman energy ($V_Z$), Doppler energy ($V_D$), superconducting gap ($\Delta_0$), and temperature ($T$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the electrodynamic response can be mapped to the superfluid stiffness tensor, and theoretical models rely on specific approximations regarding disorder effects and gauge equivalence.

**Figures summary.** Figures show sample setups, microwave resonance curves demonstrating field dependence, theoretical band structure evolution illustrating gap closing and BFS formation, and quantitative maps detailing angular anisotropy of stiffness suppression.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from experimental characterization using microwave resonators to establishing the link between anisotropic response and BFS emergence. It then uses microscopic theory (BdG) to model this behavior, followed by fitting measured superfluid density data to confirm the theoretical predictions regarding field-induced gapless phases.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the microwave electrodynamics of a proximitized two-dimensional electron gas in hybrid superconductor/semiconductor heterostructures. Using lumped-element resonators with inductor wires oriented relative to an in-plane magnetic field, we directly probe the superfluid stiffness via the kinetic inductance. As the field increases, the resonance frequency exhibits a non-monotonic and strongly anisotropic evolution that cannot be explained by orbital pair breaking alone. We show that this behavior is consistent with the emergence of Bogoliubov Fermi surfaces, which selectively suppress the supercurrent response depending on the direction of the magnetic field. Microscopic calculations of the stiffness tensor capture the observed anisotropy driven by the interplay of Zeeman and orbital Fulde-Ferrell effects. Our results establish microwave stiffness measurements as a sensitive probe of anisotropic gapless superconductivity in hybrid systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This work uses Electron Paramagnetic Resonance (EPR) spectroscopy on FeSe single crystals to characterize their superconducting properties. By analyzing non-resonant EPR signals under varying magnetic fields and temperatures, the authors determined critical parameters such as $T_c$ and $H_{c1}$. The results confirm the mixed state behavior expected for a Type-II superconductor while also identifying electronic signatures related to iron ion oxidation states.

**Why it may be interesting.** The use of high-frequency EPR spectroscopy to probe superconducting vortex dynamics and critical fields provides an alternative, highly sensitive diagnostic tool compared to standard DC magnetometry or resistivity measurements in condensed matter physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To study the superconducting and normal state properties of a single crystal of FeSe using Electron Paramagnetic Resonance (EPR) spectroscopy.

**Main result.** The EPR measurements successfully determined key superconducting parameters like $T_c$, the transition width, and the first critical magnetic field ($H_{c1}$), while also characterizing the electronic states via resonant signals corresponding to $	ext{Fe}^{2+}$ ions.

**Method.** EPR spectroscopy was employed across varying temperatures and applied magnetic fields. Non-resonant signals were analyzed for superconducting signatures (e.g., $180^\circ$ phase jumps), while resonant signals identified specific paramagnetic ion states.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a single crystal of FeSe, studied as a Type-II superconductor. Measurements compare the field dependence of non-resonant EPR signals in the mixed state to theoretical models derived from the temperature derivative of resistivity ($dR^*/dT$).

**Key observables.** Non-resonant EPR signal (field/temperature dependent), resonant EPR signals at 1400 Oe and 3400 Oe, $T_c$, superconducting transition width, and $H_{c1}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Magnetic field up to 6000 Oe; Temperature range from 3.5 K to 25 K; Critical fields ($H_{c1}$) and onset temperature ($T_{c	ext{onset}} \approx 8.8 	ext{ K}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The non-resonant EPR signal is proportional to the field derivative of the radiation power, and comparisons between electrical (FPM) and microwave (EPR) measurements are made despite methodological differences.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate $T_{c	ext{onset}}$ from magnetization ($T_c=8.8 	ext{ K}$), the nonlinear field dependence of the EPR signal in the mixed state, the characteristic $180^\circ$ phase jump identifying $H_{c1}$, and comparisons between temperature-dependent EPR signals and resistivity derivatives.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically presents measurements across different states (normal vs. superconducting) using EPR. It first establishes $T_c$ via magnetization, then uses low-field non-resonant signals to map the mixed state behavior ($H_{c1}$ identification), and finally compares the temperature dependence of these signals against established electrical transport models.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Using an EPR spectrometer, the properties of single-crystal FeSe were studied in a magnetic field up to 6000 Oe at temperatures from 3.5 K to 8 K in the superconducting state and at temperatures above 8K and up to 25K in the normal state. It was shown that by measuring non-resonant spectrometer signals in zero and weak magnetic fields up to 30 Oe, it is possible to determine critical temperature Tc, the superconducting transition width, and the value of the first critical magnetic field Hc1 in FeSe single-crystal. Resonant EPR signals are observed in fields at 1400 Oe and 3400 Oe, which corresponds to the paramagnetism of doubly ionized iron atoms (Fe2+) in the FeSe crystal. Non-resonant EPR signals in a magnetic field up to 6000 Oe at temperatures from 3.5K to 8K are a nonlinear function of the field, and correspond to the superconducting mixed state of the FeSe, but at temperatures above 8K (up to 25K) they are field independent.

</details>

**Summary.** This work predicts the existence of a Type-II Mirror Chern Insulator phase stabilized by altermagnetic ordering in 2D materials. By modeling this using a lattice Hamiltonian, the authors show that SOC gaps out Dirac nodes into a topologically non-trivial state (CM=2). The key novelty is the prediction of momentum-separated edge modes, which they suggest can be realized in the $	ext{PbSe}/	ext{V}_2	ext{Se}_2	ext{O}$ heterobilayer.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of using magnetic order (altermagnetism) to engineer specific topological invariants like the Mirror Chern number provides a powerful, symmetry-based route to realizing exotic quantum states in condensed matter systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to predict and characterize a novel topological phase called a "Type-II Mirror Chern Insulator" stabilized by altermagnetism in the presence of mirror symmetry.

**Main result.** Altermagnetic order generates symmetry-related valley-polarized Dirac nodes, which are gapped by SOC to yield a bulk insulator with a non-trivial mirror Chern number (CM=2) and distinct, momentum-separated edge modes.

**Method.** The study uses electronic structure calculations on a 2D square-octagon lattice model, analyzing the Bloch Hamiltonian and calculating topological invariants like the Mirror Chern number.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled using a tight-binding Hamiltonian incorporating hopping ($H_t$), intrinsic SOC ($H_{SOC}$), and an altermagnetic exchange field ($H_m$). A candidate material proposed for realization is the PbSe/V2Se2O heterobilayer.

**Key observables.** Mirror Chern number (CM=2), momentum-separated edge modes, valley-polarized Dirac nodes, and bulk insulating gap opening.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Altermagnetic order parameter ($\delta_m$) and SOC strength ($\lambda_{SOC}$); the lattice structure (square-octagon).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the specific square-octagon lattice model and assumes the feasibility of realizing the altermagnetic proximity effect in the proposed heterobilayer.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate spin-resolved Fermi contours, band structure evolution showing node gapping, Berry curvature hotspots confirming CM=2, and edge spectra demonstrating momentum separation compared to conventional insulators.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical model using altermagnetism in a 2D lattice, calculates the topological invariant (CM), demonstrates the unique Type-II signature via separated edge modes, and concludes by proposing a concrete material platform ($	ext{PbSe}/	ext{V}_2	ext{Se}_2	ext{O}$) for experimental realization.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Altermagnets with momentum-dependent spin splitting despite zero net magnetization can support unique topological states under broken time-reversal symmetry. We predict a mirror-symmetry-protected topological crystalline insulator with momentum-separated edge modes in a two-dimensional altermagnet. Using a square-octagon lattice model, we show that altermagnetic order generates symmetry-related valley-polarized Dirac nodes, which are gapped by spin-orbit coupling to yield a mirror Chern insulator with $C_{\mathcal{M}}=2$. In contrast to conventional mirror Chern insulators, where the two mirror-protected edge modes cross at the same momentum to form a Dirac cone, altermagnetic spin splitting and valley-selective band inversion separate these edge modes in momentum. We refer to this phase as a type-II mirror Chern insulator. We further propose a PbSe/$\mathrm{V_2Se_2O}$ heterobilayer as a candidate material for realizing this phase through the altermagnetic proximity effect. Our results establish altermagnetism as a route to mirror-protected topological phases with momentum-separated edge modes.

</details>

**Type:** experiment · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.06492v1>

**Summary.** This experimental paper characterizes how effectively different commercial cryogenic microwave attenuators function as thermal heatsinks. By measuring their temperature response under controlled heat loads, the authors provide quantitative data on their thermal transresistance and time constants. This information is vital for designing reliable and accurately controlled thermal environments in advanced low-temperature quantum experiments.

**Why it may be interesting.** While not directly about quantum dynamics, understanding the precise thermal environment is crucial for any experiment involving sensitive quantum devices (like qubits or superconducting circuits) where temperature fluctuations can cause decoherence or measurement errors.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to quantify the thermal performance of cryogenic microwave attenuators when used as thermal anchors for coaxial cables in ultra-low temperature quantum experiments.

**Main result.** The quartz crystal attenuator was found to be significantly more effective than teflon or sealed alternatives, providing better thermal anchoring and lower thermal transresistance at various temperatures.

**Method.** The authors measured the temperature response of three different commercial 0 dB attenuators by applying controlled heat loads and measuring the resulting temperature change versus applied power.

**Model / system.** The physical system involves coaxial cables used in dilution refrigerators, focusing on the thermal coupling through the inner conductor. The measurement characterizes the attenuator's ability to act as a reliable thermal heatsink.

**Key observables.** Thermal transresistance (R), temperature dependence of R, and thermal equilibration time constant ($\tau$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Operating temperatures in the millikelvin range ($T_{mx}$), applied heating power ($P$), and material types (quartz crystal vs. teflon).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The body of the attenuator is assumed to be in strong thermal contact with the mixing chamber, and comparisons are made between different thermal models (e.g., simple resistance vs. distributed resistance).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the experimental setup, show temperature readings versus applied power for a specific attenuator, plot thermal response curves and transresistance across various base temperatures, and compare temporal decay rates ($\tau$) for different materials.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the need for accurate thermal characterization in cryogenic setups, details the measurement methodology using three distinct attenuators, presents quantitative results comparing their performance metrics (R and $\tau$), and concludes with guidelines for designing better thermal environments.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Coaxial cables are widely used in radiofrequency and microwave cryogenic setups for condensed matter and quantum experiments. Since the inner conductor of coaxes is often in good thermal contact with the sample to be measured, it is desirable to know the phononic heat channeled by the inner conductor. Although cryogenic attenuators are widely used to thermalize the inner conductor of coaxes, to our knowledge, quantitative information is not available. We present data on the effectiveness of three commercially available 0 dB attenuators as thermal heatsinks. In particular, we measured the temperature of the inner pin of several 0 dB attenuators under a heat load. This information will aid in designing and carefully controlling the thermal environment of samples in high-frequency experiments mounted in dilution refrigerators and on nuclear demagnetization stages.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously assesses how well machine learning interatomic potentials (MLIPs) maintain accuracy when simulating materials across vastly different structural environments, such as bulk solids versus atomic nanowires. By testing $	ext{ZrO}_2$ under simulated desintering conditions, the authors demonstrate that simple error metrics are insufficient predictors of physical reliability. They conclude that creating geometry-diverse training data and employing mixed-geometry fine-tuning are crucial for robust MLIP application in low-coordination nanostructures.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant because it pushes the boundaries of using data-driven potentials to model complex condensed matter systems where local coordination changes drastically, which is a key challenge in nanoscale physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is assessing how reliably Machine Learning Interatomic Potentials (MLIPs) transfer their accuracy when simulating structural changes, specifically moving from bulk materials to low-coordination nanostructures like atomic nanowires.

**Main result.** Cross-geometry fine-tuning strategies are superior to geometry-specific tuning for reducing overall cross-geometry errors, and the study proves that simple energy/force error metrics do not predict complex property behavior.

**Method.** The authors benchmarked 26 pretrained MLIPs using a DFT-generated dataset spanning five geometries (bulk to wire) and compared zero-shot inference against fine-tuning protocols.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on Zirconia ($	ext{ZrO}_2$) undergoing desintering, creating complex structures including necks and atomic wires. The models are advanced MLIPs (e.g., ORB) trained to approximate the potential energy surface derived from DFT calculations.

**Key observables.** Energy and force root-mean-square errors (RMSEs), elastic properties (Young's modulus, Bulk Modulus), surface energies ($\gamma$), phonon dispersions, and structural metrics like RMSD during MD simulations.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The simulation regime involves tensile deformation, varying coordination numbers (bulk to wire), and comparison between zero-shot, fine-tuning, and training from scratch MLIP adaptation strategies.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the DFT dataset accurately captures the necessary physics for transferability; however, it notes limitations regarding isolating long-range electrostatic effects.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare energy/force errors across geometries (Fig 2A), show phonon dispersion curves (S2, S3) and vibrational DOS (S4), and track structural stability via RMSD and minimum bond distances during tensile loading (S5, S10).

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem of MLIP transferability across geometries using $	ext{ZrO}_2$. It details dataset construction and benchmarking protocols, comparing zero-shot vs. fine-tuning strategies. Finally, it validates these models by calculating macroscopic properties (elasticity, surface energy) and simulating dynamic processes (MD/Phonons).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Foundation machine-learning interatomic potentials (MLIPs) enable atomistic simulations at substantially lower computational cost than first-principles methods, but their reliability across structural geometries remains insufficiently understood. Here, we construct a density-functional-theory dataset of ZrO2 configurations spanning bulk, slab, particle, neck, and atomically thin wire environments motivated by an experimentally observed ZrO2 desintering process involving neck thinning and atomic wire formation. We first benchmark 26 pretrained MLIPs and observe pronounced geometry-dependent degradation in zero-shot predictions. Without any training, after only reference-energy alignment, the best zero-shot model (ORB-V3) reaches energy and force root-mean-square errors of 6 meV/atom and 197.3 meV/Å, respectively, with the largest force errors in neck and wire configurations. We then compare zero-shot inference, fine-tuning, and training from scratch strategies. Fine-tuning yields lower energy and force errors than training from scratch, while both require comparable wall-clock time. Geometry-specific fine-tuning improves in-domain accuracy but frequently produces negative transfer to other structural classes, whereas mixed-geometry fine-tuning reduces cross-geometry errors. Evaluations of elastic and vibrational properties, surface energies, and neck dynamics further show that rankings based on average energy and force errors do not universally predict property-level behavior. These results demonstrate that geometry-diverse target data and independent physical validations are necessary when adapting foundation MLIPs to low-coordination (ionic) nanostructures.

</details>

(d) Potential on the edges of the contour plot (c) going counterclockwise.</figcaption>
</figure>

</div>

**Summary.** This paper presents an advanced theoretical method to determine the full conductivity tensor and principal axes angle of anisotropic 2D materials using only four electrical resistance measurements on a rectangular sample. The methodology involves solving Laplace's equation via conformal mapping, yielding explicit algebraic formulas that relate measured resistances directly to the material's underlying transport properties.

**Why it may be interesting.** While not directly quantum mechanical, the rigorous application of advanced mathematical physics (conformal mapping) to solve a classical electrostatics problem in an anisotropic medium showcases powerful analytical techniques applicable across condensed matter theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to develop a simple, analytically derived experimental procedure to extract the full conductivity tensor ($\sigma$) and the principal axes angle ($\alpha$) from anisotropic 2D materials.

**Main result.** The authors provide explicit analytical formulas relating four specific resistance measurements ($R1$ through $R4$) on a rectangular sample to the components of the conductivity tensor ($\sigma_+$, $\sigma_-$, $\sigma_H$).

**Method.** The method relies heavily on solving Laplace's equation for the electric potential using conformal mapping techniques, specifically involving Schwarz-Christoffel transformations.

**Model / system.** The system is a uniform 2D rectangular sample exhibiting anisotropic electrical conductivity ($oldsymbol{\sigma}$). The analysis models current injection via point sources and drains placed on the perimeter of this geometry.

**Key observables.** Full conductivity tensor components ($\sigma_+$, $\sigma_-$, $\sigma_H$), principal axes angle ($\alpha$), and four measured resistances ($R1, R2, R3, R4$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The ratio $r$ (hypergeometric parameter) and the geometric mean of resistivity along principal axes ($ho^*$) are key intermediate parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** It is assumed that Ohm's law holds, the conductivity tensor is constant throughout the sample, and that the source/drain contacts can be modeled as being infinitesimally close to the perimeter.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the sequence of coordinate transformations (rotation, rescaling, mapping) used in the conformal mapping approach, potential contour plots comparing analytical solutions with simulations, and diagrams detailing specific vertex configurations for measurements.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from establishing the governing equations using source/drain current continuity, through deriving the complex potential solution via conformal mapping, to finally presenting algebraic relationships derived from multiple measurement configurations that allow solving for $\sigma_H$ and $\alpha$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Electrical transport measurements reveal that many 2D materials exhibit anisotropic conductivity. However, current methodologies for rectangular geometries can only extract a partial conductivity tensor or are difficult to execute experimentally. Here, we propose a simple experimental procedure to extract the full conductivity tensor (or the resistivity tensor by inversion) including the principal axes angle. Our procedure is developed by using a conformal mapping approach to obtain an analytical expression for the potential with a point source and drain on the perimeter. Our solution agrees very well with numerical simulations using COMSOL and the known limiting case where the principal axes angle vanishes. Finite source/drains can be modeled using superposition.

</details>
